Abstract
This document describes the architecture of Little Trotter X-Invoice, an invoicing and receipt-management application that produces UStG-compliant, ZUGFeRD capable e-invoices. It follows the arc42 template and is built with docToolchain. This documentation supports software development and architecture. All source code and documentation are provided in English.
Note
Notice on Transparency, AI Assistance, and Synthetic Test Data
Transparency & AI Assistance

This architecture documentation and its accompanying artifacts were authored and structured with the assistance of advanced AI development agents.

Synthetic Test Data (datagen)

All examples, organizations, personal names, invoice numbers, addresses, and monetary amounts depicted across this documentation and embedded diagrams are randomly generated synthetic test data (datagen). They do not represent real entities; any resemblance to actual businesses or individuals is purely coincidental.

Documentation Overview

arc42 architecture documentation for Little Trotter X-Invoice. Chapters 1–12 below are the parts of this arc42 Architecture Documentation; the appendices follow them.

1. Introduction and Goals

1.1. Requirements Overview

Little Trotter X-Invoice is a Java/Kotlin reference implementation for generating UStG-compliant, X-Rechnung / ZUGFeRD capable e-invoices (EN 16931) with PDF/A-3b output, for freelancers, engineering offices, specialty craft businesses, retail and service businesses and, in general, small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs). Invoices can be issued in German or in English (international layout). It covers the full invoicing workflow for a single Office Manager — the person actually writing the invoices: often the business owner, engineer or tradesperson themselves, sometimes someone experienced in bookkeeping:

  • Master data: invoice issuers, invoice recipients, catalog items (services/materials), and reference data (countries, tax rates, B2B terms).

  • Invoice lifecycle with an explicit state model (draft, issued, cancelled/annulled), line items, and travel/catering expense items.

  • Receipt management with checksum-based duplicate detection and S3-compatible object storage.

  • ZUGFeRD / X-Rechnung generation (Mustang Project) with multi-stage validation (XSD schema, EN 16931 Schematron, embedded KoSIT validator).

  • PDF rendering (Typst as the only layout engine) via a dedicated renderer/worker service, driven by asynchronous generation jobs over NATS.

  • Statistics/trend views.

1.1.1. Quick Start

In alignment with the root README.md, running the complete local evaluation stack requires Docker (Compose v2) and make:

make secrets   # once: creates .env with strong random credentials
make dev       # builds images, migrates the database, starts the 7 services

The application is reachable at http://localhost:8080. make dev-down stops the stack while preserving data. The local deployment contains seven containerized services: the invoicing web application, background job worker, document engine (PDF/A-3, XRechnung, Factur-X, KoSIT validation), PostgreSQL, S3-compatible object store, NATS JetStream, and Valkey cache. Everything operates strictly locally.

Note
 The make dev stack is for evaluation only. Productive use is a server installation set up by an IT service provider.
Note
 Per the project README, this is an open-source reference implementation — functionally complete for its core invoicing flow, not production-ready, provided as-is. This documentation is AI-assisted and all examples are synthetic test data; see the notice at the start of this document.

1.2. Quality Goals

The table below lists the top six quality goals, ordered by priority, consistent with the Architecture Communication Canvas.

Priority Quality Goal Motivation

1

Domain correctness

The output is a legal document. Every invoice passes multi-stage validation (schema, EN 16931/Schematron, embedded KoSIT validator) before it can be issued.

2

Test coverage and quality gates

A JaCoCo gate enforces 90% line and 90% branch coverage across every module, including the Vaadin UI layer. Linters (ktlint, detekt, sqlfluff where applicable), SonarQube Community Edition, and Gitleaks/Trivy must all report clean before a feature is considered done.

3

Ergonomics (German UI, accessibility)

The primary user is a single Office Manager — writing the invoices, experienced in bookkeeping, or the business owner, engineer or tradesperson themselves — not necessarily an IT specialist. The UI is German-language with UStG-compliant wording (English as fallback locale); accessibility and dark-mode support are checked on every UI/color change.

4

Operability on small hardware

The target deployment is a small Linux server on small-budget hardware (a mini PC), not a cloud cluster. Every container in docker-compose.yml carries an explicit memory limit, and the stack stays within a modest combined budget together with optional monitoring.

5

Traceability

Invoice state changes and validation results are reconstructable after the fact; DevOps-operated database and object-storage backups (Phase 1 scripts under admin-tools/scripts/backup/, expanding to the lt CLI suite in Phase 2) give the same guarantee for the underlying data — for the Office Manager’s own bookkeeping and for a tax advisor’s or auditor’s review.

6

Green IT (lean payload and storage)

Every copy a receipt or generated document produces is carried across the whole invoice chain — sender store and backup, transfer, receiver archive and backup — and occupies hardware for the retention period (invoices are kept for 8 years, books and annual statements for 10 years). Receipts are reduced at upload and embedded exactly once in a produced e-invoice (Document Size Budget, Green IT Principles) so storage and transfer volume stay lean over the whole chain, not only at the point of generation.

1.3. Scope

1.3.1. In Scope

  • Legally compliant e-invoices (X-Rechnung / ZUGFeRD per EN 16931) with PDF/A-3b output.

  • Master data, invoice lifecycle, and receipt management for a single Office Manager.

  • Asynchronous PDF/XML generation and validation via a dedicated job worker and renderer.

  • Self-hosted deployment on small server hardware; productive use is a server installation by an IT service provider, the local make dev stack is for evaluation only.

  • Issued invoices are handed over to the business’s bookkeeping (for example DATEV, Lexware or the tax advisor’s system).

1.3.2. Out of Scope

  • Peppol and other network dispatch channels — invoices are generated and stored, not sent by the invoicing application. E-mail dispatch is optional and handled by the separate mail dispatch component (kotlin-email), see System Scope and Context.

  • Bookkeeping / general-ledger accounting and DATEV export.

  • Payment tracking and open items, dunning.

  • Receiving incoming e-invoices — receipt and processing happen in the bookkeeping system.

  • Instalment invoices and final invoices with deduction of instalments (Abschlags- and Schlussrechnung).

  • Domestic reverse charge for construction services between two German businesses; reverse charge is supported for EU customers only. Domestic reverse charge would be an add-on feature on request.

  • Archiving and retention — the operator’s responsibility (invoices are kept for 8 years, books and annual statements for 10 years). Invoices are stored in the bookkeeping or archive system; because they are sent by e-mail, an e-mail archive that stores mail immutably is recommended. The application is not a document archive, see ADR-035.

  • Multi-tenancy — one operator/business per deployment.

  • Backup/restore — a DevOps operation run with Phase 1 operational scripts (admin-tools/scripts/backup/backup.sh / restore.sh) and Phase 2 consolidated CLI tooling (lt backup) against PostgreSQL and RustFS directly; not application code, not an in-app feature (see Deployment View).

1.4. Stakeholders

Role Expectations

Office Manager / Product Owner

Runs day-to-day invoicing — writes the invoices, is experienced in bookkeeping, or is the business owner, engineer or tradesperson themselves. Expects a reliable, low-maintenance system producing legally correct invoices.

Tax advisor / auditor

Consumes generated invoices, backups, and compliance artifacts. Expects consistent, complete, re-derivable records.

Invoice recipients

B2B and B2C invoice recipients (country-specific VAT-ID formats — DE/LU/AT/CH/BE/FR). Expect an e-invoice their own systems can parse and validate against EN 16931.

Developers and AI coding agents

Implement and review changes under a spec-then-delegate workflow. Expect clear architecture documentation, enforced quality gates, and greenfield-first conventions.

2. Architecture Constraints

This chapter catalogs the technical and organizational constraints that shape the architecture: language, framework, target hardware, and deployment model. They are binding decisions, not recommendations.

2.1. Technical Constraints

Constraint Rationale

Kotlin 2.4.20 / Java 25

Language and JVM target for every module (kotlin.version, java.version in the root pom.xml). No Java-only modules.

Quarkus 3.39.4

Application framework (quarkus.platform.version in the root pom.xml): CDI, persistence, REST, scheduling, health endpoints.

Vaadin 25.2.8 (Flow-based UI)

Server-side UI framework (vaadin.version in kotlin-invoice/pom.xml) for the Office-Manager-facing web application. No separate JavaScript SPA.

PostgreSQL 18 + Flyway

Relational persistence with a single editable baseline migration.

OCI-compatible container runtimes

Deployed via Docker Compose (docker-compose.yml); tested with Docker Engine/Desktop, Colima, Rancher Desktop, and Podman Desktop.

Target hardware: small Linux server on small-budget hardware

Every service in docker-compose.yml declares an explicit deploy.resources.limits.memory budget; the stack is sized for a mini PC, not a cloud cluster. The machine may be switched off in the evening; no component may assume 24/7 uptime (see Green IT Principles).

RustFS as the S3-compatible object store

A deliberate architecture decision for receipts and generated documents, including on small-budget hardware — not a filesystem fallback.

Invoice document size budget: 20 MiB

A produced e-invoice may not exceed 20 MiB: 19 distinct receipts, each embedded exactly once as Base64 inside the ZUGFeRD XML and normalized at upload towards 768 KiB, plus a 1 MiB reserve for the invoice document itself, bounded by recipients' mail gateways and the archiving path. See ADR-014 and ADR-032.

2.2. Legal Constraints

Topic Source Requirement Architectural consequence

Invoice content and sequential numbering

UStG

Mandatory invoice content; consecutive invoice number

Invoice numbers come from number ranges with gapless continuity and an append-only number journal, allocated on the transition to SENT (a storno document at its creation) (Invoice Numbering and the Number Journal).

Structured e-invoice

UStG

Electronic invoice in a structured format (EN 16931)

ZUGFeRD and XRechnung output with XSD, Schematron and KoSIT validation.

Retention

UStG, AO

Invoices are kept for 8 years, books and annual statements for 10 years

The application is not a document archive; the operator archives sent invoices (ADR-035, ADR-034).

Immutability and traceability (GoBD)

Tax-audit rules

Issued invoices must not be changed unnoticed

Issued invoices are read-only after finalisation; corrections by storno instead of edit; changes are recorded in the audit journal.

Data access during a tax audit

AO

Tax authorities can request invoice data

Provided by the operator’s archive or bookkeeping system; the application delivers XML and PDF.

Data protection

GDPR

Legal retention overrides erasure

Data of issued invoices is not erased on request while the retention period runs.

2.3. Organizational Constraints

Constraint Rationale

Solo maintainer + AI-agent workflow

A single Product Owner works with AI coding agents under a spec-then-delegate workflow: the PO specifies and reviews, agents implement.

Language layers

Source code (including comments) is English, except i18n values. Project business specifications and the frontend UI are German. Architecture documentation in the arc42 documentation module is US English.

Licensing: Apache-2.0 + CC BY 4.0

The software is licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0. The overall concept, design, layout, and core idea carry a separate attribution requirement under CC BY 4.0 — see the repository LICENSE file.

Quality gates

A build-enforced JaCoCo gate requires 90% line and 90% branch coverage (including the UI layer). Linters (ktlint, detekt, sqlfluff where migrations exist) run as mandatory Maven build gates; SonarQube Community Edition must report zero errors, and Gitleaks/Trivy security scans must pass, before a feature is considered done; false positives are a Product Owner decision.

2.4. Conventions

  • Money type: Monetary amounts use a dedicated Money value type everywhere; raw BigDecimal is reserved for quantities and factors. See ADR-001.

  • Enums as integer codes: Enumerations are persisted as small integer columns (SMALLINT or INTEGER in the baseline schema) via JPA attribute converters, never as EnumType.STRING or free-text/varchar codes. Free text is reserved for user-entered content and invoice line text. See EN 16931 Mapping in Chapter 8.

  • UStG wording: German invoicing terminology follows the Umsatzsteuergesetz (UStG) — "USt." rather than "MwSt.". "Factur-X" is spelled as such, never "Factor-X". Foreign VAT-ID designations (LU, AT, CH, BE, FR) are kept in their official form.

  • No document-path or task-ID references from code: KDoc and code comments explain why in plain domain terms; history belongs in commit messages and the architecture documentation.

  • Greenfield-first: No deprecated APIs or backward-compatibility shims in new or changed code; legacy patterns are replaced directly rather than kept alongside a fallback.

3. System Scope and Context

This chapter defines the system’s boundaries: which actors and external systems interact with Little Trotter X-Invoice, and which parts of the invoicing process are out of scope.

3.1. Business Context

The C4 system context (level 1) is the business view: who exchanges what with the system, in business terms. Channels, protocols, ports and formats are deliberately absent from the picture and listed once in Technical Context below.

Figure 1. Business context (C4 system context)

Legend: C4 Notation Reference

Canvas Views: Architecture Communication Canvas and Architecture Inception Canvas

Neighbor Role Exchanged Artifacts

Office Manager / Product Owner

Person

Master data, invoices, and receipts entered/reviewed via the web UI.

Tax advisor / auditor, invoice recipients

Person

Generated e-invoice XML (X-Rechnung / ZUGFeRD) and PDF/A-3b documents. The invoicing application never sends them; where an instance runs the optional mail dispatch component, delivery is that component’s responsibility, otherwise documents are handed over out-of-band.

Mail dispatch component (kotlin-email)

Neighboring system — its own bounded context, deployed separately, optional per instance

Receives one compose request per send (document family, reference key, recipient, placeholder values, presigned attachment links) and returns one dispatch result event. It owns the composed mail, the send journal, and the signed archive packages; this system only keeps the fact that a document was sent.

Offer module (kotlin-quote)

Neighboring system — its own bounded context, deployed separately, optional per instance

Creates, reviews and sends offers and records their acceptance or rejection on replicas of the invoicing master data. Hands an explicit invoice order over as the event quote.invoice-order.requested and receives invoice.created-from-quote in return; sends offers through the mail dispatch component.

Mailbox / SMTP provider

External system

Accepts the submitted message and archives outgoing mail on the provider side. Reached only by the mail dispatch component — the invoicing application holds no SMTP credentials.

Operator’s mail archive

External system, operated by the business, optional per instance

Receives a byte-identical copy of every dispatched invoice and quote mail by SMTP as an additional envelope recipient (journal copy, ADR-037); returns only the SMTP acceptance, which the mail component records as handover status. Reached only by the mail dispatch component. Archiving and retention stay with the operator (ADR-035).

Authentik

External system

Identity provider for Office Manager login (OIDC, authorization code flow); the application never sees a password, only the resulting ID token.

EU VIES REST API

External system

Official VAT information exchange system of the European Commission; queried on explicit demand ("VIES prüfen") to confirm foreign recipient VAT-IDs prior to tax-exempt cross-border invoicing (ADR-023).

DevOps

Person

Reviews dashboards and alerts from the monitoring stack; provisions, deploys, and maintains the server (see Deployment View).

Monitoring (Prometheus/Grafana)

External system

Scrapes application and infrastructure metrics, reviewed by DevOps; optional, operated via admin-tools/monitoring.

Backup Storage

External system

Receives age-encrypted database and object-storage backup archives copied off-site by DevOps (Phase 1 scripts under admin-tools/scripts/backup/backup.sh, Phase 2 lt backup), never by the Office Manager.

3.2. Technical Context

The table below is the technical view of the context: it has the same neighbors as the business context above and adds the channel, protocol and format behind every relationship. See Building Block View for how the system is composed inside those boundaries, from the container landscape (level 1) down to the components (level 2).

Interface Protocol Direction Purpose

Browser → Caddy → application

HTTPS

Inbound

Caddy terminates TLS in front of the Vaadin/Quarkus application (little-trotter-invoice); it is the stack’s only externally exposed service (ports 80/443).

Application ↔ PostgreSQL

JDBC

Bidirectional

Primary relational persistence (master data, invoices, receipts, jobs), schema/seed managed by an editable Flyway baseline.

Application ↔ RustFS

S3 API

Bidirectional

Object storage (S3-compatible) for receipts and generated PDF/XML documents.

Application ↔ NATS JetStream

NATS

Bidirectional

Carries small job envelopes between the interactive app, the scheduler-driven job worker (little-trotter-job-worker, same artifact, QUARKUS_PROFILE=worker), and the kotlin-xpdf renderer. Text payloads live in Valkey and PDFs/receipts in RustFS, so NATS messages stay small.

Application ↔ Valkey

Redis protocol

Bidirectional

Cache and text-payload store (PDF/query cache, trip-workbook references) shared by the app and the kotlin-xpdf renderer.

kotlin-xpdf (renderer/worker) ↔ NATS / RustFS / Valkey

NATS / S3 API / Redis protocol

Bidirectional

Consumes generation jobs, renders PDF/A-3b via Typst, generates and validates ZUGFeRD/X-Rechnung XML (Mustang Project, embedded KoSIT validator), publishes results back.

Application → Authentik

OIDC (authorization code)

Outbound

Office Manager login: the application redirects to Authentik’s authorization endpoint and exchanges the resulting code for tokens (quarkus.oidc.*); the human-readable username from the token is also stamped onto audit fields.

Application → EU VIES REST API

HTTPS REST

Outbound

Explicit on-demand recipient VAT ID verification (VatIdVerificationPort, ViesHttpClient); called only on explicit user button click, never scheduled or automated (ADR-023).

Monitoring (Prometheus/Grafana) → application and infrastructure

Metrics scrape

Inbound (pull)

Optional stack that scrapes metrics from the application and infrastructure; not fronted by Caddy, reached only via the internal invoice-net network.

Application → mail dispatch component

HTTPS, POST /mail/api/compose

Outbound

The only call this system makes towards mail dispatch: hands over the values for one mail and receives a draft id plus the URL of the component’s compose view, which the browser opens. Internal-only, authenticated with an integration token, and used only while the feature is enabled for the instance.

Mail dispatch component → application

NATS JetStream, mail.send.result

Inbound (event)

The single return path: a fire-and-forget result envelope (journal id, reference key, status, message id, dispatch timestamp) projected onto the invoice as a "sent" flag. The component never calls into the invoicing application, so no service here has to be reachable for a mail to be sent.

Application → offer module

HTTPS, /quote/api/*

Outbound

The invoicing application’s offer screens call the module’s REST API over the instance network with an integration token; Caddy answers 404 for it from outside.

Offer module → application

NATS JetStream, quote.invoice-order.requested

Inbound (event)

Invoice order for an offer as a reference event (order id, offer reference, optional delivery date or service period); the application creates the invoice draft and answers with invoice.created-from-quote or invoice.creation-from-quote.rejected. In the other direction the application publishes master data (masterdata.>) to the offer module’s replicas.

Administration (little-trotter-admin / admin-tools)

Operational scripts (Phase 1) / Go CLI & TUI (lt, lhorse, Phase 2)

Operational

Staged DevOps tooling for server provisioning, deployment, database maintenance, and age-encrypted backup/restore (admin-tools/): Phase 1 shell automation (MVP until Q4/2027); Phase 2 consolidated Go CLI (lt) and TUI (lhorse) (Q4/2027–Q1/2028 after phase-gate hardening). Not part of the core application build.

3.3. Bounded Context Relationship: Mail Dispatch

Little Trotter X-Invoice (Invoicing) and the mail dispatch component (kotlin-email) are two separate bounded contexts related through a Published Language: kotlin-mail-contract (de.littletrotter.shared.mail), a small, independently versioned module both sides depend on instead of either depending on the other’s domain model. Neither context ever imports the other’s own types; each translates the shared contract into its own local model at exactly one adapter boundary — an Anti-Corruption Layer (ACL) on both sides.

  • Invoicing is upstream for the compose command: it calls POST /mail/api/compose with a MailComposeRequest; the mail component’s own acl adapter package translates that into its local ComposeMailCommand — the mail component never sees an Invoicing domain type.

  • Mail dispatch is upstream for the result: it publishes one dispatch-result event per send attempt over NATS JetStream (mail.send.result), which Invoicing’s own adapter translates into the "sent" flag it projects onto the invoice — Invoicing never sees a mail-journal row or template.

The relationship is therefore a bidirectional Published Language, not a single upstream/downstream pair: each context is upstream for the one interaction it owns and downstream for the other. The mail component is its own self-contained system with an independent release cycle (see ADR-015 and the Self-Contained System glossary entry); Invoicing keeps functioning with the feature switched off (LT_MAIL_ENABLED=false), and the compose call is the only synchronous dependency it has on the mail component even while the feature is enabled.

3.3.1. Archive of the Operator as a Neighbor

The archive is a neighboring system of the mail dispatch component, not of the invoicing application. After a mail is sent, the component delivers the same message bytes to the configured archive address (envelope recipient, no header entry) and keeps the result in its journal as MailArchiveHandoverStatus. Invoicing only reads that status through the shared contract. The component’s signed archive package is an evidence artifact, not a copy that replaces the operator’s archive.

3.4. Out of Scope

  • Peppol network transmission and transport channels outside the optional kotlin-email contract. This system generates and stores invoices; mail composition and SMTP delivery remain in the separate mail system.

  • Bookkeeping / general-ledger accounting.

  • Multi-tenancy — one operator per deployment.

4. Solution Strategy

4.1. Overview

The table below maps each of the six quality goals from Introduction and Goals, in the same order and under the same names, to the architectural approach chosen to meet it, consistent with the Architecture Communication Canvas.

Quality Goal Approach Rationale

Domain correctness

EN 16931 domain model as a typed B2bTerm enum (kotlin-shared, one constant per BT number), persisted as SMALLINT, never free-text. Multi-stage validation (XSD, EN 16931 Schematron, embedded KoSIT) gates issuance.

An invalid e-invoice is worse than none; the type system and validation pipeline make the invalid state unreachable rather than merely discouraged.

Test coverage and quality gates

A build-enforced JaCoCo gate (90/90, including the UI), mandatory linters, SonarQube plus Gitleaks/Trivy, and independent E2E layers — Karibu component and monkey tests (kotlin-e2e-tests), and Playwright browser E2E (sibling little-trotter-admin-tools repository).

Automated, non-negotiable gates substitute for the manual review bandwidth a solo Product-Owner-plus-agents team lacks (quality despite AI-delegated development); each E2E layer catches a different failure class.

Ergonomics (German UI, accessibility)

Server-side Vaadin Flow UI in German with UStG-compliant wording (English as fallback locale); accessibility and dark-mode support are checked on every UI/color change.

The primary user is a single Office Manager, not necessarily an IT specialist; one Kotlin toolchain and no separate frontend keep the UI consistent with the domain.

Operability on small hardware

Modulith (kotlin-invoice) plus a small renderer/worker (kotlin-xpdf) and a scheduler-driven job worker (same artifact, QUARKUS_PROFILE=worker), one Docker Compose stack, Caddy for TLS.

No microservice mesh, no API gateway, no service discovery — the target is small-budget hardware, not a cloud cluster (ops minimalism).

Traceability

Explicit invoice state model with validation results kept per invoice; DevOps-operated database and object-storage backups (Phase 1 scripts under admin-tools/scripts/backup/, Phase 2 lt backup).

State changes and validation results are reconstructable after the fact, for the Office Manager’s own bookkeeping and for a tax advisor’s or auditor’s review.

Green IT (lean payload and storage)

Receipts are reduced at upload and embedded exactly once in a produced e-invoice (Document Size Budget, ADR-032); small NATS envelopes keep payloads out of the messaging layer.

Every copy of a receipt or generated document occupies hardware across the whole invoice chain for the retention period, so volume is kept lean at the source.

4.1.1. Further Strategies

Strategies that serve several goals at once and therefore have no single quality-goal row.

Topic Approach Rationale

Robust messaging on small hardware

NATS JetStream carries only small job envelopes; text payloads live in Valkey, PDFs and receipts in RustFS.

Small message bodies keep NATS itself small and fast (128 MB budget) and avoid duplicating large payloads across redeliveries.

Greenfield velocity

The Flyway baseline migration is edited in place rather than accumulating incremental migrations; legacy/deprecated code is deleted outright.

Avoids schema-migration archaeology and dead-code accumulation while the domain model is still settling.

4.3. Architectural Approach

  • Modulith, not microservices — kotlin-invoice is a single deployable Quarkus/Vaadin application organized internally by package (ui, application, domain, infrastructure, spi; see Building Block View) with explicit, enforced module boundaries rather than an undifferentiated codebase. The renderer/worker (kotlin-xpdf) and the job worker (same artifact, different Quarkus profile) are the only other JVM services within this same self-contained system, split out because rendering and scheduled re-publishing benefit from running outside the request-handling JVM. A capability with its own release cycle and no shared internals becomes its own self-contained system instead, as mail dispatch does (ADR-015).

  • Hexagonal-leaning layering inside the app — domain defines repository ports (de.littletrotter.domain.repository.*); infrastructure.persistence provides the Panache/JPA adapters (e.g. PanacheReceiptRepository); application orchestrates use cases; ui only talks to application.

  • Server-side UI, no separate frontend build — Vaadin Flow keeps the UI in Kotlin alongside the domain, avoiding a second toolchain and a REST/GraphQL API surface for a single-operator browser UI.

  • Asynchronous document generation — PDF/XML generation is never inline with a user request; it is queued as a GenerationJob, delegated over NATS, and pushed back to the UI (Vaadin push) when the renderer finishes, keeping request latency independent of rendering time.

  • Shared types, not duplicated logic — kotlin-shared compiles into both kotlin-invoice and kotlin-xpdf (B2B/EN 16931 terms, Money, NATS stream setup, payload contracts), so both processes agree on wire formats without a runtime dependency between them.

  • Test projects as first-class citizens — every test-pyramid layer is its own project (kotlin-e2e-tests, coverage-report in this reactor; e2e-test-playwright in the sibling little-trotter-admin-tools repository); see Building Block View.

4.4. Correctness Strategy

  • Typed B2B terms — B2bTerm (kotlin-shared) models every EN 16931 business term as an enum constant carrying its BT number, binding kind, and value-domain metadata; invariants (e.g. a FREE binding requires an xpath) are enforced in init and unit-tested against invalid input.

  • Multi-stage validation before issuance — generated ZUGFeRD/X-Rechnung XML passes XSD schema validation, EN 16931 Schematron rules, and the embedded KoSIT validator (Mustang Project library, kotlin-xpdf); optional veraPDF (PDF/A-3b) and LLM-based checks can be enabled per deployment.

  • Money as a value type — monetary amounts use a dedicated Money type (kotlin-shared); raw BigDecimal is reserved for quantities and factors.

  • Enums as integer codes — every enumeration is persisted through a JPA attribute converter to a small integer column, never EnumType.STRING or a free-text code, so an invalid value cannot silently enter the database as a string that happens to look right.

4.5. Quality Assurance Strategy

Figure 2. Quality gates from commit to release

  • Coverage gate — an aggregated JaCoCo bundle check (coverage-report, report-aggregate) enforces 90% line and 90% branch coverage across every module including the UI, combining unit, integration, and E2E exec data into one report so no layer under-counts the others' coverage.

  • Mandatory linters — ktlint, detekt, and sqlfluff (for migrations) run as Maven build-gate executions (make test), not optional CI steps; a missing linter binary fails the build preflight rather than silently skipping the gate.

  • Static analysis — SonarQube Community Edition analyzes compiled bytecode via the Maven scanner plugin against the LAN instance, fed by the same JaCoCo coverage data, and runs on release tags and manual dispatch (.forgejo/workflows/cicd.yml); false positives are a Product Owner decision, never a blanket exclusion (CDI findings in particular are always resolved architecturally, e.g. via constructor injection, never excluded).

  • Security gates — Gitleaks scans staged changes on every commit (pre-commit hook installed by make hooks) and the full git history on release tags in CI; Trivy scans the checkout’s dependencies and Dockerfile/compose configuration (trivy fs, .trivyignore.yaml) on the same release-tag job, then gates every container image again before it is pushed (make publish, .github/workflows/publish-images.yml).

  • Layered E2E strategy — Karibu-based component tests (kotlin-e2e-tests) exercise Vaadin views without a browser; the same project’s Karibu-based monkey tests (tagged monkey) generate randomized interaction sequences to surface robustness issues the scripted suites do not anticipate; Playwright drives a real browser against an isolated compose stack from the sibling little-trotter-admin-tools repository. See Building Block View for how each is invoked.

5. Building Block View

This chapter presents the system in hierarchical layers: the runtime containers (Level 1), the components within the main application (Level 2), the cross-cutting types used by multiple modules, and the test and tooling projects that enforce quality gates.

5.1. Level 1: Container Landscape

The diagram and table below cover the services declared in the root docker-compose.yml (profile core). arc42’s "Level 1" here corresponds to the container level of Simon Brown’s C4 model — C4’s own "Level 1" is the system context, covered in System Scope and Context.

Figure 3. Container landscape

The diagram reflects the Ubuntu-server target, not "one Docker host": PostgreSQL, RustFS and Caddy run natively under systemd, shared by every instance on the box, while the application layer (little-trotter-invoice, little-trotter-job-worker, kotlin-xpdf, NATS JetStream, Valkey, plus the optional email-app of the mail component) is Docker Compose, one project per instance (prod/dev/demo). One aggregated dependency edge stands for the many application-to-infrastructure connections; the per-container protocol (JDBC, S3 API, NATS, Redis protocol) is the detail level the table below carries. Authentik and monitoring are drawn once, as shared, cross-cutting Docker services outside every instance project: Authentik is the OIDC identity provider every Office Manager logs in through (the web app is a Quarkus OIDC web-app client), and additionally the forward-auth gate of the server’s admin site.

The dedicated single-instance variant (small-budget hardware — a mini PC or another standalone host, no instance model) instead runs the whole stack — Postgres, RustFS, Caddy included — as Docker containers from this repository’s own docker-compose.yml; see Deployment View for that variant’s own table.

Container Technology Runtime on the server Responsibility + Interfaces

little-trotter-invoice

Kotlin, Quarkus, Vaadin Flow

Docker, per instance

Interactive web application: master data, invoice lifecycle, receipts, and statistics. Reads/writes PostgreSQL over JDBC, reads/writes RustFS over the S3 API, publishes generation-job envelopes to and consumes results from NATS JetStream, and uses Valkey for caching and text payloads. Behind Caddy; the only container reachable from a browser.

little-trotter-job-worker

Kotlin, Quarkus

Docker, per instance

Scheduler-driven job worker — the same build artifact as little-trotter-invoice, deployed as a separate container with QUARKUS_PROFILE=worker and QUARKUS_SCHEDULER_ENABLED=true. One @Scheduled pass, invoice.jobs.recovery-interval (default 1h), covers both re-publishing persisted PENDING generation jobs and resetting DELEGATED jobs older than staleCutoffMinutes (default 2 minutes) back to PENDING — one interval, not two independently-firing timers. That age only makes a job a candidate; the reset itself checks the job’s own message against the real document-jobs stream before acting on it — see `kotlin-xpdf’s entry below and Generate E-Invoice for the full rule. No UI, no externally exposed port, no OIDC/Authentik route.

kotlin-xpdf

Kotlin, Quarkus

Docker, per instance

Renderer/worker service: PDF/A-3b rendering through BasePdfRendererPort (Typst CLI, the only shipped engine), ZUGFeRD/XRechnung XML generation and multi-stage validation (Mustang Project library, embedded KoSIT validator; veraPDF and an optional local-LLM check are configurable per deployment). Consumes generation jobs from NATS via two durable pull consumers, one per lane (bulk: kotlin-xpdf-group, subject document-jobs, JOB_WORKER_THREADS; interactive: kotlin-xpdf-interactive, subject document-jobs-interactive, JOB_INTERACTIVE_WORKER_THREADS, default 1; each with its own executor and heartbeat), publishes results back, reads/writes RustFS, and uses Valkey to resolve text-payload references. Each pulled message is leased for a configurable ackWaitSeconds (default 120s) and the lease is renewed by a periodic message.inProgress() heartbeat for the full duration of a job (JobConsumer, ackWaitSeconds / 3 interval), started the moment a message is handed to the bounded worker thread pool rather than only once a thread actually begins processing it — protects a genuinely slow render from being mistaken for a hung worker and redelivered even when a burst queues more jobs than the pool can immediately start. Exposes internal-only REST endpoints (/validate, /documents/presign) — not reachable through Caddy.

PostgreSQL

PostgreSQL 18

Native (systemd), shared by every instance

Primary relational store for master data, invoices, receipts, and generation jobs. One cluster per server; one database and one role-trio (lt_migrator_<instance>/lt_app_<instance>) per instance, since PostgreSQL roles are cluster-wide. Schema/seed managed by a single editable Flyway baseline (no live instance yet, see Architecture Constraints).

RustFS

RustFS (S3-compatible)

Native (systemd), shared by every instance

Object storage for receipts and generated PDF/XML documents; one bucket per instance (invoice-documents[-dev|-demo]). A deliberate architecture decision, including on the target small-budget hardware — not a filesystem fallback.

NATS JetStream

NATS 2.x

Docker, per instance

Message bus carrying only small job envelopes between little-trotter-invoice, little-trotter-job-worker, and kotlin-xpdf (streams LITTLE_TROTTER_JOBS, LITTLE_TROTTER_DLQ; see Key Interfaces). Payload rule: text lives in Valkey, PDFs/receipts in RustFS, so NATS messages stay small on constrained hardware. One broker per instance (own client/monitoring ports, offset by instance).

Valkey

Valkey (Redis-protocol)

Docker, per instance

Cache and text-payload store shared by little-trotter-invoice and kotlin-xpdf: PDF/query cache, trip-workbook references for job envelopes, and the receipt-checksum fast path for duplicate detection. One instance per instance project, no published port.

Caddy

Caddy

Native (systemd), shared by every instance

TLS-terminating reverse proxy — the stack’s only externally exposed service (ports 80/443). One shared installation generates one vhost per instance (prod/dev/demo), each reverse proxying to that instance’s own loopback app port; every other container stays unreachable from outside the server.

Why Postgres and RustFS are native, and Valkey stays in Docker. PostgreSQL and RustFS are shared, stateful services used by every instance on the box — running three separate clusters or object stores in Docker volumes would triple the memory/disk footprint for per-instance data, complicate backups (one sweep per instance instead of one shared cluster), and keep the data inside Docker’s storage driver instead of the filesystem it is easier to back up and migrate from. Caddy is native for the same reason: one systemd install serves every instance’s vhost instead of three TLS-terminating containers fighting over ports 80/443. Valkey, by contrast, is a deliberately disposable cache — losing it costs a warm-up, never data — so it stays in Docker, one instance per compose project, matching the lifecycle of the application it caches for.

5.2. Mail Dispatch — a Separate Self-Contained System (kotlin-email)

E-mail dispatch is its own bounded context, developed as the module kotlin-email of this repository (artifact little-trotter-email) and deployed as its own image per instance, optional and off by default; its only code dependencies are kotlin-mail-contract (the frozen published language both sides map in their own adapters) and kotlin-shared. It owns the mail templates, the append-only send journal and SMTP submission, and is headless: it serves only a REST API, while the mail views live in the invoicing application (see Mail UI in the Invoicing Application). This system hands it values to compose a mail and receives back only a result event, projected onto two read-only columns on the invoice — it holds no Invoice entity, no database access, and no key into this component’s data. See ADR-015.

Module Responsibility

email-core

Template and journal domain, versioning, placeholder rendering; plain Kotlin with JPA annotations.

email-service

Application core and adapters: mail schema (templates, append-only send journal), S3 storage of the authored document and the sent .eml, SMTP submission, signed archive packages, NATS results, REST API /mail/api/*.

email-app

The one deployable: email-service as a headless Quarkus image under /mail. Callers present a bearer token and the forwarded X-Lt-User/X-Lt-Groups headers; the component checks the groups again server-side (MailEdgeIdentityMechanism, MailRoleProvider).

email-e2e-tests

Full-stack tests against email-app on real Testcontainers (Postgres, NATS, Valkey, RustFS, Mailpit), driven through the REST API only; test harness, outside the coverage aggregate.

Delivery state. The component is part of the repository but not of the end-user delivery: the delivered configuration runs without the email-app image and without the mail schema. The deployment switch MAIL_ENABLED (mail.enabled, default false) controls it. While it is off, the HTTP dispatch adapter is not instantiated, the mail-result NATS consumer binds no subscription, the mail entry in the navigation drawer and the send action on the invoice are absent, and the mail card in the settings shows "not installed". The independent per-instance offer flag is described in Feature Switches.

Inside kotlin-email, its REST and NATS adapters reach the application layer only through dedicated input ports in de.littletrotter.email.service.application.port.inbound (ADR-027) — one *QueryPort/*UseCase per use case, its own CommandEntryPoint marker (the component does not import the one kotlin-invoice uses), and entity-free read models (MailTemplateView, MailJournalEntryView) in place of the JPA-backed MailTemplate/MailJournal entities:

Interface (input port) Purpose Consumer (adapter caller)

ComposeMailDraftUseCase / MailDraftQueryPort / ConsumeMailDraftUseCase

Stage a compose draft, look it up, mark it consumed after send

ComposeResource

SendMailUseCase / RetryMailSendUseCase

Enqueue a dispatch; rebuild and re-enqueue a failed one

SendResource / JournalResource

ProcessMailSendOrderUseCase

Consume a send-requested envelope and run the actual SMTP dispatch

MailSendNatsAdapter

BuildMailArchivePackageUseCase / RearchiveMailUseCase

Sign and store the archive package for a sent mail; repair a failed one

MailSendNatsAdapter / JournalResource

MailJournalQueryPort

Read send history

MailTemplateQueryPort / UpsertMailTemplateUseCase / ResetMailTemplateUseCase / SendTemplateBccTestUseCase

RenderPreviewResource

EmailInputPortArchitectureTest enforces the cut (naming, no composite ports, adapters never depend on a concrete application service or on email.core.repository.. directly).

5.2.1. Mail UI in the Invoicing Application

The mail views are Vaadin Flow routes of kotlin-invoice, not part of the component:

Route View Purpose

mail/compose/:draftId

MailComposeView

Review and send a staged compose draft; the draft is consumed only when the send is queued.

mail/journal

MailJournalView

Send journal with server-side filter, sort and paging, status chips, URL state, row details, Excel export, retry and re-archive.

mail/templates

MailTemplatesView with MailTemplateEditorDialog and MailTemplateHistoryDialog

Template list, editing with preview and Bcc test, version history, reset.

RouteAccess gates them with the AppPermission`s `MAIL_COMPOSE_AND_SEND, MAIL_JOURNAL_VIEW and MAIL_TEMPLATE_EDIT. The views inject only narrow input ports in application.mail.port.inbound (one role interface per use case, no composites). Query ports read through outbound ports; command ports run through application services and outbound ports such as MailDeliveryPort, MailTemplateWritePort and MailJournalActionPort, plus the template reset and Bcc-test ports. The outbound ports are implemented by HTTP adapters in infrastructure.mail, which call /mail/api/* and translate the contract types in their acl package.

5.2.2. Quote Module Interfaces to the Invoicing Application

The quote module (kotlin-quote, optional package) is a self-contained system next to the mail component, built from quote-core (domain without framework), quote-service (application layer, adapters, Flyway baseline) and quote-app (the Quarkus deployable, REST under /quote/api/*; the screens are Flow routes of the invoicing application). The aggregate Quote runs through the single status DRAFT, REVIEW, SENT, ACCEPTED, INVOICED, REJECTED or CANCELLED: DRAFT → REVIEW/CANCELLED; REVIEW → SENT/DRAFT (return with a note)/CANCELLED; SENT → ACCEPTED/REJECTED (a reason is required); ACCEPTED → INVOICED. An invoice order (table quote.quote_invoice_order, status REQUESTED/CREATED/REJECTED, at most one open or created order per offer) is allowed from SENT (acceptance is recorded implicitly) and ACCEPTED; acceptance alone creates no invoice. The content is editable only in DRAFT, the number is assigned on entering REVIEW. There is no second outcome attribute, no derived expiry and no revision; the validity date is a document field on the PDF. INVOICED is reached only through the reply event invoice.created-from-quote to an order. See ADR-036. Every use case has its own inbound port in application.quote.port.inbound; the module publishes through an outbox relay.

Offer statistics end at "sent": an offer in SENT, ACCEPTED, INVOICED or REJECTED counts as sent. There is no win rate, no rejection-reason statistic and no offer-to-invoice figure. The only schema addition is quote.quote.sent_on (DATE), set on REVIEW → SENT from the actor timestamp of the time source and kept through the later statuses; check constraints require it exactly for these four statuses and sent_on >= quote_date, and the index idx_quote_date (quote_date) INCLUDE (status) serves the period filter. The blocks:

Block Responsibility

quote-core, package statistics

Pure value objects StatisticsPeriod, QuoteFacts, QuoteKpis and QuoteStatusCounts; every figure (sent volume, count, average offer value, in progress, average days to sent) is defined once here.

quote-service

Read models QuoteStatisticsDashboardReadModel and QuoteRankingReadModel behind the ports QuoteStatisticsDashboardQueryPort and QuoteRankingQueryPort. SQL delivers per-offer facts with the net amount per line rounded to cents; the value objects aggregate them.

quote-app

QuoteStatisticsResource with GET /statistics/dashboard and GET /statistics/ranking (permission QUOTE_VIEW); views QuoteDashboardView and QuoteRankingRowView live in the quote contract.

kotlin-invoice

Narrow input ports for the dashboard and the ranking, HTTP adapters and an anti-corruption layer that maps the contract views. QuoteStatisticsView is built from the shared statistics components (ui/component/statistics) through QuoteStatisticsPresenter, mirroring the invoice statistics view.

The quote module keeps its own schema quote. It receives master data through replica tables fed by NATS events masterdata.> and the snapshot. Besides the existing replicas it holds number_range_replica, the definition of the quote number (prefix, date part, counter width, reset cycle); only number ranges with the subject QUOTE are replicated. The counter itself is quote.quote_number_range per (range_code, period_key) (ADR-033).

The quote settings (validity in days, XML format) stay in quote.quote_setting. Their user interface is the card "Angebot" of the settings page in kotlin-invoice, shown only when the quote module is deployed; it reaches the settings through the input ports QuoteSettingsQueryPort and UpdateQuoteSettingsUseCase.

5.3. Library Modules

Besides the two JVM processes of the invoicing system and the mail component, the reactor carries seven library modules that are never deployed on their own:

Module Responsibility + consumers

kotlin-shared

Neutral cross-cutting types (Money, DTOs, document-size budget, B2B terms); used by the invoicing modules and kotlin-email. Detailed below.

kotlin-mail-contract

Published language between invoicing and mail dispatch (package de.littletrotter.shared.mail): data classes, enums, schema version, one wire codec; no logic. Frozen — changes are additive and carry a new schema version.

kotlin-quote-contract

Published language of the offer-to-invoice hand-over (package de.littletrotter.shared.quote): wire DTOs, the subjects quote.invoice-order.requested, invoice.created-from-quote and invoice.creation-from-quote.rejected (both replies carry the order id), the streams LITTLE_TROTTER_QUOTE_INVOICE_ORDER and LITTLE_TROTTER_QUOTE_INVOICE, one codec; no logic.

kotlin-quotation-xml

UBL Quotation writer with XSD and Schematron validation, used by quote-service to embed the structured document in the offer PDF.

kotlin-ui-components

Vaadin component jar without Quarkus/CDI of its own, shared by host applications; first tenant is the free-text editor with its chip row.

kotlin-facturx

Engine client and validation stages used by kotlin-xpdf. Detailed below; its French-market Factur-X route is on standby.

kotlin-architecture-rules

Shared ArchUnit rules (first: the time rules of ADR-029) that every module checks its own production classes against; test scope only, enforced by the root POM (ADR-038).

5.3.1. kotlin-shared — Cross-Cutting Building Block

kotlin-shared is a compiled dependency of both little-trotter-invoice and kotlin-xpdf (not a deployable container in its own right); it is what lets the two JVM processes agree on wire formats and domain vocabulary without depending on each other directly.

Area Contents

B2B / EN 16931 terms (shared.b2b)

B2bTerm — one enum constant per BT number with its binding/xpath/ value-domain metadata — plus the EN 16931 code lists.

Shared value types

Money (all monetary amounts; raw BigDecimal is reserved for quantities/factors), Currency, GermanTime, VatDesignation.

Receipt size subdomain (shared.receipt)

DocumentSize, ReceiptSizePolicy, InvoiceDocumentBudget and the Base64WithReserveEstimator — the document-budget value types, policy and estimator, shared so kotlin-xpdf and kotlin-invoice apply one set of numbers instead of each keeping its own copy. See ADR-014 and ADR-032.

Messaging contracts

Connection/stream setup and payload-reference types used by the NATS producers and consumers (see Messaging).

Mail dispatch contract (shared.mail)

The published language between this system and the mail dispatch context — compose requests, placeholder values, the send-result event. Contracts only, no entities or domain logic; each side maps at its own adapter. See ADR-016.

DTOs shared across the wire

Invoice, party, receipt, and B2B-value DTOs used across the UI and export layers.

Document/validation ports

Ports for object storage and for PDF/XML/KoSIT/veraPDF/LLM validation, implemented differently by little-trotter-invoice and kotlin-xpdf.

5.3.2. kotlin-facturx — Factur-X Client/Validation Library

kotlin-facturx is a compiled dependency of kotlin-xpdf (not a deployable container in its own right, same relationship kotlin-shared has to both JVM processes above): the client for the separate facturx-engine HTTP container, its DTO mapping, and the local Schematron/BR-FR-CTC validation stages that run in-process. Everything that remains once the actual document generation has been delegated out to facturx-engine — a natural module boundary, unlike the in-process Mustang CII exporter, which stays part of kotlin-xpdf itself (de.littletrotter.xpdf.impl.mustang).

Status. The Mustang/CII route (ZUGFeRD, XRechnung) is the developed one. The Factur-X engine route and the French-market rules (BR-FR-CTC, SIRET) are on standby: kept in the code, not developed further.

Area Contents

Engine port + HTTP adapter

FacturXEngine (the port) and HttpFacturXEngineClient (the only HTTP in this codebase’s Factur-X route) — generate/convert/validate against the pinned facturx-engine container.

DTO mapping

FacturXMetadataMapper and the InvoiceMetadata wire DTOs — maps this system’s InvoiceDto onto the engine’s own JSON schema; FacturXTaxAggregation/FacturXDateMapping are the two extracted sub-concerns it depends on.

Local validation stages

FacturXSchematronValidator (the official Factur-X 1.09.2 Schematron, compiled to XSLT, embedded) and BrFrCtcValidator (the BR-FR-CTC 1.4.0 rule set, run locally rather than via the engine’s own French-seller-only execution) — both run in-process against the generated CII XML, no second container.

Response mapping

FacturXValidationResponseMapper/SvrlFailedAsserts — turns the engine’s/Schematron’s own result shapes into this system’s ValidationMessage/ValidationStageResult vocabulary.

Three of these classes (HttpFacturXEngineClient, FacturXSchematronValidator, BrFrCtcValidator) are plain classes rather than @ApplicationScoped CDI beans. Quarkus’s build-time ArC bean discovery does not find an @ApplicationScoped bean declared in a dependency module the way it finds one in the consuming module’s own Jandex index — verified live, not assumed. kotlin-facturx therefore carries plain, CDI-unaware classes (the same shape kotlin-shared’s own `AbstractS3DocumentStore uses), and kotlin-xpdf owns three small @ApplicationScoped CDI producer beans (FacturXEngineProducer, FacturXSchematronValidatorProducer, BrFrCtcValidatorProducer) that read config and construct the real instances.

5.4. Test and Tooling Projects

The test and tooling projects are first-class building blocks. This repository ships the product and the test layers that exercise it without a browser; the browser-driven E2E suite, the load/monkey tooling, and the DevOps tooling live in the sibling, private little-trotter-admin-tools repository, checked out alongside this one. Each project below is its own Maven project with its own invocation, feeding a specific quality gate (see Solution Strategy).

Project Purpose Invocation + Quality Gate

kotlin-e2e-tests

Kotlin system/integration E2E tests exercising real flows across module boundaries against a real database and, where relevant, S3 — without a browser. Also carries a Karibu-based monkey suite: bounded, seeded random walks over the real Vaadin view tree, driven headlessly.

Part of the Maven reactor (./mvnw verify, make test); feeds coverage-report’s aggregate JaCoCo bundle. The `monkey/stress tags are excluded by default and run explicitly with a chosen seed/step count.

email-e2e-tests

Full-stack and Playwright tests of the mail component against its deployable email-app.

Part of the reactor while the mail profile is active (default); outside the coverage aggregate, the graded production code is email-app.

coverage-report (ci/coverage-report)

Aggregates JaCoCo exec data from every module into one project-wide report, so coverage is measured once across unit, integration, and E2E tests.

Runs last in the Maven reactor; enforces the 90% line / 90% branch bundle gate that make test reports as GREEN or FAILED.

e2e-test-playwright (sibling repository)

Browser-driven system E2E covering the full stack through a real UI. Reads this application’s i18n texts at runtime rather than from this source tree, so neither repository depends on the other at compile time or by path.

Invoked from the sibling repository (make playwright/make qa); feeds its own happy-path/ border-case gap analysis, not this repository’s JaCoCo gate.

admin-tools / little-trotter-admin (sibling repository)

DevOps tooling and operations management following a staged rollout: Phase 1 lightweight shell automation and reference configurations (MVP until Q4/2027); Phase 2 consolidated Go CLI suite (lt) and Commander TUI (lhorse) (Q4/2027–Q1/2028 after operational hardening gates); optional Prometheus/Grafana monitoring and Playwright-based load/monkey tooling.

Invoked from the sibling repository; has its own test suites outside this repository’s JVM coverage gate.

5.5. Level 2: Components of little-trotter-invoice

The component view below reflects the top-level packages under kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/.

Figure 4. Component View — little-trotter-invoice
Component Package Responsibility

UI

de.littletrotter.ui

Vaadin Flow views, reusable UI components (e.g. VatIdField, the shared VAT-id field with visible prefix recognition), i18n, navigation, notifications, and UI-level security guards. Talks only to application — each view or component injects just the input ports it uses — plus spi for module-contributed form sections.

Application

de.littletrotter.application

Use-case orchestration and DTO mapping across invoice lifecycle, master/reference data, compliance, messaging, and caching. Exposes one input port per use case in application.<context>.port.inbound — *QueryPort for reads, *UseCase for writes — and no composite interfaces (ADR-027, Input Ports). Depends on domain and calls infrastructure adapters, never the reverse.

Domain

de.littletrotter.domain

Domain model and business rules, independent of persistence/messaging technology; holds the repository ports that infrastructure implements, plus domain events and value objects (e.g. VatIdValidator, the single VAT-ID format validator shared by invoice recipient, invoice issuer profile, and invoice issuer VAT registration rows).

Infrastructure

de.littletrotter.infrastructure

Adapters implementing the domain’s repository ports: persistence (Panache/JPA), messaging (NATS producer/consumer), storage (S3/RustFS), cache (Valkey), EU VAT-ID verification (ViesHttpClient, the VatIdVerificationPort adapter for the EU VIES REST API), and receipt PDF normalisation (infrastructure.receipt.PdfBoxReceiptPdfReducer, PDFBox, the application.invoice.port.outbound.ReceiptPdfReducer adapter — see Document Size Budget). Also holds developer-only demo-data fixtures, gated to DEVELOPMENT launch mode.

SPI

de.littletrotter.spi

Extension points for module-specific behavior — form fields, master-data extensions, and party-identifier contracts — used across application, domain, and ui.

5.6. Domain Model

The containers and components above say where code runs, not what the domain is made of. The domain model lives in the Quarkus application as JPA entities under de.littletrotter.domain in kotlin-invoice; the database is only the storage behind it. The diagram shows the core invoicing cluster — the eleven entity classes an invoice actually touches, out of roughly 48 — with their Kotlin attribute types (Money, InvoiceNumber, enums) and the associations as declared in code. Child rows (addresses, party snapshots, documents, tax variants), the B2B field catalog, the per-diem rate catalog, the compliance log and system messaging are left out on purpose: they add nodes without changing the picture. A business trip’s own children (TripExpense, TravelExpenseEntry, ADR-021) are the one exception, included because they are the shape of a whole invoice position, not incidental detail.

Figure 5. Domain Model — Core Invoicing Cluster
Entity Responsibility Key invariants (enforced in code)

Invoice

The billing document: header data, the three monetary totals, lifecycle status, one currency shared with every item.

The invoice number is assigned at the SENT transition and never changes; a storno (CORRECTED_INVOICE) is the exact negative of the invoice it cancels and is read-only from the moment it exists; dueDate never precedes invoiceDate; an explicit per-invoice seller override wins over the linked sender profile. status has a private setter — transitionTo (state-machine-checked) and the construction-time-only withInitialStatus factory are the only ways to set it (ADR-025); items/documents/partySnapshots are typed List, not MutableList, so addItem/removeItem/applyPartySnapshots remain the only mutation path.

InvoiceItem

One billed position: quantity × net unit price plus VAT, optionally created from a catalogue item (Article).

The tax snapshot (rule code, category, rate, exemption reason) is frozen once and never rewritten after the invoice is read-only; quantity is never negative — a rebate carries its sign in the unit price; position ids are never reused after a deletion.

TripExpense

One expense line (hotel, transport, mileage, taxi, other) of a TRIP position (ADR-021).

Only ever created under an InvoiceItem of category TRIP; its net amount is derived the same "quantity × price, rounded before multiply" way InvoiceItem derives its own; a storno negates its unit price, never its quantity.

TravelExpenseEntry

One per-diem day of a TRIP position, carrying its own rate snapshot and deduction flags.

Only ever created under an InvoiceItem of category TRIP; its calculated claimable amount is never negative — a storno’s credit for the per-diem share is taken at the trip total instead (ADR-021).

Recipient

An invoice recipient (master data).

Recipient number, name and e-mail are mandatory; the VAT ID is mandatory unless the recipient is a consumer; the short hash is assigned once.

SenderProfile

An invoice issuer identity the Office Manager issues invoices under — usually one, several are possible.

Name, invoice prefix, e-mail, tax number, VAT id and IBAN are mandatory; the invoice prefix is unique; at most one profile is the default.

Article (Catalogue item)

A catalogue position (material, service, unit price) a billed position can be created from.

Name mandatory, default quantity positive, default price not negative; at most one default; a position keeps its own copied price and tax snapshot when the catalogue item changes later.

TaxRate

A VAT rule: rate plus EN 16931 category and validity window.

Rate within 0–100; the category constrains the row (AE forces rate 0 and the exemption reason, S is never 0 %); rules sharing a code never have overlapping validity windows.

Receipt

An uploaded document, content-addressed by its SHA-256 checksum, stored in RustFS.

Object key and checksum are unique; a receipt is never owned by an invoice — it is linked to invoices and positions and managed on its own. Every field (description, Belegdatum, file name, object key, checksum, size) becomes immutable once any linked invoice is issued (isIssued: SENT/CANCELLED), enforced by a DB trigger too.

NumberRange

Reference data for how invoice, recipient, catalogue and issuer numbers are formatted and allocated.

A gapless range allocates in the same transaction as the document it numbers; a yearly reset requires a date part in the format; nothing references a number range — application services mediate it.

GenerationJob

One PDF/e-invoice rendering attempt, tracked across the NATS round trip to kotlin-xpdf.

Status changes only along the job state machine’s legal edges; attempts are capped before a job is failed for good; the rendering engine is snapshotted per job.

5.6.1. Aggregates at a Glance

5.7. Excel Export

de.littletrotter.infrastructure.export.workbook (kotlin-invoice only) turns invoice data into a real .xlsx so an accountant can independently recompute the totals: every money cell stays numeric and every control column is a genuine formula, not a pre-rendered string. See ADR-018 for the choice of Apache POI’s streaming writer.

Component Role

WorkbookWriter / SheetWriter / RowWriter

Generic streaming layer (SXSSFWorkbook, bounded row window, cached cell styles); one typed method per cell kind so a caller cannot write a number as text.

CellStyles

One cached style per format, embedding the currency’s ISO code in the cell’s own number format so the cell stays summable.

InvoiceHeaderSheetWriter / InvoicePositionSheetWriter / InvoiceJournalSheetWriter

Domain-specific sheet content — header, positions, and change journal — each recomputing its own control/Δ columns independently.

InvoiceExportReadRepository

The dedicated read side for the export, a scalar-tuple projection rather than the aggregate. See Persistence.

InvoiceWorkbookExportPort / InvoiceWorkbookExportService

The application-layer port — one invoice with its change journal, or a filtered set without — and its POI-aware implementation.

5.8. Key Interfaces
