6. Runtime View

The scenarios below are the load-bearing runtime paths of the system, each presented as one sequence diagram per use case, kept at container level rather than at class level. The concrete classes, NATS subjects, and infrastructure-level guarantees behind each queue hop are covered once in Messaging; this chapter cross-references that section instead of repeating it. See Building Block View for the containers and components involved.

6.1. Save Invoice Header and B2B Values

Figure 6. Sequence: Save Invoice Header

The standard and guided entry views use the same bound fields and submit one UpdateInvoiceHeaderCommand. InvoiceHeaderUpdateService.updateHeader verifies status and version, applies the invoice-owned note snapshot and its provenance, merges previously unmapped B2B header values into editable free text once, and evaluates a submitted tax decision. Invoice fields and B2B overrides commit in the same PostgreSQL transaction. A failed validation or write rolls back the entire command; the UI retains its input and shows navigable field errors. The EditInvoiceHeaderUseCase implementation reloads the DTO after the transactional call returns, before the UI reports success. PDF/XML read the saved note, not a live provider note.

Invoice unit labels use the unit catalogue’s explicit translation keys rather than derived enum names. German is the default and English is selectable. Persistence and XML keep their ordinal/UN-ECE contracts; user-facing labels and validation arguments are localized at the presentation boundary. Category wording remains a separate industry-profile concern.

6.2. Generate E-Invoice

Generating a PDF/A-3b or ZUGFeRD/XRechnung XML document is asynchronous: the Office Manager requests generation via the UI, GenerationJobCreationService.createJob() validates invoice state (VOIDED rejected, except for a REPRODUCTION job, which the document recovery requests only after the stored original, an identical copy in the store and the mail journal all came up empty; it renders the frozen state again and changes no invoice data), stores a GenerationJob in PENDING, and returns immediately.

Figure 7. Sequence: Generate E-Invoice

The job is dispatched via NATS JetStream to kotlin-xpdf, which renders documents via Typst and Mustang Project (embedded KoSIT validator) and stores them in RustFS. The result queue notifies little-trotter-invoice: the job is marked terminal (COMPLETED/FAILED) and UI status chips update via Vaadin server push (with a fallback poll tick).

Job lanes: every GenerationJob carries a lane (generation_job.job_lane, INTERACTIVE or BULK) that createJob receives as a required parameter and that never changes afterwards. Interactive: a user waits in the UI (send, print, credit note issued from the UI). Bulk: import, regeneration, reproduction, scheduled and admin sweeps, quote documents.

Aspect Bulk lane Interactive lane

Subject

document-jobs

document-jobs-interactive

Durable consumer (kotlin-xpdf)

kotlin-xpdf-group

kotlin-xpdf-interactive

Worker threads

JOB_WORKER_THREADS

JOB_INTERACTIVE_WORKER_THREADS (default 1)

Dispatch bulkhead

invoice.generation.bulkhead.max-concurrent-calls

invoice.generation.interactive-bulkhead.max-concurrent-calls (default 4)

Fast-path publish

shared managed executor

dedicated small executor

Both subjects live in the stream LITTLE_TROTTER_JOBS; results use the shared subject document-job-results. The pending-job poller orders interactive jobs before bulk jobs, then by creation time. Recovery and republish use the stored lane.

Figure 8. State Machine: Generation Job Lifecycle & Recovery

Robustness & Recovery Principles:

  • Lease Heartbeat: kotlin-xpdf’s pull consumer leases each job (`ackWaitSeconds = 120s) and sends periodic heartbeats (ackWaitSeconds / 3).

  • Durable Recovery Scan: The worker scans hourly (invoice.jobs.recovery-interval) and on startup. It resets stale DELEGATED jobs (staleCutoffMinutes = 2) back to PENDING only if their message actually vanished from the stream, burning one attempt.

  • Dispatch Gate: DocumentJobDispatchGate applies a @Bulkhead (invoice.generation.bulkhead.max-concurrent-calls) to bound concurrent bulk dispatches against memory limits. Interactive dispatches use a separate bulkhead (invoice.generation.interactive-bulkhead.max-concurrent-calls, default 4), so concurrent bulk dispatches cannot reject them. A durable database state ensures resilience without in-process retry.

6.3. Send an Invoice

Reaching SENT is never a direct, synchronous status change — it is always the result of a successfully completed, SEND-triggered GenerationJob. "Send" only requests that job (InvoiceSendService.requestSend, itself a thin wrapper over GenerationJobCreationService.createJob with trigger = SEND) and returns immediately; the invoice stays IN_REVIEW while the job is in flight (visible via the job’s own GenerationJobStatus, not a new invoice status — see Invoice Lifecycle State Machine).

Figure 9. Sequence: Send an Invoice

The job travels the same queue pipeline as Generate E-Invoice above. What differs is what happens on result delivery: GenerationJobResultConsumer calls GenerationJobResultHandler.resolveSendOutcome as a plain, synchronous, sequential continuation right after its own markCompleted/markFailed call — the arrival of the worker’s result is the trigger, so no polling and no extra @Scheduled bean exist anywhere in this path (see Green IT Principles). resolveSendOutcome reads the job’s already-merged KoSIT report directly; only a VALID outcome calls InvoiceLifecycleService.transition(id, SENT) — still the single place that stamps the invoice date, freezes the payment-term snapshot, and runs the Finalization Gate. A FAILED job or a COMPLETED job that resolves INVALID/UNAVAILABLE leaves the invoice untouched and records a message-center entry instead; a retry always creates a fresh SEND job.

The one exception stays synchronous and manual: an Office Manager can override a VALIDATION_UNAVAILABLE outcome with an audited reason, calling InvoiceLifecycleService.transition(id, SENT, overrideReason) directly — the Finalization Gate escalation, reached from its own explicit UI action rather than the "Send" button. INVALID/PENDING/NOT_VALIDATED remain non-overridable.

The SEND-triggered job always becomes the invoice’s issued document (GenerationJob.issued = true, set regardless of the invoice’s IN_REVIEW status at the moment the job is created — a deliberate, documented exception to the rule every other job creation follows) and is protected by the same single-generation rule as any other issued job once it completes.

6.4. Open the Invoice E-Mail Editor

InvoiceMailDispatchService.compose runs without a database transaction. It calls InvoiceMailComposeTransactions.prepare, a short @Transactional read of the invoice, its latest issued document and the presigned attachment URLs. The synchronous HTTP handover to the mail component (POST /mail/api/compose) follows with no open database transaction; the UI then navigates to mail/compose/{draftId}. Opening the editor does not use NATS — the streams LITTLE_TROTTER_MAIL and LITTLE_TROTTER_MAIL_RESULT carry only sending and results.

The result is MailComposeResult, either Ready(handover) or DocumentPending:

  • DocumentPending: an issued, non-failed generation job exists but has no completed, checksummed document yet. The send action creates its job in the interactive lane, so the document is normally ready within the QS-14 limit even while bulk jobs are queued (Generate E-Invoice).

  • No issued job, or only failed jobs: MailDispatchException.

  • Reproduced documents stay blocked (ReproductionNotSendableException).

InvoiceMailReadinessQueryPort.readiness(invoiceId) (implemented by InvoiceMailReadinessService) returns NOT_REQUESTED, PENDING or READY. While PENDING, the invoice send button is disabled with the hint "Dokument wird erzeugt …​". The job-finished push (GenerationJobUiPushHandler to UiEventBus.JobFinishedEvent to the InvoiceDetailView refresh) and the 5 s poll re-evaluate readiness and retry a pending compose, so the editor opens once the document exists. The quote send path (QuoteDispatchHandlers in kotlin-quote) follows the same pattern: read-only prepare, non-transactional compose, DocumentPending.

6.5. E-Mail Dispatch and Signed Archive Package (tar.gz)

After an invoice is finalized and locked (SENT), the Office Manager triggers the external customer dispatch via "Rechnung per E-Mail senden" in kotlin-invoice. The mail dispatch is handled by the headless Self-Contained System kotlin-email (ADR-015):

Figure 10. Sequence: Send E-Invoice via Mail and Package Archive

  1. Composition: ComposeResource renders the personalized mail draft using tenant master data, evaluated placeholders and the finalized invoice document (PDF/A-3 and embedded XML).

  2. Enqueue & Handover: SendResource / SendMailUseCase stores the request in mail.mail_journal (status = QUEUED) and publishes a dispatch order to NATS JetStream (mail.send.order).

  3. SMTP Delivery: MailSendConsumerService picks up the job from JetStream, transmits the MIME message (.eml) via authenticated TLS to the corporate SMTP server and updates the journal to SENT.

  4. Operator Archive Handover: When configured, an identical journal copy of the outbound email is simultaneously dispatched to the operator’s archive address (ADR-037).

  5. Deterministic Signed Archive (tar.gz): Once SENT commits, MailArchivePackageService runs the deterministic packaging (BuildMailArchivePackageUseCase):

    • Collects <journalId>.eml and <journalId>-authored.json into original/ alongside their .sha256 checksums.

    • Generates detached Ed25519 signatures in signed/ (<journalId>.eml.sig, <journalId>-authored.json.sig, manifest.json.sig).

    • Writes normalized manifest.json (timestamps, recipient, messageId, signer fingerprint).

    • Compresses deterministically into <journalId>.tar.gz (zeroed mtimes, sorted entries) and stores it in RustFS.

    • Marks mail_journal.archive_status = ARCHIVED via append-only write guard. Any packaging failure isolates to archive_status = FAILED and triggers an alarm without invalidating the already-delivered customer mail.

6.6. Upload Receipt

Receipts are uploaded once and de-duplicated by content before they can be linked to an invoice.

Figure 11. Sequence: Upload Receipt

ReceiptPoolView accepts a PDF upload (up to ReceiptSizePolicy.MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE, 10 MiB, magic-byte checked) and hands the bytes to ReceiptUploadService.upload(). A file above ReceiptSizePolicy.TARGET_RECEIPT_SIZE (768 KiB) is reduced towards it by ReceiptPdfReducer (Receipt normalisation at upload) unless the user explicitly declines reduction, in which case the smaller MAX_UNREDUCED_RECEIPT_SIZE (1 MiB) cap applies instead. A SHA-256 checksum on the stored (post-reduction) bytes drives duplicate detection: the cache is checked first as a fast path, then ReceiptRepository.findByChecksum() is the authoritative check regardless of the cache result. Only a genuinely new receipt is written to the object store and persisted; the cache entry is then marked known, without a TTL, since the database’s unique constraint on the checksum — not the cache — is the real guard against duplicates, so an evicted entry costs at most one extra query. Linking a receipt to a line item is a separate step, covered next.

6.7. Assign Receipt to a Line Item

Figure 12. Sequence: Assign Receipt to a Line Item

Assignment (setReceiptsForItem/addReceiptsToItem) links an already-uploaded receipt to an InvoiceItem — an n:m relationship, since one receipt commonly covers several items or the invoice as a whole. The service checks the invoice status and the supplied version before assignment changes, and enforces the invoice-wide receipt count of 19 distinct receipts; an estimated document size above 20 MiB warns instead of refusing the link — see Document Size Budget. Removing a link leaves its pool document intact. The common dialog refreshes both scope tables after the write commits; empty scope tables remain visible. Pool deletion is a separate operation guarded by reference usage.

6.8. Print Job Overview

PrintJobListView is a read-only, cross-invoice monitoring view over all GenerationJob records.

Figure 13. Sequence: Print Job Overview

Opening the view loads a two-level tree grid (day → jobs) using projection DTOs (PrintJobOverviewRow) via PrintJobOverviewQueryService. The new-job count badge is updated via dual channels:

  • Fast Path: Local UiEventBus (JobFinishedEvent) within the same JVM.

  • Guaranteed Path: Polling interval (≤ 2 s) active while jobs in the current filter remain non-terminal, ensuring live updates when workers run in separate processes. Grid rows do not reload on poll ticks to preserve operator scroll/expansion state.

6.9. Download Rendered PDF/XML

Figure 14. Sequence: Download Rendered PDF/XML

Neither the download nor the validation-report dialog does any work until the Office Manager clicks. DocumentStore.get() fetches bytes from the object store lazily, keyed by the pdfObjectKey/xmlObjectKey already visible in the overview row. Opening the validation-report dialog is the one place this scenario touches the database again, loading the full GenerationJob entity only at that point.

6.10. Verify an Invoice Recipient’s VAT ID

An invoice recipient’s VAT ID is one text field (VatIdField); VatIdValidator recognises its two-character country prefix, auto-completes it from the billing address when the Office Manager types digits only, and validates the format on blur. Asking the EU VIES REST API whether the id is actually registered is a second, separate step: a small "VIES prüfen" button, enabled only for a format-valid EU prefix, that the Office Manager clicks explicitly — see Error Handling and Validation for the field’s validation layer and ADR-023 for why the request is never automatic.

Figure 15. Sequence: Verify an Invoice Recipient’s VAT ID

VatIdValidator.prefixOf/numberOf split the normalised id before the call, never the address country — the number carries the country the id was actually issued for, which need not match where the invoice recipient is registered. ViesHttpClient reads VIES’s isValid together with its userError, name and address fields and resolves to one of three outcomes: VERIFIED and FAILED are recorded on the still-open dialog (RecipientView.pendingVerification) — shown immediately as the verification checkbox, badge and helper text, VERIFIED additionally showing the trader name/address VIES returned and prefilling the verification note with them if it is still blank — and only reach Recipient.vatIdVerificationStatus/vatIdVerifiedAt in PostgreSQL once the Office Manager saves the invoice recipient; cancelling the dialog discards the outcome. UNREACHABLE names the reason (timeout, VIES busy, VIES down, or the request itself was rejected) and never changes that dialog state at all — an unreachable VIES is not a statement about the VAT ID. This is the only place the system calls VIES: the invoice issuer profile and additional invoice issuer VAT registrations are format-validated the same way but never verified against VIES, and no scheduler or background job ever triggers a VIES call.

6.11. Quote Invoice Order to Invoice Draft

Recording the acceptance of a SENT quote only sets the status to ACCEPTED and writes a log entry; it sends nothing to the invoicing application. The hand-over is a separate, addressable invoice order that the user triggers with "Rechnungsentwurf erstellen" from SENT or ACCEPTED (with the permissions to decide on quotes and to create invoices). In one short transaction the quote module records the acceptance if the quote is still SENT, stores the order as REQUESTED in quote.quote_invoice_order, writes a log entry and an outbox row for quote.invoice-order.requested; the outbox relay publishes after the commit (and once at startup for rows a crash left behind) with the order id as JetStream message id and marks a row published only after the JetStream acknowledgement. A quote has at most one order in REQUESTED or CREATED. The event carries references only (order id, quote id and number, acceptance date, order reference, optional delivery date or service period, actor), because the content is immutable from REVIEW on. The invoicing application’s QuoteInvoiceOrderConsumer reads the offer snapshot, the PDF and the customer attachments through the quote contract REST and CreateInvoiceFromQuoteUseCase creates the invoice draft with its number drawn at creation, delivery date or service period set from the order. The quote number becomes the order reference (BT-14), the acceptance date its date; creation is idempotent through the quote id on the invoice, and a repeated order for an existing invoice is answered with that invoice and the new order id. The reply invoice.created-from-quote (order id, invoice id and number) marks the order CREATED and moves the quote from ACCEPTED to INVOICED; if the draft cannot be created, invoice.creation-from-quote.rejected marks the order REJECTED with the reason and leaves the quote ACCEPTED, and the user orders again, which creates a new order. Replies for unknown or already answered orders are acknowledged and logged without effect. While NATS is unavailable the order stays REQUESTED and the relay delivers it later. After five failed deliveries the message goes to the dead-letter subject and appears in the message center.

When the quote module starts without ever having received master data, it requests a snapshot (masterdata.snapshot.requested) and the invoicing application streams its reference data back in chunks; afterwards the replicas keep the module usable while the invoicing application is down.

6.12. Offer Statistics

A user with the permission QUOTE_VIEW opens the offer statistics and picks a period. QuoteStatisticsView asks its narrow input port; the HTTP adapter calls GET /quote/api/statistics/dashboard of the quote application (the ranking view uses /statistics/ranking). QuoteStatisticsResource delegates to QuoteStatisticsDashboardQueryPort; the read model selects the per-offer facts of the period (offers in SENT, ACCEPTED, INVOICED or REJECTED by quote_date, plus the open ones for "in progress") with the net amount per line rounded to cents, and QuoteKpis and QuoteStatusCounts compute the figures. The anti-corruption layer maps QuoteDashboardView to the presentation model, QuoteStatisticsPresenter formats it and the shared statistics components render it. The call is read-only and starts no transaction that writes; if the quote application is down, the view shows an error state instead of figures.

7. Deployment View

The target environment is called the server throughout this document. The primary target is an Ubuntu server; a dedicated single-instance host (a mini PC or any other standalone machine serving one operator) is the second supported shape, covered in Dedicated Single-Instance Host below. This repository’s own docker-compose.yml is the minimal product stack — the application, the renderer/worker, and the four infrastructure services it needs (PostgreSQL, RustFS, NATS, Valkey), no TLS proxy, no monitoring, no hardening overlays. The full production-style deployment described in this chapter — native Postgres/RustFS/Caddy, the instance overlay, backups, and monitoring — is assembled by the sibling, private little-trotter-admin-tools repository, which layers its own Compose overlays and native-install scripts on top of this repository’s base file rather than duplicating it. Every path below prefixed docker/compose/ or admin-tools/ therefore refers to that sibling repository, checked out alongside this one.

Productive use is a server installation by an IT service provider, following the Ubuntu server deployment described below. This repository’s make dev compose stack is meant for evaluation and development only.

7.1. Server Deployment (Production-Style Target)

Figure 16. Server deployment

On the Ubuntu server, PostgreSQL 18, RustFS, and Caddy run natively under systemd — one installation of each, shared by every instance on the box — instead of as Docker containers. Only the application layer (little-trotter-invoice, little-trotter-job-worker, kotlin-xpdf, NATS JetStream, Valkey) is Docker Compose, running as one compose project per instance (little-trotter-prod/little-trotter-dev/little-trotter-demo), each with its own Docker subnet and its own database, bucket, and role-trio on the shared native services. Caddy is the stack’s only externally exposed edge: one shared installation generates one HTTPS vhost per instance (ports 443/80), reverse-proxying to that instance’s own little-trotter-invoice container on a loopback-only port — every other container, native or Docker, stays unreachable from outside the server. Authentik and the monitoring stack (Prometheus/Grafana) are shared Docker services outside every instance project: Authentik is the identity provider for the Office Manager login of every instance (little-trotter-invoice runs as a Quarkus OIDC web-app client against it, the application itself decides who may do what) and additionally gates the separate admin site; monitoring scrapes every instance’s metrics endpoint.

Instance ports follow one offset scheme (prod +0, dev +10000, demo +20000) — for example the application’s loopback port is 8080/18080/28080 and NATS' client port is 4222/14222/24222 — while the two shared native services keep one fixed port each, independent of instance count: PostgreSQL 5432 and RustFS 9010.

Service Runtime Network / Port(s) Notes

caddy

Native (systemd), shared

all interfaces, 443/80 per instance vhost (only ports reachable beyond 127.0.0.1)

One installation for the whole server; install-caddy-ubuntu.sh regenerates one vhost per configured instance.

postgres

Native (systemd), shared

127.0.0.1:5432 (one cluster)

One database + role-trio (lt_migrator_<instance>/lt_app_<instance>) per instance; adopted or freshly created by install-postgres-ubuntu.sh.

rustfs

Native (systemd), shared

127.0.0.1:9010 (S3 API)

One bucket per instance (invoice-documents[-dev|-demo]); adopted or freshly created by install-rustfs-ubuntu.sh.

little-trotter-invoice

Docker, per instance

127.0.0.1:8080/18080/28080 (Caddy’s proxy target only)

1200M memory limit, pids: 512; reaches native Postgres/RustFS via host.docker.internal.

little-trotter-job-worker

Docker, per instance

none (no UI, no externally reachable port)

432M memory limit, pids: 512.

kotlin-xpdf

Docker, per instance

none (internal REST only, never fronted by Caddy)

768M memory limit, pids: 512.

nats

Docker, per instance

127.0.0.1:4222/14222/24222 (client), 127.0.0.1:8222/18222/28222 (monitoring)

128M memory limit; one JetStream broker per instance, never shared.

valkey

Docker, per instance

none published

192M memory limit; deliberately the one Docker-native infrastructure service — a disposable cache, not shared state.

Authentik

Docker, shared (adopted or own containers)

reached through Caddy; admin site on :8444

OIDC identity provider for the Office Manager login of every instance (Quarkus OIDC web-app client); additionally the forward-auth gate of the admin site (Prometheus).

Monitoring

Docker, shared (adopted or own containers)

127.0.0.1:9090/3000-class, SSH tunnel only

Prometheus + Grafana (admin-tools/monitoring/), scrapes every instance.

`kotlin-xpdf’s image renders with the pinned Typst CLI only (ADR-030); there is no second layout engine, no engine switch and no optional build profile for one.

PostgreSQL and RustFS keep their data directly on the server’s own filesystem, outside Docker’s storage driver (systemd-managed data directories, not named volumes) — one reason they are native: independent backup, resize, and migration. The Docker-side volumes that remain are per instance and named per compose project, so three instances never collide. little-trotter-invoice’s own `backups-data:/backups volume is where DevOps’s cron-triggered backup run (see Operations and Admin Tooling below) writes its archives before backup.sh copies them off, syncing an age-encrypted copy to an optional external SSD for prod.

Backups serve recovery, not archiving; retention is covered by the operator’s archive (ADR-035). Disk encryption at rest (for example LUKS) of the host is the operator’s responsibility; the age encryption above applies to backup copies only.

Reaching the server: sudo lt-setup build prod|dev|demo resolves that instance’s ports, installs/adopts native Postgres, RustFS, and Caddy, adopts monitoring and Authentik if present, and deploys the instance’s Docker Compose project (QUARKUS_PROFILE=prod, scheduler enabled on both Quarkus services).

7.1.1. Dedicated Single-Instance Host (Mini PC)

The second deployment shape is one machine for one deployment: a mini PC that serves a single operator. It runs this repository’s docker-compose.yml as it is — no instance model, no shared native services — so every service is a container, PostgreSQL, RustFS and Caddy included. Caddy sits on proxy-net and is the only service with published ports; everything else lives on invoice-net, reachable from the host on 127.0.0.1 at most.

Service Reachable at Memory limit

caddy

80, 443 (the only ports open beyond localhost)

128M

little-trotter-invoice

via Caddy only

1200M

little-trotter-job-worker

not reachable (no UI)

432M

kotlin-xpdf

internal only

768M

postgres

127.0.0.1:5432

768M

rustfs

127.0.0.1:9000 (S3), 127.0.0.1:9001 (console)

400M

nats

127.0.0.1:4222, 127.0.0.1:8222

128M

valkey

not published

192M

Every container carries a memory limit (about 3.9 GB in total) and the application tier a pids limit; a tighter overlay for smaller machines brings the total down to about 2.6 GB. Database and object-store data live on bind mounts under /srv/little-trotter, so they survive a full container and image cleanup. The stack is deployed with make -C admin-tools deploy, which picks the hardware overlay automatically and can pull published images instead of building them.

The memory limit above is a hard ceiling: little-trotter-invoice and little-trotter-job-worker both run with -XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError, so a burst of concurrent PDF/XLS/report generation work that exceeds it kills the whole process rather than degrading. The invoice.generation.bulkhead.max-concurrent-calls knob (INVOICE_GENERATION_BULKHEAD_MAX_CONCURRENT_CALLS / _WORKER, see docker-compose.yml) exists to keep worst-case concurrent generation work under whichever memory limit a given overlay configures; a rejected call fails cleanly into the existing durable PENDING job record (Generate E-Invoice) rather than dropping work or risking the OOM kill. Interactive document jobs pass a separate bulkhead, invoice.generation.interactive-bulkhead.max-concurrent-calls (INVOICE_GENERATION_INTERACTIVE_BULKHEAD_MAX_CONCURRENT_CALLS, default 4), so a full bulk bulkhead never rejects them; its memory share counts on top of the bulk limit. On the kotlin-xpdf side, JOB_INTERACTIVE_WORKER_THREADS (default 1) adds the interactive render threads, which the derived bulk thread count already subtracts from the heap budget. `docker-compose.yml’s own comment on that variable carries the sizing arithmetic for this repository’s default limits; a hardware overlay that lowers the memory limit further (as this section’s mini-PC table does) must lower this knob to match, which is out of this repository’s scope to prescribe for a sibling repository’s overlay.

7.2. Optional Mail Dispatch Container

The mail dispatch component (kotlin-email, see Building Block View) sends invoices by email. It is a separately built and deployed container so this bounded context — templates, provider SMTP, an append-only send journal, signed archive packages — has its own release cycle and failure domain, and so an instance can run with or without it: mail dispatch is never on the invoicing application’s critical path.

It plugs into the stack the same way the optional Factur-X engine overlay (standby, kept but not developed further) does — an email Compose profile in admin-tools, switched on per instance by one flag (LT_MAIL_ENABLED; in development make dev starts it by default, EMAIL=0 opts out) — with no port published to the internet; Caddy answers 404 for /mail/* from outside, and only the invoicing application calls the component’s REST API. Composing a mail is one HTTPS request into the component, attachments handed over as presigned links rather than a shared bucket; the outcome comes back asynchronously on the NATS subject mail.send.result, projected onto two nullable columns on the invoice so the send history stays visible even with the component stopped. The design decisions (own bounded context, published contract package, forwarded identity headers) are recorded in ADR-015, ADR-016 and ADR-019; this section covers only how the component is deployed. An instance that leaves the flag off carries none of it — no container, no schema, no bucket, no streams, no secret. Every test stack (isolated Playwright stack, stress, admin-test, dev) and the cross-module E2E suite run the real component with a Mailpit sink, never a stub; a stub HTTP server remains only in integration tests that deliberately isolate the invoicing side’s HTTP adapter (retry/timeout, malformed answers).

Switch Effect

LT_MAIL_ENABLED (instance), EMAIL (dev, on by default)

Starts email-app plus its schema, bucket and NATS streams; the invoicing image itself is unchanged either way.

Port

LT_MAIL_PORT (8090 / 18090 / 28090), 127.0.0.1 only — called by the invoicing application over the instance network; no port reaches the network.

Provisioning

Switching the flag on provisions everything the component needs on the host automatically: the mail database schema with the mail_app/mail_migrator roles, the mail-journal object-store bucket with its access key, the archive-signing key pair and the integration bearer token; the component creates its own JetStream streams at start. The only human input is the instance name; no value is typed or pasted.

Transport (MAIL_TRANSPORT)

sink (dev/demo only) points SMTP at a loopback Mailpit sink so no mail leaves the host; provider (prod) uses the SMTP provider credentials from the server-local sealed file /etc/little-trotter/mail-provider.properties.age, decrypted only in the deploy process’s environment at start time — the plaintext never exists as a file, in the instance env, in a log or in a repository. A prod instance without a sealed provider file refuses to enable mail (exit 2, env untouched).

Archive

The mail secrets of an instance (mail env keys, archive-signing key pair, and the still-sealed provider file with its key) can be exported as one age-encrypted archive under the instance backup directory and restored into a fresh env without overwriting existing keys; the archive never contains a plaintext provider password.
Note

The integration is opt-in. New instances keep LT_MAIL_ENABLED=false; enabling it on a production instance requires the sealed provider file to exist on the host first, so mail is switched on in one step once the SMTP provider and its audit requirements are settled.

7.3. Optional Quote Container

The offer module (kotlin-quote, see Building Block View) is deployed like the mail component: a separate quote-app container behind the Compose profile quote (overlay docker/compose/quote.yml in admin-tools, switched on per instance by LT_QUOTE_ENABLED; make dev QUOTE=1 in development), reached by the invoicing application over the instance network. It reuses the instance’s PostgreSQL and RustFS but is isolated by name: schema quote in the existing database with its own role pair (quote_migrator, quote_app), own bucket quote-documents, own NATS streams. No port is published; Caddy answers 404 for /quote/api/ and /quote/q/ from outside. The invoicing application gates menu, publisher and consumer with quote.enabled (QUOTE_ENABLED, off by default); an instance that leaves it off carries no container, schema, bucket or stream of the module. Offer PDFs are produced by the existing renderer through the document-job subject; offers are sent through the mail component, so a quote instance that sends offers also runs the mail component.

7.4. Database Baselines: What Runs When

Two independent Flyway baselines exist, one per component, in the same PostgreSQL database but in separate schemas with separate history tables. Neither is an upgrade path: while no live instance exists, both are edited in place (greenfield baseline, no V3+ scripts, no backfills).

Baseline Runs

Invoice ddl/V1baseline_schema.sql + seed/V2baseline_seed.sql (kotlin-invoice)

At every start of the invoicing application (Flyway, lt_migrator). Independent of MAIL_ENABLED; the invoicing application never references the mail schema and talks to the component only over HTTP and NATS.

Mail V1mail_baseline_schema.sql + V2mail_baseline_seed.sql (kotlin-email/email-service)

Only by email-app, and only when the component runs (MAIL_ENABLED=true / LT_MAIL_ENABLED), as mail_migrator into schema mail (history table flyway_schema_history_mail). With the flag off the schema is never created.

Roles mail_migrator / mail_app (db/roles/mail_roles.sql, not a Flyway migration)

Once, against the instance’s existing database, before the first mail migrate (role bootstrap of the deployment). The mail baseline then grants and revokes against these existing roles.

Verification: a fresh isolated PostgreSQL starts the invoicing application with the invoice baseline alone (FeatureModulesAlwaysActiveIT); with the flag off no NATS result consumer is bound and the start-up log carries no mail-related warning.

7.5. Operations and Admin Tooling (little-trotter-admin-tools)

Server operations, instance lifecycle management, and disaster recovery are strictly separated from application code and orchestrated by the sibling repository little-trotter-admin-tools (currently maintained as an internal operator suite by the project initiator; public release planned for Q4/2027 or Q1/2028 after multi-year operational maturity validation; operators in the interim reduce operational effort through reference hardware and administrative scripts).

Figure 17. Architecture: Operator Plane & Target Deployment

7.5.1. Staged Operations Rollout: Lean MVP Baseline vs. Phase-2 Management Plane

The operational lifecycle follows a lean, two-stage rollout model to eliminate operational waste (Muda) and ensure compliance-grade robustness:

  • Phase 1 (Operational MVP / Current Baseline until Q4/2027):

    • Lean Reference Hardware: Standardized fanless Mini-PC paired with local mirrored NAS, eliminating platform-specific driver, thermal, and configuration anomalies.

    • Lightweight Shell Automation: Dedicated shell and CLI scripts maintained by the project initiator handle core operational tasks (age-encrypted backups, atomic PostgreSQL snapshots, container rollouts, and systemd watchdog verifications).

    • Operator Baseline: Achieves an empirically validated run-effort of 2–3 person-days per year (0.75–1.5 person-days initial ramp-up).

  • Phase 2 (Consolidated Management Plane / Release Q4/2027–Q1/2028):

    • General Availability of little-trotter-admin: Packaged Go CLI suite (lt) and interactive TUI (lhorse).

    • Phase-Gate Quality Criteria: Transition to Phase 2 requires proven completion of an operational hardening cycle under real-world production conditions:

      • Non-disruptive PostgreSQL schema evolution across major version migrations.

      • Validated automated disaster recovery rehearsals with Mean Time to Recovery (MTTR) < 15 minutes.

      • Long-term zero-maintenance operation of certificate renewal, Authentik forward-auth, and storage reclamation under sustained production accounting loads.

7.5.2. Unified Operational Interfaces: lt CLI & lhorse TUI

Day-2 operations run either headless/scripted via the lt CLI suite or interactively via the lhorse Commander TUI (number-row driven navigation, live multi-instance status, container logs).

Operational Domain Core Commands (lt) Responsibility & Infrastructure Impact

Provisioning & Setup

lt setup, lt check

Guided server installation, dependency verification (systemd, Docker, mDNS, TLS, firewall).

Instance Lifecycle

lt build [instance|all], lt instance [start|stop|restart|teardown]

Deploys multi-environment stacks (prod +0, dev +10000, demo +20000) with shared native Postgres/RustFS/Caddy.

Orchestrated Upgrade

lt upgrade --source=ghcr

5-step orchestrated update: preflight check → occasion backup → container rollout → DB migration → health check. The container rollout recreates containers whose image changed, a brief interruption on this single-node deployment, not a zero-downtime swap.

Disaster Recovery

lt backup [create|verify|sync|restore]

Atomic backup of Postgres (pg_dump), RustFS (object sync), and Caddy TLS state. Age-encrypted off-site/SSD synchronization.

User & Access Management

lt user [invite|add|remove|reset-password]

Direct Authentik OIDC tenant administration, user enrollment invitations, and role assignments (user, admin, customer-admin).

Observability & Diagnostics

lt metrics, lt logs, lt tunnel

Aggregated JVM/DB/Queue metrics, real-time log streaming, and secure SSH tunnels for non-public administrative ports.

7.5.3. Manual Database Writes

The database never invents a time (ADR-029): the invoice audit trigger stamps occurred_at from the session setting app.now and rejects a write without it. The application sets app.now itself; every operator write outside it — a psql session, an ad-hoc script, a bulk loader — that inserts, updates or deletes rows of invoice, invoice_item, invoice_document, trip_expense or the receipt assignments sets it first, in the same transaction, in UTC:

BEGIN;
SELECT set_config('app.now', '2026-09-27T08:00:00Z', true);
-- the corrective statements
COMMIT;

Without it the write fails with app.now is not set: a write to <table> must carry the application time. Columns that carry the application time (invoice.invoice_date, the created_at of snapshots, jobs, validation data and maintenance events, maintenance_queue_execution.claimed_at) have no database-clock default; a manual INSERT supplies them explicitly.

Not affected: read-only psql access, and lt backup restore — a full pg_restore --clean loads the data before it recreates the triggers. The datagen loader sets app.now on every connection it opens.

7.6. Development and Test Deployment Variants

The base docker-compose.yml is the single source of truth; dev and test only add or swap overlays, never a parallel stack definition.

Variant Command App Port

Local dev stack

make dev (adds debug and veraPDF overlays; FACTURX=1 adds the optional Factur-X validation engine, standby: kept, not developed further)

https://localhost

Isolated Playwright/E2E stack (sibling repository)

make playwright in little-trotter-admin-tools — its own Compose project and network, running alongside the dev stack without disturbing it

https://localhost:9443; STACK=dev targets the running dev stack instead

CI-local

This repository’s Maven reactor gate (make test); the sibling repository’s own QA suite (make qa) covers Playwright, monkey, and this chapter’s docs build

n/a

The isolated Playwright stack keeps a full browser E2E run from competing with whatever a developer already has running under make dev.

7.7. Verification on the Test Host

At the end of a wave, the result is verified on the test host, not on the developer machine. The host runs its own Docker Compose stacks and never touches existing instance data.

  1. Transfer: the commits are sent as a git bundle and checked out into a dedicated, detached worktree on the host.

  2. Isolation: every run uses its own Compose project name (for the Playwright stack E2E_PROJECT=<name>), its own network, and a fresh database. Test data is limited to the synthetic datagen data.

  3. Order: Playwright suite, then the dependency scan (see Dependency Vulnerability Scanning), then make test.

  4. Cleanup: docker compose -p <name> down -v, then remove the worktree.

Long jobs run in a login shell (bash -lc '…​'). User-installed linters such as ruff live in ~/.local/bin, which a non-login SSH shell does not have on PATH; without it the config lint gate fails.

7.7.1. Playwright Harness Behavior

  • ERR_NETWORK_CHANGED: Chromium reports this on page loads when Testcontainers creates or removes Docker networks on the same host in parallel. The request never reaches the server. The harness reloads once and reports the application as offline only if the error persists. Integration test suites therefore do not run in parallel with Playwright.

  • Grid cells: Vaadin renders grid cell content in the light DOM inside vaadin-grid-cell-content, outside the [role=row] element. Status symbols are span[role=img] elements with an aria-label. Locators target the cell content, not row descendants.

  • Notifications: an open error notification is not replaced by a later success notification (one toast slot per UI). A test dismisses the error before asserting a following success toast.

8. Cross-Cutting Concepts

This chapter covers the domain concepts, design patterns, and architectural rules that span multiple modules: money and tax arithmetic, EN 16931 compliance semantics, messaging conventions, and the invoice state machine that governs the lifecycle.

8.1. Money and Tax Domain Model

All monetary arithmetic is strictly encapsulated by the Money value object (kotlin-shared). Raw BigDecimal is reserved exclusively for non-monetary quantities, percentages, and weighting factors.

Figure 18. Architecture Pipeline: Money Precision and Storage

8.1.1. Two Precision Worlds

Dimension Money.of(…​) (Normalized) Money.exact(…​) (Accumulation)

Scale and Rounding

Fixed 2 decimals (HALF_UP)

Caller-defined scale (typically 4) without intermediate rounding

Application

Quoted line amounts, totals, UI display, receipts

Intermediate tax proportion breakdowns across multiple line items

Conservation

Standard arithmetic (+, -, *, /)

allocate(): splits amounts via largest-remainder distribution without rounding loss

8.1.2. Key Invariants and Enforcement

  • Value Semantics: Equality compares amount via compareTo (1.50 == 1.5). hashCode strips trailing zeros to preserve the contract across scales. toString() renders two fixed decimals (1.50).

  • Compile and Architecture Gate: MoneyArchitectureTest (ArchUnit in kotlin-shared, kotlin-invoice, kotlin-xpdf) forbids raw BigDecimal for any property/method matching amount|price|total|sum.

  • Persistence Guarantee: MoneyConverter (AttributeConverter<Money, BigDecimal>) normalizes all values to scale 2 on write. PostgreSQL maps amounts to exact NUMERIC(14,2) base-10 storage (zero IEEE-754 binary floating-point drift).

  • Tax Consistency: VAT is modeled per invoice line (InvoiceItem.taxRule). The cross-border Invoice.reverseCharge toggle cascades rules to all lines and is enforced as a blocking gate at invoice finalization (ADR-003).

8.1.3. Country Reference Tax Rates

TaxRate.isReference identifies the standard reference rate of a country independently of the global isDefault UI preference. A partial unique database index permits at most one marked rate per country; a CHECK constraint requires an active, selectable, positive category-S rule that is not a hotel rate. The application rejects conflicting assignments before persistence. Switching a reference requires clearing the previous marker first.

TaxRateRepository.requireReferenceTaxRate(country, date) performs an unpaged, uncached lookup and checks inclusive validity boundaries. Missing or ambiguous references are errors, never a reason to select another rule by list order. The greenfield seed marks DE 19%, FR 20%, BE 21%, NL 21%, and LU 17% from the existing standard rules.

selectTripTaxRate is shared by trip persistence and the Vaadin preview. A fixed-price trip uses the reference for the invoice’s seller VAT registration and invoice date; the domestic registration represented by null means DE. Reverse charge takes priority and retains main-service inheritance, as do trips without a fixed price. Header saves refresh fixed-price trip taxes after country/date changes; switching reverse charge off restores references. Existing issued invoice snapshots are not rewritten by reference data edits. This implements the reference tax rate working scope.

8.2. Document Size Budget

A produced e-invoice may not exceed 20 MiB (see Chapter 2, Architecture Constraints, and ADR-014 for the decision record). One policy, ReceiptSizePolicy (kotlin-shared, de.littletrotter.shared.receipt), is the single source of truth for every number involved, so every enforcement point reads from it rather than keeping its own copy:

  • TARGET_RECEIPT_SIZE (768 KiB) — the size a receipt is reduced to at upload.

  • MAX_UNREDUCED_RECEIPT_SIZE (1 MiB) — the hard cap a user may still decline reduction up to.

  • MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE (10 MiB) — the largest raw upload accepted at all; reduction is applied.

  • MAX_RECEIPTS_PER_INVOICE (19) — distinct receipts, deduplicated by object key, per invoice.

  • MAX_INVOICE_DOCUMENT_SIZE (20 MiB) — the produced e-invoice’s hard limit.

  • INVOICE_DOCUMENT_RESERVE (1 MiB) — the invoice document’s own share of that limit.

ReceiptSizePolicy.uploadDecision(size) turns a raw upload’s size into one of three outcomes: ACCEPT_AS_IS at or below the target, OFFER_REDUCTION up to the 10 MiB upload cap, REJECT above it. The numbers add up on purpose: 19 × TARGET_RECEIPT_SIZE Base64-encoded (768 KiB inflates to exactly 1 MiB under Base64) plus the 1 MiB reserve equals 20 MiB exactly — a future edit to one constant that breaks this invariant fails ReceiptSizePolicyTest.

8.2.1. One Receipt, One Embedding

A receipt exists exactly once in the produced container: as a BG-24 AdditionalReferencedDocument / AttachmentBinaryObject (Base64-encoded) inside the ZUGFeRD XML, built by MustangTransactionBuilder. There is no rasterized annex page and no per-receipt PDF/A-3 embedded file; the produced PDF holds exactly one embedded file, the ZUGFeRD XML itself. XRechnung requires the Base64 copy regardless, so a second embedding is pure duplication. The size, mail-limit and archive-volume reasoning and the rejected alternatives are in ADR-032.

The estimator, Base64WithReserveEstimator, sizes a receipt set on the strictest channel it actually travels over: the sum of each distinct receipt’s Base64-encoded size (ceil(bytes / 3) * 4, DocumentSize.base64Encoded()) plus the INVOICE_DOCUMENT_RESERVE. Base64 is the binding choice because XRechnung carries every receipt as uncompressed Base64 text inside the invoice XML regardless of what the visible PDF does; estimating on raw bytes would understate the produced document by that 4/3 inflation factor. The estimator remains a replaceable seam (InvoiceDocumentSizeEstimator) so a later projection can account for anything this one still simplifies away — the reserve is a fixed allowance for the invoice body, surrounding XML text and embedded fonts, not a per-invoice measurement.

Deduplication is by object key, not by receipt id, and is applied consistently everywhere a receipt set is sized: the same document commonly hangs on a position and on the invoice as a whole, and the export path deduplicates the same way (MustangExportModelMapper.collectReceipts, verified against the current code). A budget that counted a shared receipt twice would refuse an invoice the exporter handles fine.

The rule is enforced at four points, deliberately layered rather than checked once:

  • Upload (ReceiptUploadService, outbound port application.invoice.port.outbound .ReceiptPdfReducer, adapter infrastructure.receipt.PdfBoxReceiptPdfReducer, PDFBox) reduces an oversized upload towards TARGET_RECEIPT_SIZE before it ever enters the pool — see Receipt Normalization at Upload below.

  • Linking (ReceiptLinkService.setReceiptsForItem/setReceiptsForInvoice, via ReceiptBudgetService.requireWithinBudget) reconstructs the invoice’s whole resulting receipt set — invoice-level receipts plus every item’s receipts, after the write — and judges it against InvoiceDocumentBudget. Only the receipt count breaking MAX_RECEIPTS_PER_INVOICE refuses the write; an estimated size above 20 MiB does not block linking, it surfaces as a warning banner in the invoice editor’s receipts tab with a link to the receipt pool, so the user can shrink individual receipts without losing the position they were building.

  • Document generation (GenerationJobCreationService.createJob) re-checks the same budget: an estimate above 20 MiB throws InvoiceDocumentSizeExceededException and no GenerationJob is created, so a set that only warned at linking time cannot silently reach the export worker.

  • Export, as a backstop (PdfExportServiceImpl.enforceSizeBackstop, kotlin-xpdf), independently refuses a produced container whose actual bytes exceed 20 MiB with InvoiceDocumentTooLargeException — the guard that fires if a receipt set ever reaches export without having passed the earlier checks, or if the estimate undercounts the true artifact size. It never truncates a document to fit.

InvoiceDocumentBudget reports a BudgetVerdict as data — Accepted, TooManyReceipts, or DocumentTooLarge — never as a thrown exception. kotlin-shared deliberately does not decide how a refusal surfaces: ReceiptBudgetService (application layer) maps TooManyReceipts to ReceiptCountLimitExceededException for the server-side guard and leaves DocumentTooLarge to the UI’s warning banner, so each layer phrases its own refusal in its own vocabulary, from one shared judgment.

8.2.2. Receipt Normalization at Upload

PdfBoxReceiptPdfReducer tries two stages in order, stopping as soon as the result meets TARGET_RECEIPT_SIZE, and a file already at or below the target is stored unchanged (ReceiptNormalisationStage.NONE):

  • Stage 1 — IMAGES_DOWNSAMPLED: walks each page’s content stream, finds every embedded image’s actual displayed size (from the CTM in effect at its paint operation, not its raw pixel count), and re-encodes any image whose effective DPI exceeds 150 as a JPEG at quality 0.75. Text, vectors and metadata are untouched — a receipt that is mostly a scanned photo with vector or text overlays often meets the target at this stage alone.

  • Stage 2 — RASTERISED: renders every page to a single 150 dpi image and rebuilds the PDF from those images. The text layer is lost, which is why this only runs once stage 1 alone is not enough; the receipt is still legible for a human reviewer, which is what the pool needs to keep proving an expense.

  • A result still above the target after both stages is rejected outright, the rejection naming the page count so the user understands why (e.g. a long multi-page statement).

150 dpi is the chosen ceiling because a receipt only needs to stay legible for human review and for OCR-assisted bookkeeping, not for print reproduction; typical phone/scanner captures arrive at 200–300+ dpi, so downsampling to 150 dpi already recovers most of the size without a visible legibility loss, and JPEG quality 0.75 is the point past which further compression buys little size back for a text-dominated scan while starting to show artifacts. Color is kept unless the source page samples as gray (checked on a coarse pixel grid): most receipts are color scans/photos even when their content is effectively monochrome, and guessing "no color needed" wrong would discard information (e.g. a highlighted total, a stamp) the derivative can never recover, whereas a page that genuinely has no color information is safely re-encoded as 1-component gray for the size win.

No original is kept (ADR-032): the reducer’s output — never the uploaded bytes — is what the pool checksums, deduplicates and stores. A receipt stored before this reducer existed, or one left at NONE because it already met the target then, can be reduced later on demand through the inbound port NormaliseReceiptUseCase; Receipt.normalisationStage and Receipt.originalSizeBytes record which stage (if any) produced the stored bytes.

Green IT rationale. The budget is not only a gateway constraint. Every receipt is copied along the whole processing chain (sender store and backup, receiver archive and backup) and transferred over several hops, and each stored copy occupies hardware for the retention period (invoices are kept for 8 years by the operator’s archive, ADR-035). Storage therefore dominates the footprint by two to three orders of magnitude over transfer. The German user guide explains this to operators with figures estimated from published agency data (UBA, LWF, EEA) — see the German user guide, appendix "Datenvolumen und Klimawirkung von Belegen". Those figures are an orientation for users, not a measurement of this system.

8.3. EN 16931 Mapping

The EN 16931 / ZUGFeRD business-term taxonomy (BT-n, plus Factur-X EXTENDED BT-X-n) is a Kotlin enum, B2bTerm (kotlin-shared), not a database-seeded catalog. Each constant carries the official BT number as its persisted SMALLINT code (B2bTermConverter, a jakarta.persistence .AttributeConverter<B2bTerm, Int>, reads via a null-safe fromCodeOrNull so a code with no matching term degrades to null instead of failing the whole read), its EN 16931 level (header, seller/buyer party, or line), its field kind (text, number, date, or enum with a value-domain code list), and — for the subset a B2bProfile can actually capture — a binding: BOUND to an existing domain property (read via reflection at rule-evaluation time) or FREE for a value with no modeled property, evidenced by an XPath into the CII export. A term with binding = null is a pure reference term: named for compliance/export purposes, but never profile-selectable beyond OPTIONAL. B2bTerm.BT_151 (the VAT category code of a line: S, AE, E, O, …) is the one deliberate exception and can never be selected in a B2B profile: the VAT category is a tax decision that follows from the tax rule chosen for the position, and a buyer’s profile must not be able to require or override it. The value is still exported — it is derived from the position’s tax snapshot, not from any profile field.

Per-country profile rules live in B2bProfile/B2bProfileRuleSet: a profile selects which BOUND or FREE terms are mandatory/conditional/optional for a given recipient, evaluated against the invoice being finalized. The layout itself is not part of a profile: every invoice renders from the one shipped layout, only the required fields differ per recipient.

The consumer note for private customers in the crafts wording profile (HANDWERK) reuses the invoice-owned note snapshot pattern of the seller’s invoice note. It is copied at creation, in the document language, when the profile is HANDWERK, the recipient type is CONSUMER and the switch is on. In draft it follows changes of recipient type and document language only while untouched, is editable and resettable in the invoice header, is frozen once SENT, and is copied by storno. The PDF prints it as a second paragraph in the note box; the XML emits it as an additional invoice note (BT-22) on the same path as the regular note. No further validation rule or blocking applies.

A business trip (ADR-021) exports as exactly one BG-25 line, never one per expense (option A of the two shapes that were considered): BT-153/BT-154 carry the trip’s title and purpose, BG-26 its period, and the forced quantity of 1. Switching the position to "detailed" adds a BT-127 line note summarizing the expenses — truncated at 1000 characters with a pointer to the attachment — and the trip workbook as a BG-24 attachment (TypeCode 916, XLSX MIME), rendered once at job-publish time and exchanged through Valkey rather than carried on the NATS envelope itself (Messaging and Event-Driven Architecture). Both the note and the attachment are supported by the Mustang/CII and Factur-X export paths (the Factur-X route is on standby: kept, not developed further).

8.4. PDF Reporting (Typst)

Figure 19. PDF Rendering Pipeline

The visible base invoice document is rendered via BasePdfRendererPort (kotlin-shared) using Typst (standard-invoice.typ, kotlin-xpdf), the only shipped layout engine; there is no fallback renderer and no .jrxml template in the render path (ADR-030). Issued invoices carry the brand mark; drafts and cancellations carry their own marks. A missing or altered document of record is resolved on access without a dialog, and a re-rendered sent invoice is a tagged reproduction that is never dispatched (ADR-034).

Property Typst Specification (kotlin-xpdf)

Engine status

Primary (shipped standard)

Template

standard-invoice.typ (resource typst/)

Layout model

Content-driven flow layout according to DIN 5008

Execution

GiroCode (EPC QR)

Determinism

Packaging
