8.4.1. Page Structure and Flow Layout

Figure 20. Invoice Layout Flow and Page Structure

  • Header (DIN 5008 Form B): Letterhead, address window, and metadata info boxes sized strictly to populated rows.

  • Line Items: Tables paginate natively across continuation pages with repeating headers; optional columns (service period, article number, VAT rate) render conditionally.

  • Totals and Payment: Payment block, totals, and notes flow dynamically. If space is tight, the payment block moves as a cohesive unit to the continuation page.

  • Universal Footer: Mandated company and register details appear on every page with automatic text fitting (fit-line).

8.4.2. Quality Assurance

Automated conformance checks replace visual review:

  • PdfLayoutInspector verifies type-area bounds, footer column spacing, and DIN 5008 address window coordinates.

  • TypstInvoiceParityIT enforces pixel-tolerance parity against the reference baseline across synthetic test fixtures.

  • Downstream pipeline (BasePdfStage, Mustang, veraPDF) validates PDF/A-3b compliance regardless of the underlying adapter.

8.5. Invoice Layout Delivery

The product ships exactly one invoice layout (ADR-030): standard-invoice.typ, a classpath resource of kotlin-xpdf. There is no template catalogue — no database rows, no upload, no per-profile or per-print-time choice, no feature switch. The layout changes only with a release, in code; what the jar delivers is what every installation renders, and the application cannot edit its own jar.

Consequently a job records no template fingerprint: generation_job carries the AI-check choice and the trigger type, nothing about a layout, and the worker renders the invoice exactly as received on the wire. A support case about a layout starts from the release version.

8.6. Invoice Lifecycle State Machine

InvoiceStateMachine (domain/invoice) is the single place in the domain that knows the legal Invoice status edges; GenerationJob uses the same shape for its own lifecycle (GenerationJobStateMachine). Each object owns a Map<Status, Set<Status>> of legal edges and exposes isLegal/assertLegal/isTerminal/successors; every write path goes through it rather than re-implementing the rule.

Figure 21. Invoice Lifecycle — State Machine

The legal edges, verified directly against InvoiceStateMachine.kt:

From To (legal successors)

DRAFT

IN_REVIEW, VOIDED

IN_REVIEW

DRAFT, SENT, VOIDED

SENT

CANCELLED

CANCELLED

(terminal — no outgoing edge)

VOIDED

(terminal — no outgoing edge)

CANCELLED and VOIDED are terminal; SENT is not — it keeps exactly one outgoing edge, the storno of a sent invoice. isReadOnly is the narrower predicate an editing view needs ("no longer accepts header/item edits"): true for every terminal status plus SENT, which otherwise accepts no edit. Absent a storno, an invoice stays SENT permanently — the normal, read-only end of its life.

A storno moves the original invoice SENT → CANCELLED, the one legal edge SENT has, and separately constructs a second document — a full 1:1 copy of the original — born directly in CANCELLED, without walking DRAFT → IN_REVIEW → SENT: there is nothing left to draft, review, or correct on an already-sent invoice. Because it never reaches a SENT edge, the storno document draws its own invoice number when it is created, from the same number range and journal as every other invoice; only then is the original moved to CANCELLED. This construction sits outside InvoiceStateMachine’s edge table by design, since there is no prior status to transition from. `Invoice.status has a private setter (ADR-025), so this born-terminal construction and synthetic demo/test data (the only two legitimate callers) go through Invoice.withInitialStatus(status), a companion factory that is deliberately outside InvoiceStateMachine’s legality check for the same reason: there is no prior status on a brand-new instance for the edge table to apply to. `transitionTo remains the only way to change the status of an invoice that already exists.

An illegal transition is a programming error, not a recoverable runtime condition: assertLegal throws, naming both statuses, and the UI never hard-codes its status-change buttons — it builds them from successors(currentStatus), so a button for an edge that is not legal cannot exist to be clicked. Status is persisted as an integer ordinal code (see Persistence below).

The IN_REVIEW → SENT edge is legal in the table above, but walking it is never a direct, synchronous UI action: "Send" only requests a SEND-triggered GenerationJob and returns immediately, the invoice stays IN_REVIEW while that job is in flight, and InvoiceLifecycleService.transition(id, SENT) itself runs only once the job completes and validation resolves VALID — see Send an Invoice in the Runtime View for the full sequence. No new InvoiceStatus was added for this: the in-flight state is visible via the job’s own GenerationJobStatus, not an invoice-level one.

8.7. Compliance Rules and the Finalization Gate

domain/compliance (kotlin-invoice) is a small rule-evaluation engine, not one monolithic completeness check: independent rule sets each look at an invoice, or a piece of master data, and report their own findings; a caller composes exactly the rule sets a situation needs.

8.7.1. Rule Sets, Subjects, and Findings

A ComplianceRuleSet declares a stable id, the ComplianceLevel`s it applies to (`SELLER, BUYER, CATALOGUE, INVOICE), and a pure, read-only evaluate(subject). ComplianceSubject is a sealed interface, so the same mechanism checks an invoice recipient or invoice issuer as a standalone master record and, in the context of one particular invoice, its effective (possibly overridden) party. ComplianceEvaluator filters rule sets by the subject’s level and flat-maps their findings, in declaration order.

A ComplianceFinding carries a ruleId whose prefix names its source (BR- EN 16931 itself, LT- a house rule, CORE. the application’s own completeness checks, B2B. a user-defined B2B profile rule), a ComplianceSeverity (ERROR blocks finalization; WARNING/INFO never do), a messageKey resolved to text only by the UI, and a FindingTarget naming where it surfaces (header, a line, seller, buyer, or a catalogue item/article).

8.7.2. The Rule Families

Rule set Blocks finalization when

CoreCompletenessRules

A mandatory header, party, or line field is missing.

B2bProfileRules

A field a B2B profile marks mandatory/conditional has no value once its trigger applies.

DueDateRules

Never — warning only; the stricter case is already a hard save-time ERROR.

FacturXEligibilityRules

The document engine is Factur-X (standby route, kept but not developed further) but neither party is in Factur-X scope.

FacturXRules

The engine is Factur-X, the recipient’s country is France, and no SIRET is set.

8.7.3. The Finalization Gate

FinalizationGate judges the validation run, not the invoice’s own content: given the ValidationStatus, the resolved validation profile, and the last KoSIT findings, it returns Allow, AllowWithWarning, or Block with a reason. FinalizationPolicy invokes both the gate and the rule-set evaluation from InvoiceLifecycleService.transition, only on the → SENT edge of the Invoice Lifecycle State Machine, after the invoice’s number is assigned but before the status itself flips. Each axis runs behind its own three-stage rollout switch (OBSERVE logs only, WARN logs louder, BLOCK enforces), so a rule already proven safe can block ahead of a global stage change via an explicit allowlist.

transition(…​, SENT) is driven by Send an Invoice's job flow. In the normal path GenerationJobResultHandler.resolveSendOutcome calls it once a SEND-triggered job’s validation resolves VALID, not the UI click directly; a ValidationStatus.UNAVAILABLE outcome allows one manual escalation, an overrideReason supplied by the Office Manager, reached from its own explicit UI action instead of the "Send" button.

8.8. Invoice Numbering and the Number Journal

Assigning an invoice number is not just formatting a string: an issued number must never be reused and never silently skipped. Three cooperating pieces implement that: the number range configuration, the counter that hands out the next value, and an append-only journal that records every allocation.

8.8.1. Number Ranges and Allocation

A number range is reference data, not code: it picks which kind of record draws from it (invoice, invoice recipient, catalogue, invoice issuer), where the prefix comes from, how much of the date is embedded in the formatted number, the zero-padding, whether issuers share one sequence or each gets its own, and whether the counter resets yearly. continuity is what the concurrency behavior actually hangs on: invoice numbers are gapless, everything else only needs to be unique.

A gap-tolerant allocation commits in its own, immediately-committed transaction — a gap left by a rolled-back save is acceptable there. A gapless allocation instead runs inside the caller’s own transaction: a single atomic upsert on the counter row serializes concurrent allocation for the same key, and a rollback releases the number again rather than burning it. Because it shares the invoice’s own transaction, the journal entry for a gapless allocation is appended in the same step, so a rollback reverts the counter and the journal entry together. The invoice number itself is allocated on the Invoice Lifecycle's → SENT edge, inside the same transaction the status change commits in; a storno document, which is created directly in CANCELLED, is numbered at creation in the same way.

A yearly-resetting range’s boundary is the invoice’s own business date, not the wall-clock instant the allocation transaction commits at: allocation takes invoice.invoiceDate, a calendar date read in the business zone Europe/Berlin, never the UTC instant of the commit (ADR-029). A → SENT transition at 00:30 on 1 January Berlin time, while UTC still reads 31 December, therefore dates and numbers the invoice into the new year, as the user sees it.

Quote numbers follow the same split as the ranges above, with the definition and the counter in different modules: the number range with the subject QUOTE (prefix, date part, counter width, reset cycle) is reference data of the invoicing module and reaches the quote module through the master-data replica, while the gapless counter lives in quote.quote_number_range per (range_code, period_key) and is drawn inside the quote module’s own status-changing transaction ("submit for review"). The invoicing module never allocates quote numbers. See ADR-033.

8.8.2. The Number Journal

Only gapless ranges are journaled — a merely-unique range’s gaps carry no audit weight. Each entry records the event, the allocated number, which entity it went to, an actor and timestamp, and a hash chaining it to the entry before it. The table is append-only at the database level, not only by convention: a database rule turns any UPDATE/DELETE against it into a no-op, regardless of role. Concurrent appends to the same chain are serialized by an advisory lock held for the transaction, not a row lock on the range.

Sealing a period appends a summary entry so the chain head at that moment can be recorded outside the system and compared back later; sealing the same period twice is rejected rather than creating a second, ambiguous head. The journal’s self-check verifies, per period, that every expected sequence number appears exactly once and that the hash chain is intact; a complementary check diffs the journal’s allocations against the actually-sent invoices, to catch a number that reached the invoice table without going through allocation, or one invoice that drew two numbers.

8.8.3. Independent Verification

A separate program, written in Go and deliberately sharing no code with the Kotlin chain implementation, re-derives the same hash chain independently and re-checks a range’s journal rows against a live database. The reasoning: a report only the audited application itself can produce is self-testimony; a second, independently written program reaching the same result is evidence. It is exercised in the test suite, which asserts it reaches the identical verdict for a clean chain, a tampered hash, a mismatched externally-supplied seal, and a smuggled control character. The binary itself remains a DevOps tool, run against a real database connection — not something CI invokes against production data on its own.

The property all three pieces protect is narrow: once an invoice number is issued, it can be shown — years later, by a program that never trusted the application that wrote it — to be exactly the number it was.

8.9. Persistence

8.9.1. Master-Data Aggregate Child Updates

SenderProfile owns additional VAT registrations. Article owns customer-specific article numbers/designations and country tax variants. Each aggregate validates the complete proposed child set before modifying its managed state. The natural keys are country and customer identity, respectively. Existing child rows retain their database identity; only added keys create rows and only omitted keys remove rows. Clearing and reinserting a collection is prohibited because insert-before-delete ordering can violate the unique parent/key constraints.

The shared domain function reconcileOwnedChildren implements this mechanism through composition. It snapshots and validates proposed values and prepares new children before mutating the owned collection. Each aggregate supplies its own key, validation, factory and field-update operations; the helper has no persistence or transaction dependencies.

Public collection views are unmodifiable. Seller VAT changes enter through immutable SellerVatRegistrationCommand values; the aggregate validates and normalizes their content. Commands express intent, while the aggregate enforces uniqueness, valid values and ownership. The existing full-form application save remains one transaction, reloads the managed root, checks the submitted revision and advances its version for child-only updates. A stale form must fail rather than replace newer child data. Commit completes before success is returned.

AbstractCrudView provides shared revision checks through typed hooks. Article and seller form DTOs retain the revision captured when the dialog opens. Section supplies full-width, border-box layout by default; individual forms control responsive field arrangement.

Figure 22. Sequence: Save Master-Data Aggregate and Child Reconciliation (SenderProfile)

Domain regression tests protect validation-before-mutation and child identity. PostgreSQL E2E tests cover repeated saves, added/removed keys and stale forms. Architecture tests pin private persistence collections and mutation entry points. JPA remains the domain write-model mapping under the project’s pragmatic architecture decision.

8.9.2. Invoice Aggregate Encapsulation and the Command/DTO Boundary

Invoice follows the same shape (ADR-025): status has a private setter — transitionTo (state-machine-checked, see Invoice Lifecycle State Machine above) and the construction-time-only withInitialStatus factory are the only ways to set it — and items/documents/partySnapshots are typed List, so addItem/ removeItem/applyPartySnapshots remain the only mutation path. The Kotlin List/MutableList split is compile-time only (both erase to java.util.List), so Hibernate’s field-access hydration of these @OneToMany collections is unaffected; only an ordinary caller’s ability to alias and mutate them is removed. receipts and llmAnalyses are deliberately left as mutable collections for now — an open follow-up named in the persistence decision, not silently claimed as migrated.

The write boundary itself is checked, not only documented: de.littletrotter.application.command defines three marker interfaces — Command, CommandResult, CommandEntryPoint — because commands share their package with plain application services, so a class-name suffix alone cannot tell a rule which types the six architecture rules below apply to.

Rule What it checks

Command purity

A Command/CommandResult type never depends on a JPA entity, EntityManager, Hibernate/Panache, a CDI-managed service, Vaadin, or an infrastructure adapter.

No entity leak

No method on a CommandEntryPoint interface leaks an entity through a parameter, return type, or nested generic collection (e.g. List<Entity>).

No direct UI mutation

No ..ui.. class calls a mutating Invoice domain method directly (addItem, transitionTo, applyPartySnapshots, …​); construction and repository/persistence-API bans are covered separately by ArchitectureRulesTest/UiLayerBoundaryTest.

Immutable commands

A Command is an immutable data carrier: no public setter, never annotated @Transactional.

Domain independent of commands

Nothing in ..domain.. depends on a Command/CommandResult type — commands translate into domain operations, never the reverse.

Entry points in the application layer

A CommandEntryPoint implementation lives in the application layer; a Command/CommandResult never carries the transaction boundary itself.

Each rule is proven twice in CommandBoundaryArchitectureTest: once against production code (must pass) and once against a small, deliberately violating test-only specimen (must fail) — a rule that only ever passes on the code that already existed is not evidence it would catch a real regression. The no-entity-leak check covers the invoice read entry point InvoiceDetailQueryPort.findLlmAnalyses: it returns the ModelReport projection (in application.dto), mapped once at the read boundary, never the InvoiceLlmAnalysis entity (ADR-025).

8.9.3. Why JPA

JPA (Hibernate ORM with Panache, under Quarkus) is the persistence approach here because Quarkus treats it as first-class — dev services, Panache repositories, and declarative transaction handling come from the same integration — and because it lets the domain model (Domain Model, Chapter 5) be written once, as JPA entities, rather than kept as a separate persistence model alongside a domain model. JPA is deliberately not asked to own the schema: quarkus.hibernate-orm.schema-management.strategy=none in every profile, so Flyway’s baseline migrations are the single source of truth for DDL (ADR-011, ADR-017) — nor to cascade a write across aggregate boundaries (Aggregates at a glance, Chapter 5): each aggregate persists through its own root, never implicitly through another’s. Query-level JPA access patterns (pagination, fetch joins, LIKE escaping) are hardened per ADR-006 rather than described again here.

8.9.4. Enum Columns

JPA entities follow one converter convention throughout: every enum-typed column is persisted as an integer code, never as a string or a raw ordinal exposed without a range check, where declaration order and business meaning could drift apart. Two column styles coexist by design: INTEGER columns with a CHECK (value BETWEEN 0 AND max) constraint (invoice.status, validation_status, currency, generation_job.status, and others), so appending a new enum constant never requires a schema change, and SMALLINT columns (e.g. system_message.severity, recipient.vat_id_verification_status). Either way, most enums use the generic OrdinalEnumConverter<E> base class (domain/shared/converters, domain/reference/converters) with the enum’s ordinal as the code; B2bTermConverter is the one exception, converting by an explicit numeric code (B2bTerm.code) because its declaration order does not match its BT numbering. InvoiceStatus uses InvoiceStatusConverter, an OrdinalEnumConverter instance over the INTEGER-with- range-check style — see Invoice Lifecycle State Machine above for its specific edge table and ordinal mapping.

8.9.5. Flyway Baseline

Schema and seed data are managed by an editable Flyway baseline: V1baseline_schema.sql (DDL) and V2baseline_seed.sql (seed data) under kotlin-invoice/src/main/resources/db/migration/ddl and …​/seed. The mail component keeps its own baseline in kotlin-email under the same rule; which baseline runs when is described in the Deployment View.

While no live instance exists yet, these two files are edited in place rather than accumulating V3, V4, …​ migrations for every schema change — a deliberate greenfield decision that trades migration history for a single, always-current baseline. The directory hygiene test enforces exactly these two files, and fresh isolated PostgreSQL checks verify schema and reference data. The approach ends the moment a real deployment’s data must survive an upgrade.

8.9.6. Time and Timezone Handling across Database, Streams, and Object Storage

As a foundational design principle, all technical systems record and exchange points in time strictly in UTC: * Database: The database never reads its own clock (ADR-029). No column has a DEFAULT NOW() or CURRENT_DATE; the application writes every date and timestamp from its TimeSource, in UTC (TIMESTAMPTZ, Hibernate property quarkus.hibernate-orm.jdbc.timezone=UTC). Where the database must stamp a moment itself — the invoice audit trigger’s occurred_at — it reads the session setting app.now, which AppNowConnectionInterceptor sets on every connection taken from the pool, and rejects the write when the setting is missing. Writers outside the application (admin scripts, the datagen loader) set app.now themselves. A test that fixes the TimeSource therefore fixes the journal’s timestamps as well. * Messaging and Event Streams: All message envelopes, event payloads, and timestamps published to NATS JetStream (e.g. GenerationJob, document-job-results) convey instants strictly in UTC (ADR-008, ADR-020). * Object Storage (RustFS): Object metadata, timestamps, and S3-compatible attributes stored in RustFS adhere strictly to standard UTC (ADR-007).

On top of this uniform UTC foundation, users interact with the system in their local time, respecting standard daylight saving transitions (DST, winter/summer time). The system default is German local time (Europe/Berlin, via GermanTime.ZONE, ADR-005, ADR-029). A stored UTC moment becomes a business day strictly through GermanTime.dayOf(…​) (kotlin-shared), never via OffsetDateTime.toLocalDate() on a UTC value or process-level ZoneId.systemDefault(). This prevents critical boundary distortions across midnight: between 00:00 and 01:00 (winter time) or 02:00 (summer time), UTC still reflects the previous calendar day. Architectural gates in ArchUnit (TimeRules.businessDay) enforce that production code never bypasses GermanTime.dayOf when deriving calendar days from stored moments.

Persisting in UTC while decoupling the user’s localized presentation also provides the architecture with the direct capability to support multi-timezone operations across geographically distributed branch offices (Niederlassungen) for mid-sized enterprises (Mittelstand).

8.9.7. Concurrent Editing

Concurrency control across independent user sessions is strictly optimistic, using one JPA @Version per aggregate root, and no lock is held across user think time (ADR-028). The decision record also holds the rationale and rejected alternatives. This mechanism is distinct from Locking, which serializes concurrent transactions within a single request.

8.9.7.1. Core Mechanism: First Committed Writer Wins

Editing contexts (dialogs, forms, grids) capture the aggregate version at read time and submit it as expectedVersion in mutation commands (e.g. UpdateInvoiceHeaderCommand, InvoiceItemCommand, TripCommand). The application layer enforces consistency before mutations occur:

  1. Pre-mutation Version Verification: The current managed aggregate is loaded. If expectedVersion != aggregate.version, the operation is aborted immediately without modifying state.

  2. Conflict Handling: Version mismatches throw jakarta.persistence.OptimisticLockException, mapped by UI layers (UiNotifications) to a dedicated conflict notification. User input is preserved in the view for manual reconciliation. An update racing a successful delete of the same row affects zero rows, which Hibernate reports as the same OptimisticLockException; ArticleManagementService.save and CustomerManagementService.save therefore flush inside their own try/catch and re-check existence on a caught conflict, reporting the readable "wurde inzwischen gelöscht" rejection instead of the ambiguous exception.

  3. Concurrent Deletions: Mutations on rows deleted concurrently abort with a domain not-found exception, never re-inserting the row; stale deletes are rejected like stale updates.

  4. Foreign-Key Concurrency: Deletions of referenced master data (e.g. Recipient, Article) racing in-flight invoice writes are resolved via explicit repository flush and translated into actionable domain errors rather than raw database constraint violations: PanacheInvoiceRepository.save flushes immediately and maps the named violations (invoice_recipient_id_fkey, invoice_sender_profile_id_fkey, invoice_item_article_id_fkey) to a readable InvalidParameterException, and the master-data repositories' delete does the same in the opposite direction.

8.9.7.2. Aggregate-Wide Version Increment (OPTIMISTIC_FORCE_INCREMENT)

Child collections such as Invoice.items and Invoice.trips are mappedBy collections whose children own the foreign key, so a child-only edit does not dirty the Invoice root. Every item and trip mutation (InvoiceItemMutationService, TripService) therefore calls InvoiceRepository.forceVersionIncrement(invoice) (em.lock(invoice, LockModeType.OPTIMISTIC_FORCE_INCREMENT)), which forces UPDATE …​ SET version = version + 1 WHERE id = ? AND version = ? on commit. The whole aggregate shares one authoritative version (ADR-028).

8.9.7.3. Per-Entity Concurrency Matrix
Entity Version Anchor Update Conflict Strategy Delete Conflict Strategy

Invoice (Header)

Invoice.version

Pre-check vs. expectedVersion; throws OptimisticLockException. Non-DRAFT writes reject via InvoiceImmutableException.

Hard deletes prevented by domain invariants (only voiding permitted).

InvoiceItem, TripExpense

Owning Invoice.version

Pre-check vs. Invoice.version. Unconditional OPTIMISTIC_FORCE_INCREMENT on parent invoice.

Requires matching expectedVersion. Concurrent delete surfaces as InvoicePositionNotFoundException.

Recipient, Article

Recipient.version, Article.version

Pre-check vs. expectedVersion. Distinguishes concurrent delete (not found) from true version mismatch.

Rejects delete if row version is stale. Cascading in-flight references protected by FK exception mapping.

Same as update strategy.
8.9.7.4. Verification Model

The concurrency contract is validated across four automated tiers:

  • Unit and Component: Isolated domain/service tests and Karibu UI mock tests.

  • Foreign Key and Race Integration: Real PostgreSQL ITs (InvoiceForeignKeyRaceIT) validating FK conflict translations.

  • High-Concurrency Invariants: Multi-threaded stress test (ConcurrentCrudStressE2EIT), verifying zero deadlocks (pg_stat_database), deterministic version progression (no lost updates), and recovery semantics.

  • End-to-End User Simulation: Browser-level Playwright specs (two-user-conflicts.spec.ts) validating UI input preservation upon conflict.

8.9.8. Locking

A transaction that must hold both a shared advisory lock and one or more row locks acquires the advisory lock first — the lock-first ordering rule — before touching any row the advisory lock is meant to protect. A row lock taken before the advisory lock, followed later in the same transaction by a wait on that advisory lock, lets two concurrent transactions each hold what the other one waits for (a classic AB-BA cycle); acquiring the advisory lock first removes the cycle by construction, since the first lock every such transaction takes is always the same one, so no interleaving of concurrent transactions can produce two of them each holding what the other waits for.

Two writers apply the rule:

  • The invoice audit journal serializes every append behind one global advisory lock, pg_advisory_xact_lock(1308). AuditGucInterceptor acquires it right after setting the audit GUCs, before the intercepted method body writes anything — the same point PostgresNumberJournalRepository.lockChain already takes its own per-range lock (1304) for the number journal (The Number Journal above). The capture trigger, capture_invoice_audit_event(), re-acquires the same lock as a safety net for a write that reaches these tables outside the application; re-acquiring a lock the transaction already holds is free.

  • The generation-job stale-delegated reset’s write step (PanacheGenerationJobRepository.resetStaleDelegatedByIds) re-selects the ids its own read step already decided on, ORDER BY j.id with a PESSIMISTIC_WRITE lock and a lock-timeout hint Hibernate maps to Postgres' FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED, then updates one row per statement — one fixed, ascending order instead of the arrival order of concurrent callers, and no bulk statement that would keep every row lock it has already taken while it waits for the next one — so a concurrent transaction holding one generation_job row is skipped rather than raced against in the opposite order. The read step that decides which ids to reset (StaleDelegatedResetCoordinator, see Messaging and Event-Driven Architecture below) runs before this lock is ever taken and involves a NATS round trip — deliberately never inside the lock-holding transaction, so a slow or unreachable broker can never turn into a Postgres lock-wait pile-up on generation_job.

8.9.9. The Excel Export’s Read Side: A Dedicated Projection, Not the Aggregate

The Excel export (Chapter 5) reads through InvoiceExportReadRepository, a JPA Criteria-API query returning scalar tuples (Array<Any?>, decoded column-by-index into row DTOs), rather than loading Invoice JPA entities the way every other read path in this application does. That is a deliberate exception, not an oversight, forced by three traps a plain findAll/entity-graph read would walk straight into for this one use case:

  • Invoice.references is a write-triggering accessor. Reading it outside a transaction that is prepared to persist the result marks the entity dirty on first touch (a lazy-initialization side effect elsewhere in the domain model, not a bug specific to export) — and an export over a filtered set of possibly thousands of invoices is exactly the kind of read that must never accidentally schedule thousands of writes.

  • InvoiceNumberConverter throws on a malformed stored value. An entity-based read decodes every converter-backed column as part of materializing the entity; one invoice with unparseable legacy number data would abort the whole export instead of just that row’s number column. A scalar projection defers decoding to the row mapper, which degrades one field, not the export.

  • Loading full aggregates for a read that only ever renders a fixed set of columns is needless N+1 risk — the projection selects exactly the columns each sheet needs, across a LEFT JOIN where a value is optional (e.g. articleRef’s article number, the invoice’s own `invoice.currency), and nothing else.

The cost of this choice is that adding a new exported column means adding a Criteria selection, an Idx constant, and a row-mapper decode step in three places kept deliberately parallel (see `InvoicePositionRowMapper’s own column-index KDoc table) rather than simply reading a new getter off an already-loaded entity — a small, explicit tax paid once per column, in exchange for an export that cannot dirty an invoice or abort on one bad row.

8.9.10. Caching (Valkey)

Figure 23. Caching Layers

Valkey is the one cache in this system, used for three distinct things: the text-payload exchange behind NATS envelopes (KoSIT reports and trip workbooks — see Messaging and Event-Driven Architecture below), the receipt-checksum fast path ahead of the database’s own unique constraint (see Upload Receipt in the Runtime View for why that constraint, not the cache, is the actual duplicate-prevention guarantee), and a handful of application-level read caches (ValkeyInvoiceCacheClient: rendered PDF bytes, job-status lookups, and invoice-overview query results — all TTL’d, all degrading to a plain cache miss rather than an error on a Valkey outage). The one rule that holds across all three, stated once here because it applies system- wide: audit-relevant dispatch never depends on this cache — see Audit-Relevant Dispatch May Not Depend on a Cache under Messaging.

8.10. Messaging and Event-Driven Architecture

Figure 24. Event-Driven Core

An event in this architecture is a lightweight reference envelope published on NATS JetStream (document-jobs, document-job-results) — never raw document bytes (ADR-008). Heavy binary artifacts reside in RustFS (S3), while transient text payloads live in Valkey.

8.10.1. Event Backbone: NATS JetStream

The messaging tier uses NATS JetStream as its durable event backbone (ADR-020): a lightweight Go broker delivering persistence, at-least-once guarantees, and consumer groups on low-power hardware. The Green IT rationale (Green IT Principles) adds a <128 MiB broker footprint, sub-100 ms startup, negligible idle draw, and power-off tolerance via PostgreSQL recovery, which makes a durable event-driven architecture viable on a mini PC that could not sustain a JVM-cluster broker. The broker is isolated in infrastructure.messaging behind domain command and result ports; replacing it with another broker is an adapter change (ADR-020).

8.10.2. Workers and Recovery Mechanics

Figure 25. Worker Recovery Layers

The generation pipeline is asynchronous: kotlin-invoice publishes a GenerationJob onto NATS JetStream (DocumentJobPublisher, GenerationJobNatsAdapter), kotlin-xpdf consumes it, renders, validates, and publishes a result back on the document-job-results subject (ResultPublisher / GenerationJobResultConsumer). The envelope principle keeps NATS messages small: only a typed reference structure (GenerationJobResultRefs in kotlin-shared — object keys, a download URL, LLM-callback fields) travels over NATS. Text payloads (KoSIT validation reports) are exchanged via Valkey behind a PayloadExchange port, keyed by a reference the envelope carries; PDFs and other binary artifacts live in RustFS (S3-compatible object storage), referenced the same way. A missing/expired Valkey entry degrades to a logged warning and a null report, never a processing failure.

8.10.2.1. Dispatch Fast Path and Safety Nets

Document generation uses a reactive primary path complemented by multi-tiered recovery mechanisms to balance near-instantaneous execution with crash-resilient guarantees.

Fast Path (Event-Driven Primary Flow)

  1. Job Creation and Enqueueing: A GenerationJob is persisted to PostgreSQL in status PENDING within the primary business transaction.

  2. Post-Commit Dispatch: Immediately following successful database commit, a post-commit flush publishes a dispatch message to the JetStream stream document-jobs (bulk lane) or document-jobs-interactive (interactive lane), chosen from the lane stored on the job.

  3. Execution: kotlin-xpdf acts as the dedicated stream consumer (JobConsumer), retrieving work directly via event streaming without polling delay.

Safety Nets (Durable Recovery Paths)

To protect against process crashes, uncommitted stream publishes, or network partitions, background safety nets ensure self-healing:

  • Startup Recovery: FastPathExecutor triggers an asynchronous sweep upon worker initialization, enqueuing uncompleted PENDING jobs created before restart.

  • Periodic Fallback Sweep: GenerationJobProcessor runs a low-frequency background scan (invoice.jobs.recovery-interval, default 1 h) guarded against concurrent overlapping execution. This serves strictly as an emergency catch-up path for lost event notifications.

  • Stale Job Re-delegation: A dedicated probe evaluates jobs remaining in DELEGATED status beyond staleCutoffMinutes (default 2 min) against the broker’s stream cursor before re-dispatching (see Stale-DELEGATED Probe).

  • Exhaustion and Dead-Lettering: Jobs exceeding retry budgets and broker ack leases are routed to LITTLE_TROTTER_DLQ for diagnostic quarantine.

Architectural Trade-Offs
Dimension Assessment

Latency and Efficiency

Sub-second rendering turnaround under normal operation. Minimizes database I/O by replacing continuous polling with event notifications.

Delivery Semantics

JetStream provides at-least-once delivery. Consumers and downstream result processors must enforce strict idempotency and durable claim semantics.

Operational Complexity

System recovery relies on coordinated states between transactional storage (PostgreSQL) and the message broker (JetStream), requiring deep diagnostic observability across both layers.
8.10.2.2. Stale-DELEGATED Probe

A DELEGATED GenerationJob is re-published (on the subject of its stored lane) only when its document-jobs or document-jobs-interactive message no longer exists in the stream — never merely because a database timestamp looks old. GenerationJob carries delegatedAt/delegatedStreamSeq (the JetStream sequence its own publish landed at, from the PublishAck), stamped in the same write as its PENDING → DELEGATED transition. Once a row is older than staleCutoffMinutes (default 2 min) it becomes a candidate, not an automatic reset.

StaleDelegatedResetCoordinator asks the broker through DelegatedMessageProbe:

Probe finding Action

Candidate’s sequence past the stream’s last sequence

Stream was purged and recreated → reset (outright).

Still ahead of the worker consumer’s delivered cursor

Never even handed out yet (queued backlog or worker down) → leave alone.

Delivered and the broker still has the message

In flight or awaiting redelivery under `JobConsumer’s JetStream lease → leave alone.

Delivered and the broker reports it gone

Acked, purged, broker data lost, no result ever recorded → reset, incrementing attempts.

No sequence was ever recorded

The publish ack never arrived → reset (same path).

A worker being down can therefore never burn a job’s attempt budget on its own — an undelivered message is never a reset candidate in the first place. A broker failure while deciding aborts that whole tick’s reset without touching any row, logged once at WARN, retried on the next tick.

One GenerationJob purpose reads this same result delivery as more than a status update: GenerationJobResultConsumer calls GenerationJobResultHandler.resolveSendOutcome as a plain, synchronous, sequential continuation right after markCompleted/markFailed, for any job whose trigger is SEND — see Send an Invoice in the Runtime View. This is deliberately not another observer on the DocumentGenerated event above: those observers are independent and unordered by design (so one reaction’s failure never rolls back a sibling’s), which is exactly wrong for a step that must run only after the validation-status resolution has already happened. resolveSendOutcome instead re-derives the outcome directly from the job’s own already-persisted KoSIT report and, only on VALID, calls InvoiceLifecycleService.transition. Nothing here polls: the worker’s result message is already the trigger, matching Green IT Principles — no new @Scheduled bean exists for this feature.

A late-but-genuine result for a job whose own stale-DELEGATED reset already raced it back to PENDING is reclaimed through the state machine’s existing PENDING → DELEGATED → COMPLETED|FAILED edges rather than rejected — a defense-in-depth safety net for that residual race, not a loosening of the state machine’s edge table.

8.10.2.3. Lease, Retry, and Dead-Letter

kotlin-xpdf’s `JobConsumer holds each message under a JetStream lease (ackWaitSeconds, default 120 s) for as long as this JVM is actually holding it — queued behind other work or rendering — renewed by a periodic in-progress heartbeat scheduled at hand-off to the worker’s bounded thread pool, not only once rendering starts, so a burst that queues more jobs than the pool can immediately start never lets a merely-queued job’s lease lapse. max_deliver on that lease is unbounded by design (JetStream, not this consumer, owns ordinary redelivery); a message whose own delivery count exceeds the worker’s configured retry budget is instead treated as poison and routed to document-jobs-dlq with a FAILED result, the same path an unparseable payload takes — the bound against a message that can never durably have its result published looping forever.

Idempotency and retry are implemented, not just intended: GenerationJobResultConsumer.consume re-delivers into the same retry loop with exponential backoff (three attempts, doubling from 100 ms), clearing the JPA persistence context before every attempt so a poisoned first-level cache entry from a failed flush cannot make every retry fail identically. A result for a job already in a terminal state (COMPLETED/FAILED) is logged and dropped rather than reapplied, so a re-delivered or duplicate message cannot double-write findings. Unrecoverable processing failures (malformed payload, message handler exception) are routed to a dead-letter subject (document-job-results-dlq) instead of retried forever; if the DLQ publish itself fails, the message is nak()-ed for JetStream redelivery rather than dropped.

8.10.2.4. Maintenance Wake-Ups

The maintenance queue follows ADR-024: PostgreSQL is authoritative, while a committed request emits a non-durable Core NATS hint on maintenance.v1.<instance>.requested. The worker validates the hint and schedules a serialized drain; the maintenance recovery scan (invoice.maintenance.recovery-interval, default 5 min) remains the safety net. Each short transaction uses a per-invoice claim-token fence. An interrupted RUNNING maintenance claim becomes UNKNOWN and requires manual requeue; it is never auto-replayed. A failed or unknown recalculation blocks dependent generation until a later successful recalculation. The drain keeps recalculation ahead of issued-document generation.

The two daily tasks (ChainVerificationScheduler, SystemMessageRetentionScheduler) fire on a plain @Scheduled(every = "24h") in-JVM timer, but each checks the most recent DatabaseMaintenanceEvent of its own type before doing any work (one interval for the whole maintenance cycle): a null last-run event (fresh deployment) is due immediately, an event less than 24 h old is skipped, and an event 24 h or older is due again. A restart shortly after a real run does not re-run within minutes, and a restart after the interval has already elapsed still catches up rather than silently waiting for the next in-JVM tick. No energy or consumption measurement is part of this mechanism; no consumption savings are claimed.

The pattern repeats above the job pipeline: every open invoice list, print-job list, and detail view currently polls every two to five seconds regardless of whether a job is in flight (LIVE_UPDATE_POLL_INTERVAL_MS/DETAIL_POLL_INTERVAL_MS), which a planned change narrows to "only while a job is pending".

8.10.3. Mail Dispatch: A Second Producer, the Same Envelope Principle

The optional mail dispatch component (see Building Block View) joins this picture without changing its rules. It owns its two streams and creates them itself: the invoicing stack’s stream setup does not know them, so an instance without the component never creates them. mail.send.requested stays private to the component — the internal dispatch queue between its own API and its own SMTP consumer, which the invoicing application must never publish to. The only subject that crosses the context boundary is mail.send.result, and it crosses in one direction: component to application, fire-and-forget, surviving an application restart, with no callback coupling in the opposite direction. The consumer for it is registered only while the feature is enabled, so a mail-less instance has neither a stream nor a consumer waiting for one.

The envelope principle of ADR-008 is unchanged and, in fact, is the reason the compose handover is deliberately a REST call rather than a message: an interactive request whose answer a user is waiting for would otherwise have to park its payload in Valkey just to satisfy the envelope rule — adding a cache dependency to the interactive path. The rule the project keeps is therefore stated as a boundary, not as a transport preference: messaging carries everything that must survive; the one synchronous call carries the one thing a human is waiting for, and it creates no durable state that matters (a lost draft means the user clicks the button again).

8.10.4. Archive Handover: Status, Retry, Administrator Notice

The handover of a dispatched mail to the operator’s archive (ADR-037) is a second durable step after sending and never part of the send itself.

  • Status: per journal entry MailArchiveHandoverStatus: NOT_CONFIGURED (no archive address), DISABLED (switch of the document family off), PENDING, HANDED_OVER (archive server answered SMTP 250) and FAILED (with error text and time). The axis is independent of the status of the signed archive package.

  • Trigger and durability: the outbox handler for MailSent sends the byte-identical message to the archive address as an envelope recipient only; the result is committed to PostgreSQL before success is reported, so a crash between sending and handover leaves a retryable PENDING entry instead of a lost copy.

  • Retry: FAILED entries are retried automatically with a limited number of attempts and backoff, in the pattern of the send sweeper; a manual retry is available on the journal endpoint.

  • No lock: a failed handover never blocks or reverts the customer dispatch.

  • Administrator notice: a failure and a missing archive address produce a notice in the message center for administrators; the missing address stays visible until it is configured.

  • Configuration: archive address and the two switches (invoice, quote) belong to the mail component and are changeable by administrators only. The invoicing UI shows the status as a symbol in the mail journal and the dispatch log.

8.10.5. Audit-Relevant Dispatch May Not Depend on a Cache

A sent mail leaves the building and is evidence for the tax office, which makes one question architectural rather than an implementation detail of one service: which store may a dispatch in flight depend on? The answer the mail dispatch concept establishes, and which this documentation records as the system-level rule:

Nothing that can leave the building may depend on a cache. Valkey holds pre-decision state only — the compose draft. From the moment the user presses send, PostgreSQL and object storage hold everything the dispatch needs.

Concretely: the journal row and the authored content are written durably before any dispatch envelope is published, the consumer reconstructs the whole send from those two sources, and what is read back is verified against the checksums recorded at authoring time before a message is built — a mismatch aborts with a recorded failure instead of sending. The failure mode this buys is the mild one: a cache outage degrades to "no new mail can be composed" instead of "queued mails fail", and it removes the class of defect where a truncated cache entry decodes into a valid-looking order and a real invoice recipient receives an empty mail, or an invoice mail without its attachment, recorded as successfully sent. This differs on purpose from the generation path above, where a missing Valkey payload is a documented degrade to a null validation report: a validation report that does not arrive stays inside the system, a mail that does not arrive correctly does not.

8.11. Input Ports

The UI and the inbound adapters reach the application layer only through input ports: role interfaces cut per use case (ADR-027). There are no composite interfaces that bundle several roles and no *Facade types; each caller injects exactly the ports it uses.

Convention Rule

Package

de.littletrotter.application.<context>.port.inbound — e.g. application.invoice, application.masterdata, application.reference (inbound, because in is a Kotlin keyword).

Reads

*QueryPort (InvoiceListQueryPort, InvoiceDetailQueryPort, RecipientQueryPort, TaxRateQueryPort, …​). Never a CommandEntryPoint.

Writes

*UseCase (CreateInvoiceUseCase, EditInvoiceHeaderUseCase, ChangeInvoiceStatusUseCase, ManageRecipientUseCase, ManageTaxRateUseCase, …​). Always a CommandEntryPoint, so the command/DTO rules above apply to every write port.

Reference data

One <Catalogue>QueryPort and one Manage<Catalogue>UseCase per catalogue; read-only catalogues (languages) have only the query port.

Implementation

One application-layer class per port, named after the use case (InvoiceListQueries, EditInvoiceHeader, TaxRateQueries, ManageTaxRate); write authorization (@RequiresPermission) sits on the use-case implementation.

Size

At most 12 methods per port (overloads count individually), guideline ≤ 8; cut by caller cluster, not by single method.

Composition

No input port extends another; no interface combines two or more of the application’s own interfaces.

Signatures

DTOs and value types only; entities never cross a port.

A view that needs many ports is split into injected sub-components rather than handed a parameter object: InvoiceDetailView composes InvoiceCockpitHero, InvoiceDetailTabs and InvoiceEntryPane, and each of them takes only its own ports. InputPortArchitectureTest checks the package, naming, size and composition rules against production code and against violating specimens; CommandBoundaryArchitectureTest covers the write ports.

8.12. UI Concept

The UI is built with Vaadin (server-side rendering, MainLayout as the shell). German is the default display language and English the backup, both served from one bundle pair (messages_de.properties/messages.properties, never resolved through the host JVM’s own default locale). TranslationService (ui/i18n) implements Vaadin’s I18NProvider against that pair; UiTranslations gives non-Vaadin call sites (dialogs, mappers, the invoice/trip workbook export) the same lookup outside a component’s own translation context; I18nBundleResource serves the resolved bundle as JSON at /api/i18n, read by the sibling Playwright suite so its label assertions never drift from a hand-copied string. All three planes resolve a bundle value’s {term:…​} markers (the per-instance wording profile’s vocabulary substitution, ADR-022) before any java.text.MessageFormat pass, since a term marker is not a valid MessageFormat argument index and would otherwise throw. A missing translation key or an unresolvable term marker both render visibly (!{key}!/!{term:name}!) and are logged once per JVM lifetime, so neither is silently swallowed at render time; a parity test additionally checks that every {term:…​} marker used in either bundle resolves for all three wording profiles and both locales, so a gap surfaces at build time rather than in production. Formatting call sites that do not go through the bundle (dates, month names, currency) construct Locale.GERMAN/ Locale.GERMANY explicitly (MoneyFormatter, TrendChart, list/detail views) instead.

Dark mode follows the OS color scheme, not an in-app toggle: AppShell injects a small inline script that reads prefers-color-scheme before first paint and keeps following runtime OS changes, setting theme="dark" on the document root; both Lumo/Vaadin components and the app’s own CSS tokens (themes/littletrotter/styles.css) read the same attribute, so there is one source of truth for light/dark rather than two independently toggled surfaces. Accessibility is addressed incrementally at the component level (explicit ariaLabel`s on icon-only actions and custom widgets) rather than through a single blanket mechanism; every UI/color change is checked against contrast and dark-mode rendering as a matter of review discipline, per the project’s color guidelines. Menu ergonomics (drawer behavior, breadcrumbs, notification bell/badge) are covered by dedicated Karibu tests (`MainLayoutDrawerKaribuTest, MainLayoutBellBadgeKaribuTest, MainLayoutBreadcrumbKaribuTest, and siblings).

The mail views are part of this UI: MailComposeView (mail/compose/:draftId, reached from the send action on an invoice), MailJournalView (mail/journal) and MailTemplatesView (mail/templates, with MailTemplateEditorDialog and MailTemplateHistoryDialog). The journal filters, sorts and pages on the server, keeps filter, sort and page in the URL, shows status chips and row details, exports to Excel and offers retry and re-archive for failed entries. The routes are gated in RouteAccess by MAIL_COMPOSE_AND_SEND, MAIL_JOURNAL_VIEW and MAIL_TEMPLATE_EDIT and use the same i18n bundles, theme and dark mode as every other view; the drawer entry and the send action appear only while MailAvailability allows them.

8.13. Security and Operations

Caddy is the stack’s sole externally exposed service (docker/caddy/Caddyfile), terminating TLS 1.2/1.3 in front of the Vaadin/Quarkus application, setting HSTS/X-Content-Type-Options/ X-Frame-Options/Referrer-Policy headers, and blocking direct external access to Quarkus management endpoints (/q/*) that a catch-all reverse proxy would otherwise expose. TLS_MODE selects between Caddy’s internal CA (development/LAN) and Let’s Encrypt ACME (public domains) at container-start substitution time, without changing the Caddyfile.

Every application-owned service in docker-compose.yml sets security_opt: no-new-privileges:true; the applications' own containers additionally drop all Linux capabilities (cap_drop: [ALL]) and carry explicit memory/PID limits under deploy.resources (infrastructure services — Postgres, RustFS, NATS, Valkey — deliberately keep their default capability sets, scoped out of the capability-drop by a documented decision, not an oversight). Backups are a DevOps operation (not application code) and are age-encrypted end-to-end: in Phase 1 (operational MVP until Q4/2027), admin-tools/scripts/backup/backup.sh executes backups and never leaves a plaintext archive behind an unverified ciphertext — its scrub step refuses to delete plaintext components unless a matching *.tar.gz.age file already exists in the target directory; in Phase 2 (from Q4/2027), this is executed via lt backup under the consolidated management plane. Authentik (OIDC) is not part of the repository’s base compose file; on the server it runs as a shared service outside the instance projects and is the identity provider of every instance (Chapter 7). Where it is used, it gets its own PostgreSQL database (little-trotter-auth) in the same instance rather than a second Postgres instance or a shared schema, so an identity-store restore never has to touch invoice data and vice versa.

8.13.1. Data Protection (GDPR)

The application processes personal data of invoice recipients and senders: customer master data, contact persons, and e-mail addresses (mail dispatch). Issued invoices contain this data and are subject to statutory retention: the legal retention period for invoices takes precedence over erasure requests until it ends at the operator’s archive (ADR-035). The application has no automated erasure, anonymization, or blocking function; personal data in master data is changed or removed through the regular editors only. The workbook export carries a data-protection notice section (ExportSheetWriter).

8.14. Test Strategy and Test Architecture

Testing is split across the projects in this repository’s own Maven reactor, plus one browser-E2E project in the sibling little-trotter-admin-tools repository (see Building Block View for how the two repositories divide the test and tooling projects):

Project Scope Mechanism

kotlin-invoice (src/test)

Unit tests, JPA/Flyway integration tests (Testcontainers PostgreSQL), and headless Vaadin UI tests for views, layout, and menu ergonomics

JUnit 6 + Testcontainers; Karibu Testing for server-side Vaadin components (*KaribuTest naming), no browser involved

kotlin-e2e-tests

System-level integration tests exercising real infrastructure end-to-end (NATS redelivery/dedup, S3 round-trips, receipt/print annexes, e-invoice generation) rather than mocked collaborators; also carries the Karibu-based monkey suite — seeded random walks (MonkeyRun support class) over the invoice, profile, and system views, surfacing states scripted scenarios do not think to try

JUnit 6 + Testcontainers (PostgresTestResource, NatsTestResource, S3TestResource, ValkeyTestResource), test classes suffixed E2EIT; the monkey suite runs as ITs tagged monkey, excluded from the default failsafe run

coverage-report

Aggregates unit, integration, and E2E exec data across kotlin-shared, kotlin-xpdf, kotlin-invoice, and kotlin-e2e-tests into one coverage figure, gated at 90% line / 90% branch (jacoco.line.coverage/jacoco.branch.coverage in the root pom.xml) — the UI layer is included in this gate, not carved out

e2e-test-playwright (sibling little-trotter-admin-tools repository)

Linter gates run as Maven exec-maven-plugin executions bound ahead of the test phase and are mandatory, not advisory: ktlint-check and detekt-check (with a ratcheting baseline — detekt-baseline-ratchet.sh — that only ever tightens) cover Kotlin, sqlfluff-check covers SQL migrations, plus a config-format check; all four can be bypassed locally only via an explicit -DskipLinterGates=true flag, never silently. The sibling repository’s make playwright CMD=check target runs a TypeScript check and a traceability matrix (tools/spec-matrix.ts) reporting which specification scenario IDs are implemented, marked fixme, or still open — the same scenario-ID convention that makes Playwright and Kotlin E2E test titles traceable back to their originating specification without a separate mapping document.

8.15. Error Handling and Validation

Validation is layered, each layer catching a different class of mistake before it reaches the database. At the UI, editor forms validate per field via Vaadin’s Binder (IBAN, email, postal code, and similar single-purpose validators) before a save is even attempted. Below the UI, the domain enforces its own invariants independently of whatever the Binder already checked, because not every rule is expressible per field (e.g. a cross-object consistency check); these surface as a small, typed exception hierarchy — not-found, illegal transition, immutable, storno-not-allowed, and one consolidated field-validation exception carrying a machine-readable error code rather than pre-rendered text. At the application boundary, a translator narrows that variety down to three generic outcomes — bad input, not found, conflict — that a UI presenter can react to without knowing domain vocabulary. Above both, the Compliance Rules and the Finalization Gate are a later layer: they judge the invoice as a whole, at the one moment (the → SENT transition) after every per-field and per-object check has already passed.

Every resolved error reaches the user through one shared notification component, distinguishing auto-dismissing confirmations from warnings and from errors that require an explicit close, rather than through ad hoc alerts scattered across views; an exception resolves to displayed text only through an i18n bundle key, never a hard-coded string. Every shown error is also persisted to the Message Center with a short dedup window, so a repeating failure does not flood the log and an admin auditing the system later sees what users saw live.

Background job failures are a distinct case: nothing is on the call stack of a user action to catch. Their retry and dead-letter semantics are covered in Messaging and Event-Driven Architecture above; from this section’s perspective, a terminal job status is simply a normal, renderable UI state, not an exception a view has to catch.

The one rule that matters for an audit-relevant path: a Block from the finalization gate is never bypassable from the UI — there is no "save anyway" affordance — and whether a check currently blocks at all is itself staged (OBSERVE/WARN/BLOCK), never silently hard-coded, so a newly added rule is observed against real data before it can stop anyone.

8.15.1. VAT ID: One Field, Visible Prefix Recognition

A VAT ID (German: USt-IdNr.) stays one text field everywhere it appears — on an invoice recipient, on an invoice issuer profile, and on an additional invoice issuer VAT registration row — rather than splitting country and number into two fields; VatIdValidator (domain/value) is the single validator behind all three, so the format rule is defined once and the three places can never quietly drift apart. The shared VatIdField UI component (ui/component) layers the visible ergonomics on top of it: normalizing the value on blur (whitespace stripped, upper-cased), auto-completing the two- character prefix from the linked address country when the Office Manager types digits only (vatPrefixFor, the alpha-2 code except Greece, whose EU VAT prefix is EL rather than GR), showing the recognized country’s name as a suffix once a prefix is known, and offering a country- specific placeholder (DE123456789, ATU12345678, NL123456789B01, …) rather than one generic example.

Format validation runs on blur and returns a structured VatIdValidator.Violation (TOO_LONG/NO_PREFIX/UNKNOWN_PREFIX/FORMAT) rather than a pre-rendered sentence, resolved to its message through the i18n bundle the same way every other validation outcome in this section is — never a hard-coded string. A prefix that disagrees with the linked address country (a German company invoicing from an Austrian VAT registration is a legitimate case) is information, not an error, for an invoice recipient and an invoice issuer profile; the same disagreement on an additional invoice issuer VAT registration row is an error, because that row’s own country combo is the definitive answer and nothing else can be. Verifying the id against the EU VIES REST API is a separate, later step, recipient-only, and never automatic — see Verify an Invoice Recipient’s VAT ID and ADR-023.

8.16. Configuration

Configuration is layered, and each layer owns a different kind of value: application defaults live in code, per-environment overrides live in docker-compose.yml, and secrets live in an untracked .env file — a value that must differ between installs is never baked into an image.

Quarkus’s own application.properties is the base layer, with a development-mode default via ${VAR:default} syntax; its profile mechanism (%dev, %test, %prod, and the project’s own %worker) overrides specific keys per profile in the same file rather than via separate property files. A handful of security-sensitive properties (datasource password, S3 credentials, OIDC client secret) declare no %prod fallback at all, so a production deployment missing the corresponding variable refuses to start rather than silently running against a real database with a development default.

docker-compose.yml turns a built image into a specific running container: which profile it runs, whether its scheduler is enabled, and which hostnames/ports it reaches its dependencies on are all set through environment: entries there, never by building a different image per role — the app and the job worker are the same build artifact, told apart only by their environment.

Secrets are never committed and never hardcoded: they live in a repo-root .env file, generated by scripts/dev/setup-secrets.sh (make secrets), which refuses to overwrite an existing file and, on make secrets-check, rejects known-weak placeholder values.

The table below lists the parameters DevOps actually sets — not internal Quarkus tuning.

8.17. Feature Switches

An optional capability is gated twice, and the two gates are independent. The deployment switch says whether the capability is installed at all; the instance offer says whether an installed capability is offered to the users of one instance. A capability is usable only when both are on, so an operator can install a component without exposing it, and no setting in the UI can conjure a component that is not deployed. The e-mail component is the reference case: MailAvailability combines both gates, and every UI entry point and adapter asks it instead of reading either flag; QuoteAvailability.offered does the same for the offer component. The delivered configuration has the component switched off.

Switch Scope Default Effect

MAIL_ENABLED (mail.enabled)

Deployment

false

Off: no HTTP dispatch adapter, no mail-result consumer, no drawer entry, no mail route, no send action; the settings mail card reads "not installed".

feature.email.enabled (FeatureFlag)

Instance

on

Off: an installed component is not offered to this instance’s users; no effect while the deployment switch is off.

QUOTE_ENABLED (quote.enabled)

Deployment

false

Off: no offer drawer section, no offer route, no settings offer card.

feature.quote.enabled (FeatureFlag)

Instance

on

Off: offer section, routes and command palette entries disappear and offer-changing calls are refused; the settings offer card stays so the module can be switched on again. Master-data replication and accepted-offer events keep running, so switching back on needs no resync.

8.18. Observability

Both Quarkus services expose the standard Quarkus health endpoints (/q/health/live, /q/health/ready; kotlin-xpdf remaps its root to /health). docker-compose.yml’s own container healthchecks curl these directly, and `depends_on: condition: service_healthy gates startup ordering on them — the app and worker only start once Postgres, RustFS, NATS, and Valkey report healthy. The health paths are the one deliberate, unauthenticated hole in the HTTP auth policy, so Caddy’s own healthcheck can reach them.

Logging is asymmetric by design: kotlin-xpdf logs structured JSON from the start; kotlin-invoice logs Quarkus’s default human-readable console format. Log level is configured per Quarkus category and profile, not through a separate logging config file. There is no project-wide structured-logging or trace-propagation concept; it is not implemented and is tracked as technical debt in chapter 11.

Metrics and dashboards are an optional overlay, not part of the core stack; see Operations and Admin Tooling (Chapter 7) for what it scrapes and how it is reached. Because it is optional, its absence changes nothing about the core stack’s own health checks and logs, which are the observability signals actually relied on if the overlay is never started.

8.19. Green IT Principles

Green IT is a design constraint of this system, not a feature: the target is small-budget hardware (a mini PC running Ubuntu Server), the load is 20–200 invoices a month, and DevOps may switch the machine off in the evening to save power. The principles below are what a change is checked against; where a rule is not yet fully met, the polling work package names the planned change, and a baseline measurement (watts at the wall, RSS per container, queries per page view) is the acceptance criterion the PO schedules separately.

  1. Small-budget hardware is the target. Every compose service carries an explicit memory limit (Chapter 7); outgrowing the target is a documented swap path (ADR-020), never a reason to grow the default footprint.

  2. No work without a trigger — push over poll. A new generation job reaches the worker through the fast path and NATS (ADR-020), not a timer. The worker’s hourly recovery scan (invoice.jobs.recovery-interval) and asynchronous startup pass recover persisted generation jobs when that trigger is missed; UI live updates still use polling until a planned change narrows it to pending jobs.

  3. On demand, not always-on. The optional Factur-X engine container (standby, kept but not developed further) starts for its first job and stops again after an idle period — the pattern for every optional heavyweight component.

  4. Power-off tolerance. Generation-job state survives a worker restart because recovery reads PostgreSQL and runs at startup. The two daily maintenance tasks each check the most recent DatabaseMaintenanceEvent of their own type before running, so a restart neither re-runs one within minutes of its last run nor silently skips a day it missed. All state lives in PostgreSQL, JetStream’s file store, and RustFS; Valkey is a cache and shared fast path whose loss costs latency, never data.

  5. Maintenance is DevOps-driven. The database is vacuumed, reindexed, and backed up when DevOps decides, from the System tab or the admin tooling; the application neither runs maintenance on its own nor snapshots its status on a timer.

  6. Reuse over recompute. Validation verdicts are cached by content hash and profile; receipts are deduplicated by checksum; a re-published job is deduplicated by the broker — a second export of an unchanged document must not cost a second validation run.

  7. Optional capabilities are off unless configured. The local LLM analysis (oMLX; see AI-Assisted Validation Review below) runs only when MLX_ENDPOINT is set — unset means the capability does not exist for that deployment: no UI element, no job option, no connection attempt.

  8. Measure before optimizing — deferred, not dropped. The first measurement is the acceptance criterion for everything above and is scheduled by the PO for a later wave; until then, no optimization beyond these rules is started on assumption alone.

8.20. Dependency Vulnerability Scanning

Dependabot alerts are the first source for known vulnerabilities in direct dependencies. The OWASP dependency-check Maven plugin (org.owasp:dependency-check-maven, version 13.0.0) is the second source: it also resolves transitive runtime JARs and matches them against the NVD.

8.20.1. NVD API Key

The plugin reads the key from an environment variable (plugin parameter nvdApiKeyEnvironmentVariable, variable NVD_API_KEY), never from a command-line property, because properties appear in process lists and logs. The key is stored in ~/.nvd.env (file mode 600) on the developer machine and on the test host. A scan sources that file first (source ~/.nvd.env). The value is never printed, logged, or committed.

8.20.2. Scan and Triage

  • The scan runs over the whole reactor on the test host (see Verification on the Test Host) and produces the HTML report dependency-check-report.html.

  • The scan is the Dagger function security in ci/main.go (dagger call security --source=.. --nvd-api-key=env:NVD_API_KEY); Dagger passes the key into the build container as a secret variable, so it does not appear in the container’s logs.

  • Critical and high findings with a fixed upstream version are resolved by pinning that version in the dependencyManagement section of the parent POM, so the pin applies to every module and to transitive paths. Example: kotlin-reflect is managed to ${kotlin.version}.

  • Findings without a fix, and false positives (for example an advisory for another language binding of the same project, or an affected version range that ends below the version in use), are not suppressed in the scan configuration. The repository holds no suppression file; each such finding is recorded with its reason in the vulnerability record of the release and in the commit text, and is re-evaluated at the next scan.

  • The commit text of a scan-driven change states the plugin version and the finding count per severity.

8.21. AI-Assisted Validation Review (Optional, Prototype)

Status: prototype development. The running application can send one prompt to a local model on an oMLX server and get a second opinion on an invoice’s validation findings; that possibility is the whole feature. The check never gates anything: it runs asynchronously after finalization, and its result — a rating, a summary, issues, and suggestions — is stored as an InvoiceLlmAnalysis record on the invoice, surfaced as an advisory alongside the compliance/KoSIT findings but never consulted by the Finalization Gate.

The check is enabled by installing oMLX, a local OpenAI-compatible inference server (Apple MLX), on a Mac or a mini PC on the operator’s own network (see Dedicated Single-Instance Host, Chapter 7), and pointing MLX_ENDPOINT at it; no endpoint configured means the feature does not exist for that deployment — no UI element, no job option, no connection attempt. A deployment-level switch (littletrotter.features.llm-analysis.enabled) can additionally take the whole path out of service regardless of the endpoint. Because the endpoint runs on the operator’s own network, no invoice data leaves it for this check.

9. Architecture Decisions

This chapter curates the architecturally significant decisions behind the current system. They are drawn from two sources: the project’s running decision logs (architecture decision records and architecture analyses, referenced below by topic) and decisions documented only in code and project practice, which are recorded here for the first time as a proper ADR. Curation favors significance over completeness: this is not every decision ever made, only the ones that shape the architecture a maintainer needs to understand today. The originating documents are left untouched; each ADR below cites its source.

ADR-016 and ADR-019 are merged into ADR-015; both numbers stay reserved and both anchors still resolve, so existing references to either keep working.

ADR-032

ADR-033

The definition of the quote number (prefix, date part, width, reset cycle) is reference data (number range subject QUOTE, default AN-JJJJ-NNNN), replicated to the quote module over the master-data replica; the gapless counter stays in quote.quote_number_range and is drawn in the status-changing transaction. Quote settings stay in schema quote; only their card moves into the settings page, without a runtime toggle

Product owner 2026-10-06; code (kotlin-invoice, kotlin-quote, kotlin-shared)

ADR-033

ADR-034

A missing or altered document of record is resolved on access without a dialog: regenerate when never sent; when sent, restore a byte-identical copy (document store, then the mail component’s sent attachment) before re-rendering; a re-rendered sent invoice is a tagged reproduction that is never dispatched; every step is journalled with reason, source and hashes

Product owner 2026-10-01; code (kotlin-invoice, kotlin-email)

ADR-034

ADR-035

Archiving and retention are the operator’s responsibility: sent invoices (including the XML) are stored in the bookkeeping or archive system, an immutable e-mail archive is recommended, and the application is not a document archive

Product owner decision

ADR-035

ADR-036

The offer life cycle is a single status (DRAFT, REVIEW, SENT, ACCEPTED, INVOICED, REJECTED, CANCELLED); no outcome attribute, no derived expiry, no revision chain; a changed proposal after sending is a rejection with a reason and a new offer

Product owner 2026-10-07; code (kotlin-quote)

ADR-036

ADR-037

Every dispatched mail (invoice and quote, each switchable) is handed over byte-identical to one operator-configured archive address as an additional envelope recipient, triggered by the durable MailSent outbox handler; own status axis NOT_CONFIGURED/DISABLED/PENDING/HANDED_OVER/FAILED; a failed handover never blocks sending, is retried automatically and notified to administrators; the signed archive package stays an evidence artifact, not a retention medium

Product owner decision 2026-10-08

ADR-038

ADR-038

9.2. ADR-001: Money Value Type Instead of Raw BigDecimal

  • Decision: All monetary arithmetic is encapsulated by Money (kotlin-shared), never raw BigDecimal.

  • Consequence: Universal HALF_UP rounding at scale 2, enforced via ArchUnit gates (MoneyArchitectureTest) across all modules.

9.2.1. ADR-001: Money Value Type Instead of Raw BigDecimal

9.2.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.2.1.2. Context

Every monetary amount in an invoicing system is a candidate for rounding drift: repeated BigDecimal arithmetic scattered across call sites, each choosing its own scale and rounding mode independently, reliably produces off-by-a-cent totals that are hard to trace back to a single cause. BigDecimal also does not distinguish "this is a final, quotable amount" from "this is an intermediate value being accumulated before one final rounding" — both look identical at the type level.

9.2.1.3. Decision

All monetary arithmetic goes through a single Money value object (kotlin-shared), never a raw BigDecimal. Money normalizes to two decimals with HALF_UP rounding on its normal operators (plus, minus, times, div, percentage), but also exposes an explicit "exact" precision path (Money.exact, plusExact, times(scale, rounding)) for accumulators that must round only once at the end of a computation. allocate() distributes an amount across weighted parts using largest-remainder rounding so the pieces always sum back to the original amount exactly. Money lives in kotlin-shared specifically so both kotlin-invoice and kotlin-xpdf share the one rounding rule, since kotlin-xpdf depends on kotlin-shared but not on kotlin-invoice.

Each module (kotlin-shared, kotlin-xpdf, kotlin-invoice) carries an ArchUnit test (MoneyArchitectureTest) asserting that no money-named field, constructor parameter, or method return type is a raw BigDecimal — the rule is enforced by the build, not left to code review.

9.2.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • One rounding rule, applied consistently everywhere a monetary amount is produced.

  • The type system distinguishes "final amount" from "intermediate, unrounded accumulator" — Money.of vs. Money.exact — so a caller cannot round early by accident.

  • The ArchUnit guard turns a style convention into a build-breaking regression test.

Negative

  • Money equality is by value (comparing the underlying amount), not by scale, which differs from BigDecimal’s default `equals (1.50 != 1.5) — a caller relying on BigDecimal scale-sensitive equality would be surprised; sameValueAs/compareTo make the intended comparison explicit instead.

  • An extra wrapper type at every domain boundary (persistence converters, DTOs, Jackson (de)serialization) that a plain BigDecimal would not need.

Mitigation

  • MoneyConverter (JPA AttributeConverter) is the one thing kept in kotlin-invoice rather than kotlin-shared, since it needs jakarta.persistence, which kotlin-shared must not depend on.

  • Jackson @JsonCreator/@JsonProperty annotations keep the wire shape byte-identical to a plain {"amount": …​} object, so the wrapper is invisible to API/DTO consumers.

Architecture Decision Record — source: `kotlin-shared/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/shared/Money.kt`

9.3. ADR-002: EN 16931 Business Terms as a Kotlin Enum, Not a Seeded Catalog Table

  • Decision: Full EN 16931 business-term taxonomy is modeled as a Kotlin enum (B2bTerm, SMALLINT BT-code), replacing seeded database catalog tables.

  • Consequence: Compile-time validation and refactoring safety for all e-invoicing terms instead of late runtime database errors.

9.3.1. ADR-002: EN 16931 Business Terms as a Kotlin Enum, Not a Seeded Catalog Table

9.3.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.3.1.2. Context

The original B2B-term catalog (b2b_field_catalog) was a Flyway-seeded database table: every EN 16931 business term (BT-n) a B2bProfile could require was a row, inserted by the baseline seed migration and validated at runtime by a DB CHECK constraint (chk_b2b_field_catalog_binding_target).
