Extending coverage beyond the original ~15 curated terms to the full EN 16931 / Factur-X EXTENDED business-term set (over 200 terms) by adding rows would have meant growing an unreviewable SQL INSERT block and re-deriving the same invariants (binding requires an XPath or a bound property; an enum-kind term requires a value domain) as ad hoc CHECK constraints, with no compile-time verification that a term’s metadata was internally consistent.

9.3.1.3. Decision

The business-term taxonomy is a Kotlin enum, B2bTerm (kotlin-shared), one constant per BT number. Each constant carries its EN 16931 level, field kind, and — where applicable — a binding (BOUND to an existing domain property, or FREE with a documented XPath into the CII export). Two init-time invariant checks (assertB2bTermBindingInvariant, assertB2bTermValueDomainInvariant) enforce these rules in the domain model; the schema carries no CHECK constraint for them. The persisted form is a SMALLINT column holding the term’s official BT number (B2bTerm.code), via a hand-written JPA converter (B2bTermConverter) rather than the enum’s ordinal, because declaration order and BT numbering do not coincide. Display text (short name, definition) is resolved per locale from a shared i18n resource bundle keyed by the term, not embedded in the enum itself, so it is loaded lazily rather than sitting on the heap permanently for every term in every language.

9.3.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • Every term’s metadata is checked by the compiler and by unit tests, not only by a runtime CHECK constraint discovered on write.

  • Coverage of the full ~200-term EN 16931/EXTENDED business-term set is tractable: most new entries are pure reference terms with no capture logic, generated once and reviewed as code.

  • A term already carried by an existing domain property (BOUND) needs no new UI capture widget at all — the existing property is read reflectively at rule-evaluation time.

Negative

  • The enum is large (~250 entries) and a regenerated block is only as trustworthy as the generator and its review, not hand-verified per entry.

  • Persisting by an explicit numeric code rather than ordinal means the converter must be kept in sync by hand if a term’s numbering scheme changes — a mismatch the code round-trip test in B2bTermTest guards against.

Mitigation

  • B2bTerm.BT_151 (the line’s VAT category code) is the one deliberate exception (catalogExcluded): the VAT category is a tax decision that follows from the position’s tax rule, so no buyer profile may require or override it; the exporter derives the value from the tax snapshot. An architectural guard test (B2bProfileMigrationIT) enforces the exclusion rather than leaving it as a convention.

  • A schema-range test (B2bTermSchemaRangeTest, kotlin-invoice) checks the SMALLINT column can hold every allocated code, including the reserved 10000 + n block for BT-X-n extension terms.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-shared/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/shared/b2b/B2bTerm.kt, `kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/reference/converters/B2bTermConverter.kt`

9.4. ADR-003: Reverse Charge Is an Invoice-Level Decision, Not Derived from Line Items

  • Decision: Invoice.reverseCharge is declared at the invoice header, cascading tax rules downward to all invoice items.

  • Consequence: Mixed tax treatments on a single invoice are eliminated by construction; disagreements trigger a blocking finalization gate.

9.4.1. ADR-003: Reverse Charge Is an Invoice-Level Decision, Not Derived from Line Items

9.4.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.4.1.2. Context

Reverse Charge (cross-border EU B2B, VAT category AE) was originally a line-item decision: the user picked a tax rule per line, and Invoice.reverseCharge was purely derived (reverseCharge = hasReverseChargeItems()). Fiscally, though, Reverse Charge is a property of the transaction as a whole, not of individual lines — a mix of Reverse-Charge and standard-rate lines on one invoice is almost always a data-entry error, and the previous design only caught that after the fact, via per-line deviation hints.

9.4.1.3. Decision

Invoice.reverseCharge becomes the leading decision, set by the user at the invoice header, no longer derived from the lines. It can only be enabled when the recipient is Reverse-Charge eligible (EU cross-border, business, verified VAT ID); enabling it retags every existing line to the Reverse-Charge rule (0%, category AE, exemption text snapshotted) with a confirmation dialog naming the affected line count, and disabling it re-applies the standard rule suggestion per line, likewise with confirmation. While the decision is active, the tax-rule selector on line items is locked to the Reverse-Charge rule. A hard gate blocks finalizing the invoice if any line disagrees with the header decision, or if the recipient no longer meets the Reverse-Charge prerequisites (e.g. master data changed after the decision was made) — a validation error citing the offending line numbers, not merely a warning.

The reverse_charge column is written directly by the user’s header decision and propagated to the lines; it is not computed on save.

9.4.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • Eliminates by construction the invoice state that would otherwise require post hoc detection (mixed AE/standard lines on one invoice).

  • Export (XML AE + VATEX-EU-AE, PDF exemption-text hint) is unchanged — it already read off the per-line tax rule, which always agrees with the header.

Negative

  • A profile/master-data change after the Reverse-Charge decision is made (e.g. the recipient’s VAT ID becomes unverified) can retroactively invalidate a decision that was correct when made; this is caught at finalization time, not at the moment the master data changes.

Mitigation

  • The finalization gate re-validates recipient eligibility every time, so a stale decision can never reach a finalized invoice undetected.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/invoice/Invoice.kt (reverseCharge), ReverseChargeConsistency.kt.

9.5. ADR-004: PDF Templates Assigned per B2B Profile, Chosen at Print Time

  • Decision: Superseded; PDF layout is a single product-shipped Typst template, with no per-profile assignment or print-time template choice.

  • Consequence: Layout changes are made in code, not through master data.

9.5.1. ADR-004: PDF Templates Assigned per B2B Profile, Chosen at Print Time

9.5.1.1. Status

Superseded by ADR-030

9.5.1.2. Context

The product ships one Typst template and no user-assignable PDF templates; per-profile template selection and the print-time template choice do not exist. The original problem and decision are kept below as the record this ADR supersedes.

Original context

PDF template management already existed (upload with compile-check via the kotlin-xpdf worker, display name, a GERMAN/INTERNATIONAL format axis, fallback to the shipped invoice-report.jrxml), but template assignment was keyed to the invoice issuer (issuer profile), one template per format, and the print flow offered no choice — resolution happened silently in the DTO mapper. Different B2B invoice recipients legitimately need different layouts (e.g. a craftsman-oriented invoice showing cost-center/order/project references and customer article numbers), which an issuer-keyed, no-choice model could not express.

9.5.1.3. Decision

Templates are additionally assignable to a B2bProfile (nullable FK on pdf_template; NULL stays a general-purpose template usable by any profile), managed in the existing template administration screen. The issuer-keyed assignment remains as the fallback when the recipient’s profile carries no specific template. At print time, the export panel offers a choice from exactly the set "templates of the recipient’s profile, plus general templates, plus the shipped default" — a per-print-job choice that overrides the silent resolution. The Jasper data context was extended per line (BT-155/BT-156 seller/buyer item numbers, line service period) and per header (cost center BT-19, purchase order BT-13, project BT-11, contract BT-12, internal order BT-18) so templates can render these values. A shipped "Nekar craftsman invoice" template exercises the full field set and is seeded against the one remaining demo profile ("Nekar" — the generic "B2B-Standard" demo profile was retired in the same cleanup).

9.5.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • Template choice follows the actual business relationship (the invoice recipient’s profile), not only the invoice issuer issuing the invoice.

  • The print-time override means a one-off exception does not require changing master data first.

Negative

  • Three resolution layers now exist (explicit print-time choice, profile assignment, issuer fallback) instead of one silent lookup — more surface for an Office Manager to reason about when a printed invoice does not look as expected.

Mitigation

  • The choice is always a bounded, explicit set shown in the UI (never a hidden precedence the Office Manager has to remember), and a NULL profile assignment keeps behaving exactly as a general-purpose template always did.

Architecture Decision Record — source: pdf_template.b2b_profile_id, the template administration view and export panel in kotlin-invoice, InvoiceDtoMapper.

9.6. ADR-005: Displayed System Time Is Always German Local Time

  • Decision: All display timestamps use Europe/Berlin as default via a centralized GermanTime constant, while technical persistence and messaging remain in UTC.

  • Consequence: Deterministic date/time rendering across all time zones and container environments, with capability for multi-timezone branch offices in mid-sized enterprises.

9.6.1. ADR-005: Displayed System Time Is Always German Local Time

9.6.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.6.1.2. Context

The application’s containers run without an explicit TZ, i.e. on UTC. Every display that used ZoneId.systemDefault() or a zone-less LocalDateTime.now() (print-job timestamps, the message center, the validation report’s "checked at" timestamp, the status-tile dialog, backup timestamps) therefore rendered UTC — two hours off from German local time during summer (CEST).

9.6.1.3. Decision

The application always displays German local time (Europe/Berlin, DST-aware), as a product property rather than a deployment setting: Little Trotter X-Invoice is a German e-invoicing product, and its primary audience reads timestamps in German local time regardless of what timezone the host happens to run in. This is implemented in two layers: a central constant, GermanTime.ZONE = ZoneId.of("Europe/Berlin") (kotlin-shared), which every display call site and every zone-less LocalDateTime.now() write path (including the two analyzedAt persistence writes) uses instead of ZoneId.systemDefault(); and, as a supporting deployment measure, TZ: Europe/Berlin set on the application’s own containers in the root compose file, so log timestamps also read German local time. Persisted timestamps stored as OffsetDateTime/Instant are untouched — they already carry their own offset and remain correct as points in time; only the conversion used for display is German local time.

9.6.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • Display timestamps are correct and consistent regardless of the host’s system timezone or container TZ configuration — the code-level conversion does not depend on the deployment measure to be correct.

  • A single named constant makes every display call site auditable by search, rather than each one independently deciding which zone to use.

Negative

  • A German-only default time-display policy requires explicit configuration or per-tenant/branch settings when serving users outside Germany’s timezone in a multi-region deployment.

Mitigation

  • The core data layer, event streams, and object storage persist strictly in UTC (ADR-029). With Europe/Berlin serving as the out-of-the-box default, localized presentation can be parameterized per branch or user profile to support multi-timezone operations across distributed branch offices (Niederlassungen) for mid-sized enterprises (Mittelstand).

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-shared GermanTime, root docker-compose.yml (TZ: Europe/Berlin).

9.7. ADR-006: JPA Access Pattern Hardening (LIKE Escaping, Stable Pagination, Explicit Fetches)

  • Decision: Mandatory wildcard escaping for LIKE queries, deterministic tiebreakers in paginated queries, and explicit entity fetch joins.

  • Consequence: Elimination of query injection vectors, unstable page shifts, and Hibernate lazy initialization surprises.

9.7.1. ADR-006: JPA Access Pattern Hardening (LIKE Escaping, Stable Pagination, Explicit Fetches)

9.7.1.1. Status

Accepted, implemented

9.7.1.2. Context

A code review of the Panache repository layer found four JPA access patterns worth fixing, ranked by severity: (1) the invoice overview’s LIKE filters built raw %text% patterns without ESCAPE, so a literal _/% in a search term matched unintended rows — a correctness bug, not a security issue, since values were always bound as parameters; (2) several paginated queries had no ORDER BY, or an ambiguous one (date only, no id tiebreaker), so PostgreSQL could return duplicate or missing rows across pages; (3) findByIdWithItems did not actually fetch items despite its name, leaving every one of eleven call sites to rely on lazy loading inside an open transaction; (4) recipient address loading forced initialization per row (addresses.size), producing up to 61 SELECTs for a 60-row page.

9.7.1.3. Decision

  • Every LIKE clause (production and native SQL) uses a shared likePattern() helper plus an explicit ESCAPE '\' clause; a regression test pins the fixed filter behavior, and a guard test scans production Kotlin for a LIKE :param occurrence without a matching ESCAPE on the same line.

  • Every paginated query gets id as an explicit tiebreaker (or a full ORDER BY where none existed), verified by a test with two rows sharing a sort key across a page boundary.

  • findByIdWithItems performs a LEFT JOIN FETCH i.items (with SELECT DISTINCT, the same shape findInvoiceByItemId uses), so the method name is true and none of the eleven call sites needs to change.

  • Recipient address loading keeps the force-init loop, which defines when addresses load, because Quarkus’s Hibernate ORM extension defaults fetch.batch-size to 16 — measured to keep a 40-recipient page under 10 JDBC statements rather than the 61 a per-row load would need; a config regression test pins the setting.

9.7.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • Search results and pagination are provably correct under a repeatable test, not just "usually right in practice."

  • The measured batch-fetch behavior shows the N+1 problem is bounded, so no two-stage-loading refactor is needed.

Negative

  • The LIKE/ESCAPE guard test is a lightweight source scan, not a full static-analysis rule — it can be defeated by an unusual formatting of the offending line.

Mitigation

  • None further planned; the guard test is judged sufficient given the small number of query call sites in this codebase.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/infrastructure/persistence/PanacheInvoiceRepository.kt, BasePanacheRepository.kt (likePattern), PanacheRecipientRepository.kt.

9.8. ADR-007: RustFS as the S3-Compatible Object Store, Including on Small-Budget Hardware

  • Decision: RustFS (S3-compatible) serves as the standard object store for receipts and generated documents across all hardware profiles.

  • Consequence: Unified S3 API from local dev to mini-PC and cloud, replacing fragmented local filesystem storage.

9.8.1. ADR-007: RustFS as the S3-Compatible Object Store, Including on Small-Budget Hardware

9.8.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.8.1.2. Context

Receipts and generated PDF/XML documents need durable, content-addressable, S3-compatible object storage, independent of the relational database. The project’s target deployment is a small Linux server on small-budget hardware, not a cloud cluster, which puts memory and CPU footprint on equal footing with API compatibility when choosing an object-store implementation.

9.8.1.3. Decision

RustFS, an S3-compatible object store, is used as the receipt/document object storage backend (docker-compose.yml service rustfs), addressed through the standard S3 API from both kotlin-invoice and kotlin-xpdf. This is a deliberate, standing choice, not a placeholder: it remains the storage backend on the small-budget deployment target as well as in development, and it is not to be replaced by a plain filesystem mount even where that would look like a simpler option for a single-operator deployment — S3-compatible semantics (content addressing, checksum verification, backup tooling built around pg_dump-style object export) are treated as an architectural property of the system, not an implementation detail to simplify away.

9.8.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • One API (S3) for object storage across every deployment target, from a developer laptop to the small-budget mini PC deployment target.

  • Checksum-based duplicate detection for receipts composes naturally with content-addressable storage.

Negative

  • An additional stateful service (with its own data volume, rustfs-data) to operate and back up, compared to writing files directly to a mounted filesystem path.

Mitigation

  • Backup (planned): the object store’s data is backed up alongside PostgreSQL by the same encrypted backup and restore flow, so the added operational surface adds no second backup concept.

Architecture Decision Record — source: docker-compose.yml (rustfs service)

9.9. ADR-008: Small NATS Envelopes, Valkey for Text, RustFS for Binaries

  • Decision: NATS carries only lightweight reference envelopes. Binary documents live in RustFS; cached text payloads live in Valkey.

  • Consequence: Constant message size and low queue memory footprint independent of invoice document payload sizes.

9.9.1. ADR-008: Small NATS Envelopes, Valkey for Text, RustFS for Binaries

9.9.1.1. Status

Accepted, implemented

9.9.1.2. Context

PDF/XML generation is asynchronous, coordinated between kotlin-invoice and kotlin-xpdf over NATS JetStream. Putting full payloads (KoSIT validation reports, rendered documents) directly on the message bus would make NATS a de facto document store, working against JetStream’s design as a message broker and bloating stream storage/ack-pending accounting with payloads the broker never needs to interpret.

9.9.1.3. Decision

NATS carries only small, typed envelope structures — GenerationJobResultRefs (kotlin-shared) for job results — never the documents or reports themselves. Text payloads too large or too transient for a message (KoSIT validation reports) are exchanged through Valkey via a PayloadExchange port, addressed by a reference key the envelope carries. Binary artifacts (rendered PDFs, generated XML) are written to RustFS/S3 and referenced by object key. A missing or expired Valkey entry is treated as a documented degrade path (a null report, logged as a warning), not a processing failure, since Valkey is a cache/exchange, not the system of record.

9.9.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • NATS stream storage and maxAckPending stay proportional to job count, not to document size.

  • The envelope’s typed shape (GenerationJobResultRefs) gives every reader/writer one place that lists the known fields, instead of a bare Map<String, String> with magic keys.

Negative

  • Three systems (NATS, Valkey, RustFS) must all be healthy for a job to complete cleanly, instead of one.

Mitigation

  • Each store’s failure mode is handled independently and explicitly at the point of use (Valkey miss degrades to a warning + null; RustFS/NATS failures propagate through the existing retry/DLQ path — see the messaging retry and idempotency behavior in Messaging and Event-Driven Architecture of Cross-cutting Concepts), rather than a single combined failure mode that is harder to diagnose.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-shared/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/shared/GenerationJobResultRefs.kt, kotlin-invoice/…​/infrastructure/messaging/GenerationJobResultConsumer.kt (PayloadExchange), kotlin-xpdf/…​/infrastructure/messaging/ResultPublisher.kt.

9.10. ADR-009: Editions Abolished — One Component, Not a Community/Commercial Split

  • Decision: Abolish community/commercial edition splitting in favor of a single unified kotlin-invoice codebase.

  • Consequence: Single build artifact, zero feature-toggle branch drift, and unified test coverage.

9.10.1. ADR-009: Editions Abolished — One Component, Not a Community/Commercial Split

9.10.1.1. Status

Accepted, implemented

9.10.1.2. Context

A planned community/private split would have required an edition mechanism: a feature gate, SPI extension points (InvoiceFormModule/PartyFormExtension), and a thin per-edition runnable/Dockerfile, with edition-bound identifiers threaded through the codebase. "Haflinger" is a release name, not a product-line identifier.

9.10.1.3. Decision

The project is a single component with a single build artifact; it carries no edition mechanism, and the modules such a mechanism would gate live directly in kotlin-invoice. No haflinger-branded identifier exists in the code; i18n keys use the invoiceModule.* prefix. "Haflinger" appears only as a display-only release/version-name label in the System area.

9.10.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • One build, one deployment artifact, one set of tests — no edition-conditional branches to keep in sync or accidentally diverge.

  • Removes an entire class of "which edition is this feature in" questions from code review.

Negative

  • A genuinely restricted/limited build, if ever needed, has to be built from scratch rather than resuming a paused mechanism.

Mitigation

  • None needed; a future split, if ever required, is a new decision made on its own merits, not a revival of the abolished mechanism.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice i18n bundle key prefix invoiceModule.*.

9.11. ADR-010: Fault Tolerance Is Hand-Implemented, Not Framework-Driven

  • Decision: Hand-crafted retry, dead-letter, and heartbeat mechanics instead of heavy general-purpose fault-tolerance frameworks.

  • Consequence: Minimal dependency footprint and transparent, inspectable queue error semantics.

9.11.1. ADR-010: Fault Tolerance Is Hand-Implemented, Not Framework-Driven

9.11.1.1. Status

Accepted, implemented

9.11.1.2. Context

A general-purpose fault-tolerance extension in the dependency tree, applied without being exercised anywhere meaningful, is worse than not having one: no annotation actually matters for the application’s failure modes, yet it adds build size and a plausible-looking-but-inert annotation surface that a future contributor might reasonably assume is doing something.

9.11.1.3. Decision

The project carries no general-purpose fault-tolerance framework: smallrye-fault-tolerance and its rhino scripting dependency are absent from the dependency tree. Fault tolerance is not abandoned as a concern — retry-with-backoff and dead-lettering are hand-implemented where they matter today (the NATS result consumer’s retry loop and DLQ routing, see Messaging and Event-Driven Architecture) — but a general-purpose framework is added only when a specific critical component needs it, rather than kept installed "just in case."

9.11.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • Smaller dependency footprint; no dead annotation surface implying protection that is not actually configured anywhere.

  • The existing hand-rolled retry/DLQ logic is easier to audit precisely because it is the only fault-tolerance mechanism in the codebase, not one of several overlapping ones.

Negative

  • Reintroducing structured fault tolerance (circuit breakers, bulkheads) for a genuinely critical component later means adding the dependency back and instrumenting it from scratch, rather than un-commenting existing annotations.

Mitigation

  • None needed before a concrete need arises; the decision is deliberately revisit-when-needed rather than speculative.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/infrastructure/messaging/GenerationJobResultConsumer.kt.

9.12. ADR-011: Flyway Baseline Edited In Place Until a Live Instance Exists

  • Decision: Schema changes during greenfield development directly update V1baseline_schema.sql and V2baseline_seed.sql.

  • Consequence: Zero migration sprawl or temporary patch scripts prior to public production release.

9.12.1. ADR-011: Flyway Baseline Edited In Place Until a Live Instance Exists

9.12.1.1. Status

Accepted, 2026-09-16

9.12.1.2. Context

Flyway migrations are normally append-only: every schema change is a new versioned file, because a migration already applied against a live database can never be safely edited afterward. But while no live/production instance of Little Trotter X-Invoice exists yet, treating every schema iteration during active greenfield development as a permanent, unchangeable migration produces a long chain of throwaway V3, V4, …​ files that exist only to correct the previous one, adding noise without adding safety.

9.12.1.3. Decision

The PO reaffirmed this rule on 2026-09-16. The two baseline files, V1baseline_schema.sql (DDL) and V2baseline_seed.sql (seed data), are edited directly in place for as long as no live instance exists. A schema change during this phase is a diff to V1/V2, not a new V3. The directory hygiene test enforces exactly these two files. Mail projections, feature settings, currency-aware statistics, maintenance ownership and travel-time rate state are defined directly in V1. V2 contains the final catalogue names, feature defaults and supplied PDF template; it does not patch previous seed values. The incremental workflow of ADR-017 is deferred until upgrades are needed. This rule ends the moment a real deployment’s data must survive an upgrade — at that point, the direct-edit rule stops applying and normal append-only Flyway versioning takes over. During greenfield development, baseline edits require a fresh disposable database because Flyway detects changed checksums. This is not authorization to reset an existing instance: tests provision isolated empty databases and use only fixtures from the single synthetic datagen default when business data is needed.

9.12.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • No accumulation of migration files whose entire content is "undo the mistake from two commits ago" during a phase where no data durability is at stake yet.

  • The schema and seed data stay legible as a single current-state file each, rather than requiring a reader to replay a chain of increments to understand the current shape.

Negative

  • Every baseline edit invalidates locally applied migration checksums, requiring a development database volume recreate — a manual step that is easy to forget and shows up as a confusing Flyway checksum-mismatch error if missed.

  • The rule only works because no live instance exists; it is not a general Flyway pattern and must not be copied into a context with real data.

Mitigation

  • The rule is explicitly time-boxed ("until a live instance exists") rather than a permanent project convention, so it cannot be mistaken for standard practice once a real deployment exists.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/resources/db/migration/ddl/V1baseline_schema.sql, kotlin-invoice/src/main/resources/db/migration/seed/V2baseline_seed.sql.

9.13. ADR-012: Docker Engine, Not Podman, for Both Development and the Production Host

  • Decision: Standardize on Docker Engine for local development and target production servers.

  • Consequence: Consistent Compose engine semantics across macOS and Linux, avoiding daemonless rootless edge-case bugs.

9.13.1. ADR-012: Docker Engine, Not Podman, for Both Development and the Production Host

9.13.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.13.1.2. Context

Podman is a candidate for the dedicated production Linux host: daemonless, rootless by default, with systemd/Quadlet unit integration and journald logging — properties that suit a small, single-purpose server well in principle. An evaluation kit was built and reverted before completion; it defined three hard acceptance criteria: full compose-file fidelity under Podman (including the facturx profile toggle and healthcheck-based depends_on), rootless port 443 for Caddy, and operational parity (admin-tools tunnels, the security-scan tooling against a Podman socket). Development machines were never in scope for a switch — macOS Docker Desktop remains free under the project’s usage threshold and gives the fastest local builds via BuildKit.

9.13.1.3. Decision

The project stays on Docker Engine for both development and the production host. The project’s position is plainly "Docker, not Podman." No Quadlet units or Podman-specific tooling ship in the repository, and no Quadlet/podman files exist under admin-tools/ or docker/. The host runs Docker Engine in rootless mode, retaining part of the security benefit that motivated the evaluation without the runtime switch.

9.13.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • One container runtime end to end — the same docker-compose.yml and overlay files behave identically in development and on the host, with no Podman-compose semantic-diff risk to monitor.

  • Existing admin-tools (backup, deployment, monitoring) that assume a Docker socket need no dual-runtime abstraction layer.

Negative

  • Foregoes Podman’s daemonless/rootless-by-default properties on the production host; rootless Docker Engine remains the operating model there instead.

Mitigation

  • Rootless Docker Engine on the host keeps part of the security benefit the evaluation was after, without a second container runtime to maintain.

Architecture Decision Record — source: root `docker-compose.yml`

9.14. ADR-013: Authentik Gets Its Own Database in the Same PostgreSQL Instance

  • Decision: Authentik runs with an isolated PostgreSQL database within the shared cluster.

  • Consequence: Clean operational boundary: invoice database restores never overwrite or roll back user credentials.

9.14.1. ADR-013: Authentik Gets Its Own Database in the Same PostgreSQL Instance

9.14.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.14.1.2. Context

The optional Authentik-based OIDC overlay needs a persistence decision among three real options: a second PostgreSQL instance, a separate database within the existing instance, or a separate schema within the existing, shared database. Measurement against the running stack shows the fixed cost of an additional database in the same instance is negligible — a few megabytes of disk and effectively no additional RAM — since the expensive resources (shared_buffers, WAL, background/backend processes) are per instance, not per database.

9.14.1.3. Decision

Authentik uses its own database, little-trotter-auth, inside the same PostgreSQL instance as the invoicing database, plus its own schema (authentik) within that database. A second instance is rejected: it would add 150–250 MB of additional RAM and a second WAL stream for no measurable benefit here. A shared schema in the invoicing database is rejected because it would mix invoice and identity data in one pg_dump and weaken role separation to search_path alone. The decisive factor is independent backup/restore: resetting the invoicing database must never take every login with it.

Authentik itself is not part of the default deployment: the application does its own OIDC (authorization code flow with PKCE) rather than depending on Authentik as a forward-auth gateway, so authentik/auth-redis live in an optional overlay (docker/compose/auth.yml) rather than the default docker-compose.yml. The database-separation decision above applies unchanged to any instance that enables that overlay.

9.14.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • Two independent pg_dump streams from one server: an invoicing-database restore cannot destroy Authentik logins, and vice versa.

  • Database-level role separation: the Authentik role has no access to the invoicing database.

Negative

  • A separate schema and a separate database is arguably redundant defense-in-depth — the non-default schema name adds friction to pg_restore without adding real isolation beyond what the separate database already provides.

Mitigation

  • The schema is kept as-is rather than migrated to public, since a schema rename would require reinitializing Authentik — not worth the risk for a cosmetic simplification.

Architecture Decision Record — source: docker/compose/auth.yml, docker/postgres/init-authentik.sh.

9.15. ADR-014: Invoice Document Size Budget — Early Guard Plus Export Backstop

  • Decision: Strict budget of 19 receipts max, 1 MiB per receipt, and 20 MiB total per invoice.

  • Consequence: Enforced early at UI upload and backstopped at rendering, guaranteeing compliance with mail/e-invoice size limits.

9.15.1. ADR-014: Invoice Document Size Budget — Early Guard Plus Export Backstop

9.15.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.15.1.2. Context

A guard that lives only in the export worker (kotlin-xpdf) refuses an annex above 20 MiB of raw receipt bytes, but only after the user has already assembled the whole invoice and sent it for generation. A second, unrelated limit that capped receipts per position or per invoice at a fixed count would have no size-related reason. Recipients' mail gateways and the archiving path bound how large a legally transmitted invoice may be (Architecture Constraints), so the size limit is an architecture concern, not an implementation detail of one worker.

9.15.1.3. Decision

One rule is enforced in four places: upload, receipt-assignment linking, document generation and export. The budget is checked early, at receipt assignment, before the invoice ever reaches the export worker; document-generation re-checks it once more before a GenerationJob is even created; and the export worker keeps its own backstop pulling the same constants from the shared policy rather than a locally duplicated number. There is no per-target count cap; the one rule is 19 distinct receipts per invoice, counted across positions and invoice level together and deduplicated by object key. Of the two things a receipt set can violate, only the count refuses a write at linking time — an estimated size above 20 MiB warns instead, since a single oversized receipt is fixed by re-normalizing it in the pool, not by picking different receipts.

The numbers are one source of truth (ReceiptSizePolicy, kotlin-shared, de.littletrotter.shared.receipt), chosen to add up exactly: 19 receipts, each normalized at upload towards TARGET_RECEIPT_SIZE (768 KiB) and Base64-encoded for the estimate (inflating to exactly 1 MiB), plus a 1 MiB reserve for the invoice document itself, equals 20 MiB. A user may still decline reduction up to MAX_UNREDUCED_RECEIPT_SIZE (1 MiB); nothing above MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE (10 MiB) is accepted at all, reduction or not. The subdomain lives in kotlin-shared for the same reason as Money (ADR-001): kotlin-xpdf depends on kotlin-shared but not on kotlin-invoice, so only kotlin-shared lets the application, the UI and the export worker share one rule instead of separate copies of the same numbers.

Measuring only the finished artifact was rejected: it would give the user no early hint, since the failure would surface only after generation is attempted. Keeping both the old count cap and the new size rule was rejected too: a receipt set could be refused by the count cap while comfortably inside the size budget, or vice versa, with no shared vocabulary to explain either refusal. ADR-032 narrows what the estimate has to account for: the container holds exactly one copy of each receipt, with no second or third embedding on top of the Base64 copy — see that record for the reasoning.

9.15.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • One user-visible rule instead of two unrelated ones: "19 distinct receipts, 20 MiB total" is the whole story, stated at capture time rather than discovered at export.

  • A unit test in kotlin-shared pins the invariant 19 × TARGET_RECEIPT_SIZE.base64Encoded() + INVOICE_DOCUMENT_RESERVE == MAX_INVOICE_DOCUMENT_SIZE, and the export worker’s own configuration default is asserted equal to ReceiptSizePolicy.MAX_INVOICE_DOCUMENT_SIZE rather than carrying its own copy.

  • Deduplication by object key matches what the export path already does, so the budget never refuses a receipt set the exporter would in fact handle fine.

  • Upload-time normalization (ADR-032) means most receipts land well under the 768 KiB worst case the invariant is built on, so the estimate is close to the produced container’s actual size rather than a deliberately optimistic lower bound.

Negative

  • The early estimate still is not a byte-exact measurement of the produced file (Cross-cutting Concepts): reserve-only overhead (invoice body, surrounding XML text, embedded fonts) is a fixed allowance, not computed per invoice.

  • Four enforcement points mean four places whose numbers must be kept aligned by test, rather than one place enforcing the rule once.

Mitigation

  • The estimator is one small, replaceable component (InvoiceDocumentSizeEstimator); when a more accurate projection is worth having, it replaces that component alone, and the application, UI and export worker do not change.

  • The export worker keeps its own hard check as the backstop, so an undercounted estimate still cannot ship an oversized document.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-shared/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/shared/receipt/ (DocumentSize, ReceiptSizePolicy, ReceiptSize, InvoiceDocumentSizeEstimator, Base64WithReserveEstimator, InvoiceDocumentBudget), kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/invoice/ (ReceiptUploadService, ReceiptLinkService, ReceiptBudgetService, GenerationJobCreationService), kotlin-xpdf/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/xpdf/impl/PdfExportServiceImpl.kt.

9.16. ADR-015: The Mail Component Is a Self-Contained System

  • Decision: E-mail dispatch is an isolated bounded context (little-trotter-email) with separate schema, queue contract, and forward-auth identity.

  • Consequence: Invoicing remains fully functional without email container; zero SMTP credentials inside core application.

9.16.1. ADR-015: The Mail Component Is a Self-Contained System

9.16.1.1. Status

Accepted, implemented

9.16.1.2. Context

Invoices are generated and stored by the invoicing application; sending them by e-mail is a capability an instance can enable or leave off entirely, carrying none of it when unused — no container, table, stream, secret or menu entry. The capability is substantial: mail templates with a placeholder catalog, an append-only send journal, SMTP submission, and a signed archive package per sent mail, since an outgoing invoice mail is evidence for the tax office. None of that is invoicing domain knowledge, and it has its own lifecycle: a template change, an SMTP provider migration or an archive-format fix has no reason to redeploy the invoicing application, or vice versa.

9.16.1.3. Decision

The mail component is a self-contained system next to the invoicing application: it owns its own data, is its own deployment unit with its own release cycle, and stays useful — and independently deployable — without the other. Integration happens only at the edges, over a small, explicit contract and asynchronous events, never through shared internals or a shared database.

Bounded context and deployment

The component is a separate bounded context, in an Invoice Issuer/Invoice Recipient relationship with the invoicing context, developed as its own Maven module (kotlin-email) and deployed as its own image, with its own object-storage bucket and access key, its own two NATS streams, its own Valkey database index and its own archive signing key. It reuses the instance’s shared infrastructure services but shares no data with the invoicing stack, and is enabled per instance by one switch, off by default. It owns what it sends; the invoicing application keeps only a projection ("this document was sent"), fed by a result event that stays true even if the component is later decommissioned.

Amendment 2026-09-25: one repository, two self-contained systems

The component’s source moved from its own repository (little-trotter-email) into this repository as the Maven module kotlin-email; runtime and release independence are unchanged. Self-containment is a property of runtime, data and release, not of the repository. The component therefore lives in this repository as the module kotlin-email while every runtime boundary of this decision stays in force: its own image, schema, Flyway directory and roles, bucket and key pair, NATS streams, Valkey index, archive signing key and the single LT_MAIL_ENABLED switch, off by default.

Reasons: one rule set (a single detekt.yml, AGENTS.md, ArchUnit patterns and build lock) keeps both systems at the same quality bar without copying and drift checks; the published-language contract in kotlin-mail-contract changes atomically with both of its adapters, with both sides' contract tests in one build; one repository means one project memory and one access grant for agents.

The boundary is enforced by the build, not by repository separation:

  • kotlin-email depends on kotlin-mail-contract and kotlin-shared only, never on kotlin-invoice, kotlin-xpdf or kotlin-facturx (Maven), and neither side imports the other’s packages (ArchUnit MailModuleBoundaryTest with a negative specimen, and its counterpart in ArchitectureRulesTest).

  • The contract package de.littletrotter.shared.mail is the only shared contract and lives in its own module kotlin-mail-contract — the dedicated contract artifact anticipated by ADR-016. Changes stay additive, versioned by schema version, and never shared domain logic. Each side translates the contract in its own adapter (acl package); neither domain nor application code of either side sees it.

  • The component is built, tested and published as its own image (email in the image pipeline); a component release never requires redeploying the invoicing application, and vice versa. Own release tags email-v*, analogous to the shared-v* releases of kotlin-shared, are planned.

Amendment 2026-10-02: UI in the invoice app, component headless

The mail views (compose, journal, templates) moved from the component into the invoicing application; kotlin-email serves only its REST API /mail/api/* and the NATS adapters.

Reasons: the component’s own Vaadin UI was a second web application with its own session, theme, translations and test stack, embedded into the invoicing UI as a frame; every change to navigation, permissions or wording had to be made twice, and the frame forced the forward_auth edge and a shared edge secret. One UI in the invoicing application uses its existing OIDC login, AppPermission model, i18n bundles and Karibu tests, while the component keeps owning data, rules and the send path.

Decision details:

  • The invoicing application hosts the routes mail/compose/:draftId, mail/journal and mail/templates, gated by the permissions MAIL_COMPOSE_AND_SEND, MAIL_JOURNAL_VIEW and MAIL_TEMPLATE_EDIT in RouteAccess. The views depend only on narrow input ports in application.mail.port.inbound; HTTP adapters in infrastructure.mail implement the outbound ports and translate contract types in acl.

  • The component authenticates callers by a bearer token and the forwarded X-Lt-User and X-Lt-Groups identity headers, and checks the groups again server-side (MailEdgeIdentityMechanism, MailRoleProvider). It stays unreachable from outside: the edge answers 404 for /mail/*. There is no forward_auth and no edge secret; the GET /internal/auth/verify hop is no longer part of the mail path.

  • The compose draft is consumed only when the send is queued, so an abandoned or failed attempt leaves the draft reusable.

  • email-ui, Vaadin and the browser-driven integration tests are removed from kotlin-email; email-e2e-tests drives the REST API only.

The runtime boundaries of this decision (own image, schema, bucket, streams, switch) are unchanged.

Published-language contract

The two systems share a published language and nothing else: data classes, enums, constants and one placeholder codec — no domain logic, no entities, no service interface of either side. Each side maps the contract into its own local model at its own adapter, so neither side’s internal model leaks across the boundary. The three document-family enums (invoicing’s billed format, the contract’s crossing value, the component’s template family) stay separate on purpose: unifying them would couple the two release cycles, since a value one side does not know is an anti-corruption-layer decision, not a compile error.

Because the two sides deploy independently, every request and event carries a schema version, and unknown fields are ignored on read; enum values travel by name, never by ordinal. The contract package de.littletrotter.shared.mail lives in its own Maven module, kotlin-mail-contract, so the contract changes only together with both of its adapters and stays free of any other shared code.

Schema and delegated identity

The component’s tables live in a schema of their own inside the invoicing application’s existing PostgreSQL database — not a database of its own, and not a schema shared with the invoicing tables via search_path. It keeps its own Flyway migration directory, its own history table, and its own migrator and application roles granted on that schema and nothing else; its migrations manage that schema exclusively. One database to back up, restore and monitor keeps the mail journal restorable to the same point in time as the invoices it documents.

The component has no identity provider, user store or login of its own, and is not reachable from outside the machine. The invoicing application is its authentication gate: the reverse proxy answers 404 for /mail/*, and the invoicing application is the only caller: it authenticates the user with its own OIDC session and calls the component’s REST API with a bearer token and the forwarded X-Lt-User/X-Lt-Groups headers (see the 2026-10-02 amendment). Its one load-bearing assumption is that the component is reachable only from the invoicing application’s network.

Transaction boundary: one journal step, never the whole dispatch

A database transaction in the component is exactly one journal step. @Transactional therefore sits on the repository implementations in service.adapter.persistence — each method performs one state function (mail.mark_mail_journal_*) or one insert — and nowhere else on the adapter side; the application layer opens no transaction of its own. This is a deliberate departure from the invoicing application’s rule that transactions are declared in the application layer, for a reason: a dispatch is not one atomic unit of work but a chain of steps across three stores, and pulling S3 and NATS into one database transaction would only pretend to make them atomic.

The chain is safe without a distributed transaction because every non-database step is idempotent and verifiable: the journal row commits (CREATED), then authored.json is written to S3 under a key derived from the journal id with canonical content, then the row commits QUEUED together with that object’s SHA-256, then the envelope is published; the consumer reads the state from the row, never from the message, verifies the object against the anchored hash, and claims the single SMTP attempt by a compare-and-set on the row (QUEUED → SENDING). A redelivered envelope on a terminal row is a no-op, a stalled QUEUED row is republished by the sweeper, an orphaned S3 object (process death between the write and QUEUED) costs storage but never correctness, and a missing app.now on a connection fails the step loudly instead of stamping the server clock. MailTransactionBoundaryTest (ArchUnit, email-app) enforces that only classes implementing an email-core repository port may open a transaction on the adapter side.

9.16.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • An instance without the capability carries nothing of it — no container, schema, bucket, stream, Valkey index, route, secret or backup entry; the invoicing application’s image is identical either way.

  • Independent release cycles and failure domains for the send path, one database and one identity to administer, and a contract that keeps neither side’s persisted values or deployment timing constraining the other’s.

Negative

  • A cross-context foreign key from a mail row to an invoice row is physically possible; the boundary is enforced by grants and review, not by the database engine.

  • The mail views are unusable without the invoicing application: they are part of it, so if it is down no mail can be composed or inspected even though the component itself is healthy.

  • A change to the component’s REST API requires a matching change to the invoicing application’s HTTP adapters; the frozen contract package and schema versions limit that coupling.

Mitigation

  • Everything the capability adds is gated on one switch — container, schema, bucket, streams, route, secrets, backup set — so "enabled" and "disabled" are two states, not a matrix of half-configured ones.

  • The schema’s exclusivity and the internal-only requirement are each a rule with a single owner (the migration directory; the reverse-proxy configuration), stated in code, not left implicit.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-email, kotlin-mail-contract/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/shared/mail/, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/infrastructure/mail/, docker/caddy/Caddyfile.

9.17. ADR-017: Flyway Versions Are One Global Sequence Across ddl/ and seed/, Not Two Blocks

  • Status: Superseded by PO decision 2026-09-16 (maintain V1 DDL and V2 seed directly during greenfield phase).

9.17.1. ADR-017: Flyway Versions Are One Global Sequence Across ddl/ and seed/, Not Two Blocks

9.17.1.1. Status

Superseded by ADR-011, 2026-09-16

9.17.1.2. Context

Superseded for greenfield development by the PO decision of 2026-09-16 and ADR-011. The historical rationale below applies when incremental upgrades become necessary; it does not authorize additional migration files during the current greenfield phase. The current hygiene guard requires exactly ddl/V1baseline_schema.sql and seed/V2baseline_seed.sql.

Flyway is configured with two locations, db/migration/ddl and db/migration/seed (quarkus.flyway.locations), and orders migrations by version across all of them. Folder membership is a filing convention of this repository — schema changes here, reference/seed data there — and means nothing to Flyway itself.

MigrationDirectoryHygieneTest guards that filing. Alongside the guarantees it exists for, it also asserted something stronger that nobody had decided: that ddl/ is a gap-free run V1..Vn and that seed/ continues at Vn+1. That is a block layout — every DDL migration before every seed migration — and it broke the first time a new DDL migration had to be added after both folders already held files: the block rule demanded the next ddl/ file reuse a version seed/ already used, and satisfying it would mean renumbering an already-applied seed migration, i.e. rewriting the filename, checksum and history row of a file already applied wherever an instance runs. The property being defended is not one Flyway has: an interleaved sequence across the two folders is a perfectly ordinary one for it.

9.17.1.3. Decision

The guard asserts the single global sequence and nothing about folder order: the union of ddl/ and seed/ is a gap-free run V1..Vn starting at 1, no version number is used twice within a folder or shared between the folders, and every filename matches V<digits>__<description>.sql. Together these still mean every version exists exactly once, globally, with no gap a later migration could fill out of order and no slot that could be applied twice — the guarantees the guard was written for.

The guard deliberately no longer asserts that every ddl/ version precedes every seed/ version. Migrations interleave (ddl/V1, seed/V2, ddl/V3, …): a change takes the next free number and lives in the folder its content belongs to.

Renumbering the seed migrations on every future DDL migration is rejected: it rewrites files Flyway has already applied and the cost recurs forever, to preserve a property Flyway does not have. Folding new columns into the frozen baseline instead of adding a migration is rejected as out of scope: direct baseline editing is its own decision (ADR-011) with its own preconditions. Loosening the guard so a change passes without fixing the underlying assertion is rejected outright — a guard weakened to fit the change under review is the failure mode it exists to prevent.

In a working tree shared by concurrent work streams, the next free version number should be reserved explicitly when work starts rather than derived from a directory listing when the file is written, since an uncommitted migration file is invisible to that derivation but visible to anyone listing the directory, and two streams can otherwise pick the same number.

9.17.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • A new migration is one new file with the next free number; nothing existing is touched.

  • The guard now states an invariant Flyway actually has, so it can be reasoned about from the test alone.

  • The DDL/seed split stays a readable filing convention instead of becoming an ordering constraint.

Negative

  • The two folders no longer read top-to-bottom as "schema, then data"; the chronological order is the version numbers, across both folders.

  • A reader who expects the old block layout may briefly mistake the interleaving for a mistake.

Mitigation

  • The test’s own documentation states plainly what it does and does not assert.

  • Reserving a migration version explicitly, rather than deriving it from a directory listing, avoids the collision a shared working tree would otherwise risk.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/test/kotlin/de/littletrotter/infrastructure/persistence/MigrationDirectoryHygieneTest.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/resources/db/migration/{ddl,seed}, application.properties (quarkus.flyway.locations).

9.18. ADR-018: Apache POI for the Excel Export, Not a Hand-Rolled or Commercial Alternative

  • Decision: Excel exports use Apache POI streaming (SXSSFWorkbook).

  • Consequence: Constant low memory consumption during large tabular exports, verified via automated round-trip readback tests.

9.18.1. ADR-018: Apache POI for the Excel Export, Not a Hand-Rolled or Commercial Alternative

9.18.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.18.1.2. Context

The invoice export lets an accountant or auditor pull every invoice header, position and change-journal entry into a spreadsheet and recalculate the totals independently. That sets three requirements for the generator: the file must be a genuine .xlsx that opens without a repair dialog; money cells must stay numeric so the spreadsheet can sum them; and an export of tens of thousands of rows must not be held in memory. Only kotlin-invoice needs the export; kotlin-xpdf builds to a native image and must not gain a JVM-only dependency.

9.18.1.3. Decision

Apache POI (poi-ooxml, Apache-2.0, pinned in the root pom.xml) is the export’s only document-generation dependency, used from one package in kotlin-invoice. Files are written with POI’s streaming workbook (bounded row window, temp files compressed, disposed in a finally), and every cell style is created once per workbook, because POI caps the number of styles per workbook. The full poi-ooxml artifact is used, not the lite variant, because the control-column highlighting needs conditional formatting. The workbook’s palette mirrors the application’s design tokens; the header fill is Material Blue 700 rather than the accent blue to keep a 5.4:1 contrast against white text, and a test guards that ratio.

Alternatives Considered

  • Hand-written SpreadsheetML. Avoids the dependency, but shared strings, styles and package relationships written by hand are exactly the class of subtle file-format bug the export cannot afford, and every value guard POI provides would have to be reimplemented.

  • CSV renamed to .xls. Not a spreadsheet: no sheets, cell types, conditional formatting or frozen header. The positions-only CSV export stays deliberately narrow for that reason.

  • A commercial library. Adds license cost and a closed-source dependency to a project that otherwise runs on Apache-2.0/MIT-class licenses; rejected on that basis alone.

9.18.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • Every exported file is verified in tests by reading it back with POI, proof that it opens.

  • The streaming writer keeps memory bounded regardless of export size.

  • Apache-2.0 like the rest of the dependencies; no NOTICE entry needed.

Negative

  • xmlbeans and commons-compress come along transitively and are not small; they land in the kotlin-invoice image.

  • log4j-api (API only, never log4j-core, so no Log4Shell exposure) is a second logging facade in the module and is routed to the Quarkus log manager.

Mitigation

  • Confined to kotlin-invoice; kotlin-xpdf does not and must not depend on POI.

  • The dependency, its transitives and the pinning are explained next to ${poi.version} in pom.xml, so a version bump needs no archaeology.

Architecture Decision Record — source: pom.xml (${poi.version}), kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/infrastructure/export/workbook/.

9.19. ADR-020: NATS JetStream as the Event Backbone

  • Decision: NATS JetStream acts as the messaging backbone between application, worker, and rendering engine.

  • Consequence: High-throughput event queues with small memory footprint, fully capable on mini PCs.

9.19.1. ADR-020: NATS JetStream as the Event Backbone

9.19.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.19.1.2. Context

PDF/XML generation between kotlin-invoice and kotlin-xpdf is asynchronous and needs a message broker as its backbone (Messaging in Chapter 8 and ADR-008). The deployment target is a single dedicated Linux server on small-budget hardware — a mini PC in a small business, running the whole stack without a separate monitoring cluster (Deployment View) — and every service in it, including the broker, declares an explicit memory limit (Architecture Constraints). A JVM-based broker’s baseline heap and coordination overhead sit in direct tension with that budget.

9.19.1.3. Decision

NATS, with JetStream as its persistence layer, is the event backbone for job/result exchange between kotlin-invoice and kotlin-xpdf. This keeps an event-driven architecture affordable on small hardware, which is the Green-IT idea behind the choice: a small footprint, a single static binary with no external coordination service of its own, and JetStream (built into NATS Server 2.2+) adding durable streams, acknowledgements and replay on top of that same binary rather than requiring a separate persistence tier. The deployment table (Server Deployment) prices the nats service at 128 MiB (96 MiB under the small-footprint overlay) — the same order of magnitude as valkey and well under postgres, rustfs or the application tier, a footprint a JVM broker plus its own coordination layer (ZooKeeper, or KRaft’s controller quorum) could not match on the same box.

A medium-sized enterprise or larger organization, running this system’s architecture at a scale that outgrows a single small-budget host, can switch to Kafka. Neither RabbitMQ nor a plain database job table is a better fit at that scale either: both cover the same job/result queue need with more to run and back up, or with weaker push and dedup properties, than NATS with JetStream offers today. A managed cloud queue is excluded outright by the self-hosting requirement (Technical Constraints).

The envelope principle (ADR-008) keeps everything broker-specific out of business logic — only small typed reference structures cross NATS, never documents or reports — so a broker swap is a messaging-adapter concern, not a domain one. The messaging contract itself is thin: two logical queues (jobs, results) plus one DLQ per queue, which bounds a Kafka switch to subjects becoming topics, durable pull consumers becoming Kafka consumer groups, the Nats-Msg-Id dedup window becoming Kafka’s idempotent-producer configuration, DLQ subjects becoming DLQ topics, a partitioning key preserving per-job ordering, and each side’s already-isolated NATS client code being where the adapter changes land: both kotlin-invoice and kotlin-xpdf keep every class that talks to the broker in its own dedicated infrastructure.messaging package, so a broker swap touches one package per module. Retry/backoff and DLQ routing are hand-implemented rather than framework-driven (ADR-010) and are transport-adjacent, not broker-specific, so they carry over unchanged.

9.19.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • The broker’s own resource cost stays proportional to a small deployment (single binary, roughly 100 MiB memory budget), leaving headroom for the application and rendering tiers on constrained hardware.

  • One operational surface (one binary, one data volume) instead of a broker plus a separate coordination service.

  • The envelope principle and the thin jobs/results/DLQ contract bound what a Kafka switch would touch to a small, enumerable list of adapter changes.

Negative

  • NATS’s ecosystem (connectors, schema registries, stream-processing frameworks) is smaller than Kafka’s; anything beyond the job/result/DLQ shape this project needs would likely have to be hand-built rather than bolted on.

Mitigation

  • The adapter-only exposure (subjects, consumer groups, dedup, DLQ, ordering, client placement) is documented here precisely so a Kafka switch has a checklist rather than a rediscovery exercise.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-shared/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/shared/NatsStreamSetup.kt, kotlin-xpdf/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/xpdf/infrastructure/messaging/JobConsumer.kt, little-trotter-admin-tools/docker-compose.yml (nats service).

9.20. ADR-021: Business Trip as One Invoice Position

  • Decision: A business trip (TRIP) is represented as a single parent invoice position grouping child expenses and per-diems.

  • Consequence: Clean single-item presentation on customer invoice (BG-25) while preserving detailed cost break-downs internally.

9.20.1. ADR-021: Business Trip as One Invoice Position

9.20.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.20.1.2. Context

A business trip used to leave no trace on an invoice as a thing: hotel, taxi and mileage were flat InvoiceItem positions with free text, per-diem days a separate category with their own table, and neither the PDF nor the e-invoice XML knew a trip existed — the invoice recipient saw a pile of the invoice issuer’s own cost items instead of the one service they bought (called a Workshop under the service-company wording, ADR-022). Grouping this into one recipient-facing position also has to survive a storno and a Reverse-Charge invoice, and export to EN 16931/CII without turning back into a second, per-expense line item — which would reopen the per-line VAT-category questions ADR-003 already closed at invoice level.

9.20.1.3. Decision

A business trip is one InvoiceItem of category TRIP (InvoiceItemCategory, persisted code 5, appended after the three deleted travel categories so an old row is never misread) with two owned child collections, cascaded and deleted with the item, never their own repository: TripExpense (trip_expense, one row per hotel/transport/mileage/taxi/other line) and TravelExpenseEntry (travel_expense_entry, per-diem days). Quantity/unit are forced to 1/FLAT_RATE; an optional tripFixedNetPrice replaces the computed sum on the invoice, its delta to calculatedTripNet() shown only in the trip’s own Excel export, never on the invoice. Tax is never chosen on the trip: it inherits the invoice’s main-service rate (TravelExpenseTaxInheritance, § 10 UStG Nebenleistung), the same single-rate-per-invoice guard ADR-003 relies on. calculatedTripNet() sums expense net amounts plus per-diem claimable amounts; on a storno (InvoiceTypeCode.CORRECTED_INVOICE, type code 384) the per-diem share is subtracted instead, because a per-diem day’s claimable amount is never negative — negateForStorno() negates the fixed price and every expense’s unit price but leaves per-diem rows untouched.

For EN 16931/CII export, one trip is exactly one BG-25 line: BT-153/BT-154 (title/purpose) come from the item’s description fields, BG-26 (period) from its service period, quantity is the forced 1. A "detailed" trip additionally carries a BT-127 line note summarizing the expenses (truncated at 1000 characters, "… siehe Anlage") and the trip workbook (ADR-018) as a BG-24 attachment (TypeCode 916, XLSX MIME) — both absent from the grouped rendering. The workbook is rendered once, at job-publish time, and exchanged through Valkey (lt:tripwb:{sha256}), the same envelope/payload-exchange principle ADR-008 applies to the invoice template; kotlin-xpdf resolves and SHA-verifies it, degrading to no attachment rather than failing the job on a miss. The Mustang/CII engine route carries the note and attachment; the Factur-X engine route does not yet — planned, not built.

Alternatives Considered

  • One BG-25 line per expense (the ZUGFeRD E22-style shape). Rejected as the default: it decomposes the one purchased service back into its cost components on the wire and reopens the per-line VAT-category question ADR-003 already settled; kept as a possible future opt-in on explicit invoice-recipient demand, not built.

  • Keeping the three deleted categories as independent positions, grouped only in the UI. Rejected: the domain model would still let an expense or per-diem day exist outside any trip, a fact the business never wants to represent.

9.20.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • The invoice recipient’s invoice shows one purchased service, matching what was actually sold, with tax structurally guaranteed to agree with the invoice’s main service (ADR-003).

  • Owned, repository-less children mean storno copying, cascade deletion and the read-only-once- issued guard reduce to the existing InvoiceItem-level rules, not a second lifecycle.

Negative

  • The storno per-diem subtraction is an asymmetry a maintainer must know explicitly (everything else negates, per-diem days do not), and the document size budget (ADR-014) does not yet count the trip workbook attachment.

  • A "detailed" trip renders differently depending on export engine: Factur-X lacks the note and attachment Mustang/CII already carries.

Mitigation

  • The asymmetry is documented at its source (InvoiceItem.calculatedTripNet) and exercised by both the CII and PDF storno tests; extending the budget and closing the Factur-X gap are named follow-ups, not silent omissions.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/shared/InvoiceItemCategory.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/invoice/ (InvoiceItem, TripExpense, TravelExpenseTaxInheritance), …​/domain/reference/TravelExpenseEntry.kt, kotlin-shared/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/shared/TripExportDto.kt, kotlin-xpdf/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/xpdf/impl/mustang/MustangTransactionBuilder.kt, kotlin-xpdf/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/xpdf/infrastructure/messaging/JobConsumer.kt.

9.21. ADR-022: Wording Profile by Term Substitution

  • Decision: Instance-wide wording profiles (Handwerk/Handel/Beratung) resolve {term:…​} placeholders in translation bundles at runtime.

  • Consequence: Single localization bundle without file duplication; existing issued invoices maintain immutable wording snapshots.

9.21.1. ADR-022: Wording Profile by Term Substitution

9.21.1.1. Status

Accepted, 2026-09-13

9.21.1.2. Context

The same domain concepts read differently depending on the kind of business an instance serves: a trade/craft business calls a purchased good "Material", a retail/trade business calls it "Artikel", and a consulting business calls it "Produkt(e)". A business trip, however, is called "Dienstreise" in every one of them — ADR-021's trip position is one concrete case, and it is the same label regardless of which of the three vocabularies is active. This is a wording choice for the whole instance, not per B2B relationship or sender profile, and it must never touch persisted texts, key names, route slugs or enum names — an already-issued document must render exactly as it did when it was produced.

9.21.1.3. Decision

WordingProfile (kotlin-shared) is a three-value enum, HANDWERK/HANDEL/BERATUNG (persisted code 0/2/1 respectively, HANDWERK the default), resolved by WordingSettingsGate: an env default (littletrotter.wording.profile/LITTLETROTTER_WORDING_PROFILE, an unknown value falling back to HANDWERK with one logged warning) overridable live, without restart, by a system_setting row (key='wording.profile') behind a 10-second cache — the same fail-open/ short-cache shape FeatureSettingsGate and ValidationSettingsGate already use. Switching it is an admin-only action under System → Einstellungen (AppPermission.SYSTEM_VIEW, held only by AppRole.ADMIN).

Only the sellable-position noun varies by profile (Term.PRODUCT_SINGULAR/PLURAL — Material/Artikel/Produkt(e)); a business trip is a plain, profile-independent i18n value ("Dienstreise"/"Business trip") like any other bundle text, never resolved through TermResolver.

The mechanism for the one remaining term is substitution, not a second bundle: a bundle value may carry a {term:name} marker (Term.PRODUCT_SINGULAR/PLURAL only), resolved against a small curated wording-terms(en).properties pair by TermResolver and substituted by TermSubstitution.apply before any MessageFormat pass — a {term:…​} marker is not a valid MessageFormat argument index and would otherwise throw. An unresolvable marker degrades to !{term:name}!, the same convention a missing i18n key already uses. TermSubstitution/ TermResolver are invoked at three points: the UI/REST layer (UiTranslations, backing both TranslationService for Vaadin and I18nBundleResource’s `/api/i18n, against the live gate on every lookup, and reaching the Artikelverwaltung view and every level of the invoice-position tables/exports the same way); the invoice/trip workbook export label builders, which take a tr: (String) → String parameter and are substitution-aware whenever that parameter is UiTranslations::tr (the interactive trip-workbook download) or the job-publish-time TripExportDtoMapper.documentLanguageTranslator; and kotlin-xpdf’s print-label loader, `ReportFormatHelpers.Labels.label(key, language, profile), decoding the profile from the wire-carried InvoiceMetaDto.wordingProfile snapshot rather than a live gate, since an already- queued job must render with whichever profile was active when it was published. No invoice-labels*.properties value carries a {term:…​} marker today, so PDFs render identically under all three profiles — the overload exists for a future label to opt in, not because one uses it yet. referenceData.group.reisekosten ("Reisekosten-Sätze") stays a fixed reference-data term, deliberately outside this mechanism.

Alternatives considered

  • Per-B2B-profile or per-sender-profile wording. Rejected: the vocabulary is a property of the operating company, not of a business relationship — one company, one language, regardless of which invoice recipient or issuer profile an invoice uses.

  • A second, parallel bundle per profile (e.g. messages_handwerk.properties). Rejected: it doubles every translated string for what is, in practice, a handful of nouns, and the parity check would need to cover a bundle × profile cross-product instead of one small term catalog.

  • Keeping the trip label as a term. Rejected: a business trip is called "Dienstreise" the same way in every one of the three trades this project actually serves, so carrying it through TermResolver would add an indirection with no real variation behind it — a plain i18n value degrades more simply (a missing key renders !{key}!, no separate unresolved-placeholder path to reason about) and needs no entry in wording-terms*.properties.

9.21.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • One indirection for the one term that actually varies, reused by the UI/REST layer and the workbook export lambda that already existed, rather than a fourth lookup mechanism; I18nBundleParityTest fails the build if a marker used in either bundle has no curated text for all three profiles and both locales.

  • Live switching needs no restart and no redeploy, and every already-issued document keeps rendering with whatever profile was active when it was produced, by construction.

  • With no trip term, a whole dimension of failure is absent (a workbook cover subtitle carrying an unresolved {term:trip.singular} marker into MessageFormat, which throws), and no real per-instance variation is lost — the trip wording never varies.

Negative

  • Three call sites, not one, mean a future fourth writer of a translated label can reintroduce a gap by copying the wrong one — the job-publish path (TripExportDtoMapper.documentLanguageTranslator) and the interactive download path (UiTranslations::tr) are two independent implementations of the same substitution contract, not one shared function.

  • ResourceBundle.getBundle(baseName, locale) without an explicit no-fallback Control silently resolves to a different locale-suffixed sibling bundle when the JVM’s own default locale matches that sibling instead of the requested locale — e.g. an English request resolving to messages_de.properties on a JVM whose default locale is German, because the JDK’s default fallback-locale search consults Locale.getDefault() before giving up. TranslationService, TermResolver, B2bTermTexts, ReceiptService and TripExportDtoMapper.documentLanguageTranslator therefore all guard against this with ResourceBundle.Control.getNoFallbackControl(…​); a call site without the guard is silently correct only while no bundle value it reads differs between the German and the (fallen-back-to) English bundle.

Mitigation

  • The interactive download path proves the mechanism itself is correct, so closing the three-call- site gap above is confined to wiring each job-publish-time label function the same way, not to the resolver; Term/TermResolver stay the one place a new writer calls, never re-implements.

  • Any new ResourceBundle.getBundle call site for i18n.messages or i18n.wording-terms must pass ResourceBundle.Control.getNoFallbackControl(ResourceBundle.Control.FORMAT_PROPERTIES) — there is no legitimate reason for this project’s bundle lookups to ever consult the JVM’s default locale.

The catalogue wording follows the same profile-independent vocabulary contract: the subject is shown as "Leistungsverzeichnis" in German (the default catalogue name is "Katalog"); its categories and units are defined in ADR-026.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-shared/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/shared/wording/ (WordingProfile, Term, TermResolver, TermSubstitution), kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/settings/WordingSettingsGate.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/ui/i18n/ (UiTranslations, TranslationService, I18nBundleResource), kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/invoice/TripExportDtoMapper.kt (documentLanguageTranslator), kotlin-xpdf/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/xpdf/impl/ReportFormatHelpers.kt.

9.22. ADR-023: VAT id Is One Field, VIES Is Queried Only on Request

  • Decision: VAT ID is a single input field with automated country prefix parsing; EU VIES validation triggers only on explicit user click.

  • Consequence: External VIES outages never block data entry; verified trader names prefill notes without schema bloating.

9.22.1. ADR-023: VAT id Is One Field, VIES Is Queried Only on Request

9.22.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.22.1.2. Context

A VAT ID (German: USt-IdNr.) is, in every EU-recognized format, one string: a two-character country prefix followed by a number whose shape varies per country. Splitting it into two input fields — a country selector plus a number field — is not the industry standard and pushes a decision onto the Office Manager (which country?) that the id itself already answers; it also invites a second failure mode where the selected country and the typed prefix silently disagree. Keeping it as one field, however, means the software has to demonstrate it actually understood the value instead of treating it as an opaque string — otherwise an invoice recipient’s own observation ("the VIES check does not separate the country code from the rest") reads as broken software rather than an unclear field.

The EU VIES REST API is the other half of the problem: its terms of use forbid bulk or automatic queries, so any design that calls it eagerly (on save, on blur, on a scheduled sweep) is a compliance risk regardless of how well-intentioned the automation is. VIES’s own failure modes are also easy to misread: it answers isValid: false not only for a genuinely invalid id but also for MS_UNAVAILABLE, TIMEOUT, IP_BLOCKED and half a dozen other userError codes that mean "VIES could not answer", not "the id is wrong" — collapsing all of them onto one boolean produces exactly the false "invalid" reports this decision closes.

9.22.1.3. Decision

A VAT ID stays one text field (VatIdField) everywhere it is entered — invoice recipient, invoice issuer profile, additional invoice issuer VAT registration — backed by one validator (VatIdValidator) that recognises the two-character prefix, auto-completes it from the linked address country on digit-only input (alpha-2, except Greece’s EL), and validates the full format against a per-country regex table on blur. The separation the id contains is shown, not structural: a suffix names the recognised country, and a country-specific placeholder replaces a generic example.

Verification against EU VIES is a second, independent action, available only for an invoice recipient’s VAT ID, and only on an explicit button click ("VIES prüfen") — never on save, never on blur, never scheduled. ViesHttpClient reads not only VIES’s isValid flag but also its userError, name and address fields, and resolves to one of three outcomes: VERIFIED (id registered; the returned trader name/address are displayed and offered, once, as a prefill for the verification note — never stored in their own columns, since VIES’s answer can change and this project keeps no system of record for a foreign registry’s data), FAILED (userError: INVALID — VIES actively says the id is not registered), and UNREACHABLE (every other case, userError or none: VIES itself, the network, or a malformed response — carrying the reason for display, but never treated as a statement about the id). Only VERIFIED and FAILED change Recipient.vatIdVerificationStatus/vatIdVerifiedAt; UNREACHABLE never does.

Alternatives considered

  • Two fields (country selector + number). Rejected: not the industry-standard shape for a VAT id, and it moves a decision onto the user that the id itself already encodes; it also permits a selector/prefix disagreement the current one-field design cannot produce.
