  • Query VIES automatically (on save or on a schedule). Rejected outright: the VIES terms of use forbid bulk/automatic queries, and an unreachable VIES has nothing to do with whether the id is actually valid — the ID would flip status on outages that have nothing to do with the invoice recipient.

  • Store the VIES-returned trader name/address in their own columns. Rejected: this project keeps no system of record for VIES’s own registry data, and a stored copy would silently go stale the moment VIES’s answer changes; displaying it once and offering it as an editable note prefill carries the same information without a schema commitment.

9.22.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • One validator behind three entry points means the format rule can never drift between invoice recipient, invoice issuer profile, and invoice issuer VAT registration — a defect fixed once is fixed everywhere.

  • UNREACHABLE as its own outcome, distinct from FAILED, means an outage at VIES’s end can never be misreported as "this id is invalid" again — the specific defect that prompted this decision.

  • No VIES payload gets its own column, so there is no new schema surface and no stale-copy problem to maintain.

Negative

  • VatIdVerificationStatus now carries three meaningfully different states (VERIFIED/FAILED/unset) instead of a simple boolean, which every reader of the field (UI badge, export, report) must handle explicitly rather than defaulting to "not verified = false".

  • The verification note is a free-text prefill, not a structured record — a future need to query "which invoice recipients were verified with this exact trader name" would require parsing it back out.

Mitigation

  • The three-state status is exhaustively handled at its one rendering site (the invoice recipient’s tax tab); a when over the sealed VatIdVerificationResult fails to compile if a fourth branch is ever added without being handled.

  • The note stays free text on purpose (see Alternatives above); a structured need, if it ever arises, is a new, explicit column — not a retrofit of this one.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/value/VatIdValidator.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/ui/component/VatIdField.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/infrastructure/vies/ViesHttpClient.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/masterdata/VatIdVerification.kt (VatIdVerificationPort, VatIdVerificationResult), kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/masterdata/Recipient.kt (vatId, vatIdVerificationStatus, vatIdVerifiedAt, vatIdVerificationNote).

9.23. ADR-024: Maintenance Wake-up Hints over Core NATS

  • Decision: Maintenance tasks persist durably in PostgreSQL; best-effort Core NATS notifications trigger immediate processing.

  • Consequence: Low-latency execution without queue duplication; periodic database polling acts as guaranteed fallback.

9.23.1. ADR-024: Maintenance Wake-up Hints over Core NATS

9.23.1.1. Status

Proposed

9.23.1.2. Context

Maintenance work is represented by a PostgreSQL queue because requests must survive broker outages, process restarts, and offline demo-data loading. Waiting for a periodic database scan adds avoidable latency after an operator or loader queues work. A notification can provide a prompt wake-up, but making that notification durable would duplicate the database queue and create a second ordering and acknowledgement protocol.

9.23.1.3. Decision

The application publishes a best-effort Core NATS notification after the transaction that inserted the maintenance request commits. The subject is maintenance.v1.<instance>.requested; the instance token matches [A-Za-z0-9_-]+. The version-1 JSON envelope contains only a positive requestId and version: 1.

The publisher is reached through an application port by an AFTER_SUCCESS observer and runs on the transaction-cleared executor. A consumer validates the envelope and asks the maintenance processor to schedule its serialized drain; it never performs database work on the NATS dispatch thread. Duplicate hints are harmless because PostgreSQL claims remain authoritative and drain requests are coalesced. Lost hints are handled by the worker’s startup pass and five-minute recovery scan. A publish failure is logged and never causes the committed enqueue to fail.

This hint uses Core NATS. ADR-020 remains the decision for the job/result JetStream backbone. No new JetStream stream or subject is added, because the existing app/xpdf stream reconcilers own that stream and could remove an unrelated subject.

The default instance is default, suitable for the existing per-instance broker deployment. Deployments that share a broker must configure a unique instance token.

9.23.1.4. Alternatives Considered

  • A second durable NATS stream: rejected because it duplicates PostgreSQL durability and ordering, and would require another acknowledgement/replay lifecycle.

  • Only a periodic scan: rejected because it adds unnecessary dispatch latency after a committed maintenance request.

  • Publishing before commit: rejected because a rolled-back request could trigger work that has no durable database record.

9.23.1.5. Consequences

  • PostgreSQL remains the sole source of truth for ordering, retries, and crash recovery.

  • NATS outages affect wake-up latency only; startup and periodic recovery remain available.

  • Duplicate notifications must be tolerated by the serialized drain path.

  • The five-minute recovery interval is configurable, with five minutes as the initial default.

  • Core NATS delivery and serialized drain behavior still require isolated PostgreSQL/NATS integration verification before this decision can be accepted.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-shared/…​/MaintenanceWakeup.kt, kotlin-invoice/…​/MaintenanceJobQueuedObserver.kt, kotlin-invoice/…​/MaintenanceWakeupNatsAdapter.kt.

9.24. ADR-025: Invoice Aggregate Encapsulation and the Command/DTO Boundary

  • Decision: Invoice.status has private setter access; all child collections are read-only List. Boundaries are enforced by ArchUnit (command boundary architecture rules).

  • Consequence: Eliminates domain state mutation bypassing aggregate rules and prevents entity leaks to presentation layer.

  • Amended by: ADR-027 — the command entry points are the *UseCase input ports.

9.24.1. ADR-025: Invoice Aggregate Encapsulation and the Command/DTO Boundary

9.24.1.1. Status

Accepted, implemented

9.24.1.2. Context

The Persistence concept (Chapter 8) names two structural gaps an Invoice aggregate must not have: a public status var any caller could assign directly, bypassing InvoiceStateMachine, and items/documents/partySnapshots collections exposed as MutableList, so any caller holding a loaded Invoice could alias and mutate them without going through a domain method. It also requires the six rules of the command/DTO boundary to exist as enforced ArchUnit checks rather than only as a written convention that the write entry points InvoiceItemCommand, TripCommand and UpdateInvoiceHeaderCommand happen to follow.

Two constraints shaped the fix. First, Invoice is a Hibernate entity read via field access (the @Id sits on a field); any encapsulation had to keep working with that, not switch to constructor-only immutability the ORM cannot hydrate into. Second, a handful of legitimate call sites construct an Invoice already in a non-DRAFT status at birth — a storno document (InvoiceStornoService.createCreditNote, born CANCELLED) and synthetic demo/test data (DevDataFixtures) — and must keep doing so without replaying a full lifecycle.

ADR-027 amends this decision: the command entry points are the *UseCase input ports, one per use case; no composite interface or *Facade type exists.

9.24.1.3. Decision

  • Invoice.status keeps its JPA annotations but gets private set; the only way to change it on an existing instance is transitionTo (already the class’s own state-machine-checked method). A new Invoice.withInitialStatus(status) companion factory is the single, explicit, construction-time-only door around the private setter, used by the two legitimate born-non-DRAFT call sites above.

  • items, documents and partySnapshots are declared as List<T> instead of MutableList<T>. This is a compile-time-only change — Kotlin’s List/MutableList split erases to the same java.util.List at the bytecode level, so Hibernate’s field-access hydration is unaffected — but it removes every external caller’s ability to call .add()/.remove()/.clear() on the collection or reassign it wholesale. addItem/removeItem/applyPartySnapshots are the only mutation path, casting to MutableList internally. applyPartySnapshots performs an unconditional clear() rather than a PartyRole-filtered removeAll: the collection only ever holds the two PartyRole values, so a filter’s "neither" branch would be structurally dead.

  • documents/llmAnalyses/receipts are read-only or left mutable per case: documents has the read-only shape above because nothing legitimately adds to it from the owning side (a generation result is persisted directly through InvoiceDocumentRepository); receipts and llmAnalyses are explicitly not locked down — they match the Persistence concept’s named exemption for master-data/child-entity screens not yet migrated (the remaining Receipt usages are separate migration slices), recorded here as the open follow-up.

  • Three marker interfaces (de.littletrotter.application.command: Command, CommandResult, CommandEntryPoint) give CommandBoundaryArchitectureTest a concrete boundary to scan, since commands share their package with plain application services and a class-name suffix alone is not a reliable signal (a warning the Persistence concept already gives). InvoiceItemCommand, TripCommand, TripExpenseCommand, PerDiemEntryCommand and UpdateInvoiceHeaderCommand implement Command; InvoiceDetailDto and ModelReport implement CommandResult; every *UseCase input port (ADR-027) extends CommandEntryPoint.

  • The six rules (command purity, no entity leak, no direct UI mutation, immutable commands, domain independent of commands, entry points in the application layer) are each one ArchUnit rule with a positive check against production code and a negative check against a small, deliberately violating test-only specimen (..arch.specimen.., ..domain.invoice.specimen.., ..ui.specimen.. packages, excluded from the production scan by virtue of being test sources, carrying no business data).

  • The no-entity-leak check (no entity leak through a command entry point’s signature) is satisfied by projection: the invoice read entry point’s findLlmAnalyses returns ModelReport values (in application.dto) rather than the JPA entity InvoiceLlmAnalysis, and the UI call sites receive the already-projected ModelReport list instead of mapping the entity themselves.

  • A CLASS-level jacoco execution (check-aggregate-command-boundary-100, modeled on the existing check-calculation-core-100) requires LINE/BRANCH COVEREDRATIO 1.00 for Invoice, Invoice$Companion and the five command classes above — a gate addition alongside the existing bundle gate and check-calculation-core-100’s own `Invoice rule, not a replacement for either.

9.24.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • No ordinary caller — UI, an application service reaching for a shortcut, a future contributor — can move Invoice.status or alias its collections around InvoiceStateMachine/addItem/ removeItem/applyPartySnapshots; the compiler enforces it, not a code-review convention.

  • The six rules are checked rules with a proof they reject a violation, not only a description in a decision document.

  • `ModelReport’s move closes a genuine architecture gap rather than papering over the finding with an ArchUnit exemption.

Negative

  • Three marker interfaces and the application.dto type ModelReport add a small amount of indirection for anyone reading InvoiceItemCommand/InvoiceDetailDto/ModelReport for the first time.

  • receipts/llmAnalyses remain publicly mutable collections on Invoice; a reader of the entity source sees inconsistent treatment across its five collections unless they also read this ADR or the exemption in Persistence.

Mitigation

  • The receipts/llmAnalyses exemption is named explicitly here and in Persistence, with the migration slices they belong to (Receipt/TaxRate/Article/GenerationJob UI usages) already tracked as open follow-ups rather than silently dropped.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/invoice/Invoice.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/command/CommandContracts.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/test/kotlin/de/littletrotter/arch/CommandBoundaryArchitectureTest.kt.

9.25. ADR-026: Catalogue Categories and X-Rechnung Units

  • Decision: The sellable catalog is partitioned into three explicit categories (Material, Unit Price, Service) with bound X-Rechnung unit subsets.

  • Consequence: Prevents illegal unit combinations in e-invoice XML before positions reach the invoice drafting stage.

9.25.1. ADR-026: Catalogue Categories and X-Rechnung Units

9.25.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.25.1.2. Context

The sellable catalogue (Article) needed a way to constrain which X-Rechnung unit codes make sense for a given kind of position, without introducing a second, redundant "type" column next to the existing InvoiceItemCategory.

9.25.1.3. Decision

The sellable catalogue is exposed as three category views: Material, Einheitspreis, and Dienstleistung (Arbeitsstunden). A business trip (TRIP) is an invoice position, not a catalogue item, and is therefore excluded from the catalogue views. Category-specific units are kept in the X-Rechnung vocabulary: material allows Stück, Meter, Quadratmeter, Kubikmeter, Kilometer, Liter, Kilogramm, Anfahrt, Gramm, and laufender Meter; unit price allows Quadratmeter, Meter, laufender Meter, Kubikmeter, Hektar, and Pauschale; service allows Stunde, Tag, Pauschale, and Anfahrt. The active category makes an additional type column redundant. A catalogue position referenced by an invoice is protected from deletion.

9.25.1.4. Alternatives Considered

  • A separate type column alongside category: rejected — the category already determines which units are valid, so a second column would only ever duplicate that information or drift from it.

  • One unrestricted unit list for every category: rejected — it would let a material position pick a service-only unit (e.g. "Stunde") and vice versa, an invalid combination the X-Rechnung vocabulary does not actually support for that category.

9.25.1.5. Consequences

  • Generated demo data and UI selection rules share one explicit unit contract and cannot create catalogue rows that the invoice form cannot represent.

  • A catalogue position’s category is fixed once units are chosen from its list; changing category later requires re-validating the unit.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/shared/InvoiceItemCategory.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/shared/InvoiceItemUnit.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/masterdata/Article.kt.

9.26. ADR-027: Dedicated Input Ports per Use Case, No Composite Interfaces

  • Decision: UI and inbound adapters inject narrow role interfaces in application.<context>.port.inbound — *QueryPort for reads, *UseCase (a CommandEntryPoint) for writes; composite interfaces and *Facade types do not exist.

  • Consequence: Each caller depends only on the use cases it calls; ArchUnit (InputPortArchitectureTest) enforces naming, the 12-method cap and the no-composite rule.

9.26.1. ADR-027: Dedicated Input Ports per Use Case, No Composite Interfaces

9.26.1.1. Status

Accepted, implemented

9.26.1.2. Context

The Vaadin UI and the inbound adapters need a boundary into the application layer. One way to provide it is a composite interface: an empty or near-empty interface that bundles several sub-interfaces through inheritance and is then injected everywhere, often under a Facade name. The name misleads — nothing is simplified, the type is only a collection point — and the shape is a *fat interface (an Interface Segregation Principle violation):

  • every caller depends on every method of the bundle, not only on the ones it calls, so a change to an unrelated use case touches its compile and test surface;

  • tests mock the whole bundle, which hides which use case a view really exercises;

  • the single implementing class accumulates every use case and can only stay within the complexity thresholds (detekt TooManyFunctions, LongParameterList) through helper bundles that exist for no domain reason;

  • the query side and the command side blur, so the command/DTO boundary of ADR-025 has no precise surface to check.

This decision amends ADR-025: the command entry points that ADR names are the *UseCase input ports described here.

9.26.1.3. Decision

  • The UI and inbound adapters depend only on input ports: role interfaces cut per use case, in de.littletrotter.application.<context>.port.inbound (port.inbound, not port.in, since in is a Kotlin keyword). Each caller injects exactly the ports it uses.

  • Two port kinds, distinguished by name:

    • *QueryPort — reads, e.g. InvoiceListQueryPort, InvoiceDetailQueryPort, InvoiceStatisticsQueryPort, RecipientQueryPort. A query port is never a CommandEntryPoint.

    • *UseCase — writes, e.g. CreateInvoiceUseCase, EditInvoiceHeaderUseCase, EditInvoiceItemsUseCase, ChangeInvoiceStatusUseCase, ManageRecipientUseCase. Every use case port extends CommandEntryPoint and is therefore subject to the command-boundary rules of ADR-025.

  • Reference data follows the same cut per catalogue: <Catalogue>QueryPort plus Manage<Catalogue>UseCase in application.reference.port.inbound (e.g. TaxRateQueryPort / ManageTaxRateUseCase; languages are read-only and have only LanguageQueryPort).

  • Each port has one implementing class in the application layer, named after the use case (InvoiceListQueries, EditInvoiceHeader, TaxRateQueries, ManageTaxRate); write authorization (@RequiresPermission) sits on the use-case implementation.

  • No composite interfaces: no interface combines two or more of the application’s own interfaces, and no input port extends another input port. No type carries a *Facade name.

  • A port declares at most 12 methods (overloads count individually; guideline ≤ 8). The cut follows caller clusters, not single methods, so ports stay cohesive without degenerating into one-method interfaces.

  • Port signatures use DTOs and value types only; entities stay behind the port.

  • A large view does not compensate for many ports with a parameter object. It is split into injected sub-components that each take only their own ports — InvoiceDetailView is composed of InvoiceCockpitHero, InvoiceDetailTabs and InvoiceEntryPane, which in turn delegate to beans such as InvoiceEntryHeaderFields, InvoiceEntryPartyCard, InvoiceEntryDatesSection, InvoiceEntrySaveFlow, InvoiceEinvoiceEntryTab, EinvoiceGuidedSteps, InvoiceOverviewPanel, InvoiceItemsTab and InvoiceReceiptsTab.

  • The mail dispatch component (kotlin-email, ADR-015) follows the same cut in its own de.littletrotter.email.service.application.port.inbound package: *QueryPort/*UseCase per use case, no composite interfaces. Since the component depends only on kotlin-shared and stays self-contained, it declares its own CommandEntryPoint marker there rather than importing the one kotlin-invoice uses. A port’s command and read-model carrier types (e.g. ComposeMailCommand, MailTemplateView, MailJournalEntryView) live next to the port itself in port.inbound rather than in the implementing application.<usecase> package, so that package never has to import back out of its own port — keeping the two packages free of an import cycle.

9.26.1.4. Enforcement

InputPortArchitectureTest (ArchUnit) checks, against production code and against deliberately violating test-only specimens:

  • no input port extends another input port;

  • no interface in de.littletrotter combines two or more of the application’s own interfaces;

  • an input port declares at most 12 methods;

  • a *QueryPort is never a CommandEntryPoint, a *UseCase always is, and nothing else lives in a port.inbound package (so a *Facade there fails the naming rule);

  • production contains input ports at all, so the rules are not vacuous.

CommandBoundaryArchitectureTest applies the command/DTO rules of ADR-025 to every CommandEntryPoint, i.e. to every *UseCase port.

EmailInputPortArchitectureTest checks the same shape for kotlin-email’s own `port.inbound package, plus two adapter rules that mirror the invoicing side: no adapter (service.adapter..) depends on a concrete @ApplicationScoped application service instead of going through an input port, and no adapter outside service.adapter.persistence depends on email.core.repository.. directly.

9.26.1.5. Consequences
Positive

  • A caller’s constructor states exactly which use cases it depends on; unused methods are not reachable, and changes to unrelated use cases do not ripple into it.

  • Unit and Karibu tests mock only the ports of the component under test; E2E suites inject the concrete port and exercise the real pipeline.

  • Implementations stay small and cohesive, within the detekt thresholds without helper bundles.

  • The query/command split is visible in the type name and checked by ArchUnit, giving the command boundary a precise surface.

Negative

  • More, smaller types: dozens of port interfaces with one implementation each, and views with several injected ports or sub-components.

  • The port cut is a design decision per use case; a cut that is too fine produces one-method interfaces, a cut that is too coarse drifts back towards a fat interface.

Mitigation

  • The 12-method cap and the no-composite rule bound the coarse side; cutting by caller cluster bounds the fine side.

  • New role interfaces are created directly as *QueryPort/*UseCase in port.inbound, so the naming rule catches any other shape at build time.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/command/CommandContracts.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/test/kotlin/de/littletrotter/arch/InputPortArchitectureTest.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/test/kotlin/de/littletrotter/arch/CommandBoundaryArchitectureTest.kt.

9.27. ADR-028: Optimistic Concurrency, First Committed Writer Wins

  • Decision: Every aggregate write checks the caller’s expectedVersion against the aggregate root’s @Version before mutating; invoice items and trips check Invoice.version, not their own entity-level version column. A mismatch rejects before any row is touched.

  • Consequence: No lost updates across header, items, trips, recipient, article and reference data; one conflict notion and one optimistic-lock notification UI-wide. See Concurrent Editing (Chapter 8) for the per-entity coverage table.

9.27.1. ADR-028: Optimistic Concurrency, First Committed Writer Wins

9.27.1.1. Status

Accepted, implemented

9.27.1.2. Context

A concurrency audit (2026-09-26) found the invoice header already checks a submitted revision against Invoice.version before applying a change (InvoiceHeaderUpdateService.updateHeader, AbstractCrudView’s shared revision hooks) and `Recipient is fully covered (RecipientAddressSaveConcurrencyIT, Karibu), but invoice items had no version check at all: InvoiceItemCommand/TripCommand carry no expectedVersion, so two users editing the same position silently overwrite each other — a lost update, not merely an untested path. Article/Leistungsverzeichnis and reference-data catalogues sit in between: a version check exists (OptimisticLockException on a stale row, proven in ArticleManagementServiceSaveTest/CatalogueWritesTest), but only at unit-test granularity, with no delete-vs-update or two-session proof. A delete racing a concurrent update was inconsistently handled: sometimes a readable not-found error, sometimes an unguarded removal of changes the deleting session never saw.

Two designs were available for versioning invoice items: give each item write its own version check against InvoiceItem.version (the column Hibernate already maintains on that entity), or route every item/trip write through the owning Invoice aggregate’s single version. Invoice does not behave like the latter for free: Invoice.items is a mappedBy collection — the item owns the foreign key and its own @Version — so Invoice.recalculateTotals() before InvoiceRepository.save(invoice) only dirties the invoice row when the recomputed totals actually change; an item-only edit that leaves them numerically the same (a description-only edit, for example) leaves Invoice.version unadvanced, a real lost update caught by stress-test invariant 6. Making Invoice.version advance on every item/trip change regardless is therefore an explicit, separate part of this decision (see below), not a side effect already true of the aggregate.

9.27.1.3. Decision

  • Concurrency control is optimistic, via JPA @Version, one version per aggregate root — never a pessimistic lock held across a user’s think time. Locking (Locking above) covers a different problem: mutual exclusion between concurrent transactions inside one request, not contention between two separate user sessions across think time; the two mechanisms are not substitutes for each other and this decision does not change lock-ordering.

  • The invoice aggregate’s version (Invoice.version) is the single version checked for every write that touches the invoice, its items, or its trips. InvoiceItemCommand and TripCommand carry an expectedVersion: Long? field, populated from the invoice version the editing dialog last loaded, checked the same way UpdateInvoiceHeaderCommand.expectedVersion already is — before InvoiceItemMutationService/TripService mutate the loaded aggregate, never after. InvoiceItem.version/TripExpense.version remain as Hibernate’s own optimistic-lock columns (unremovable without restructuring the entity mapping) but are not the value application code compares; comparing the aggregate’s one version keeps the check consistent with how the header already works and avoids a second, item-scoped conflict notion the UI would also have to surface.

  • Because InvoiceItem/TripExpense are separately versioned, mappedBy entities, an item/trip write does not reliably dirty the invoice row on its own (recomputed totals can come out unchanged, e.g. a description-only edit). InvoiceItemMutationService.addItem/updateItem/ deleteItemByPosition and TripService.addTrip/updateTrip therefore each call InvoiceRepository.forceVersionIncrement(saved) (em.lock(invoice, LockModeType.OPTIMISTIC_FORCE_INCREMENT)) immediately after InvoiceRepository.save(invoice), unconditionally, so Invoice.version advances on every item/trip write regardless of whether it changed any column of invoice itself — the same pattern already used to force SenderProfile/Recipient/`Article’s own version forward when only a child row changed.

  • First committed writer wins: whichever session’s transaction commits first advances Invoice.version; every other session holding the now-stale version is rejected on its next write, never allowed to overwrite silently. A version mismatch is checked and rejected before any row is touched — the transaction is never a partial write rolled back afterward, it never starts making changes.

  • A version mismatch throws jakarta.persistence.OptimisticLockException, surfaced to the UI as a dedicated optimistic-lock notification; the offending dialog/editor stays open with the user’s input intact rather than closing or discarding it.

  • An update against a row deleted meanwhile fails with a readable "changed or deleted by another user" error and never re-inserts the row; a delete carrying a stale expectedVersion is rejected the same way a stale update is — a delete is a write like any other and is bound by the same check, never a silent removal of edits the deleting session never saw. An update racing a successful concurrent delete of the same row is distinguishable from a genuine version conflict and reported as the deletion it is, not as an ambiguous OptimisticLockException.

  • A recipient, sender or article deleted concurrently while an invoice or item write referencing it is in flight yields a readable rejection, never a raw foreign-key violation reaching the caller.

  • Reference-data catalogues keep the existing shared CatalogueWrites.rowFor(id, expectedVersion) helper (a null expectedVersion intentionally skips the check, for programmatic/seed writes that have no prior read to compare against).

  • Verification spans four levels, each proving a different part of the same contract: unit/Karibu tests per entity; the ConcurrentCrudStressE2EIT 100-thread storm (kotlin-e2e-tests), extended with a lost-update invariant and a pg_stat_database.deadlocks delta invariant; a Playwright two-user conflict spec per entity (e2e-test-playwright/tests/concurrency/, sibling little-trotter-admin-tools repository); and the stress suite’s deadlock-delta gate at full-stack scale. The storm checks 7 invariants (zero unexpected failures, zero deadlocks, no lost update) and the Playwright suite covers 10 conflict specs; the module suite runs under the coverage gate and detekt. See Concurrent Editing for the per-entity coverage table.

9.27.1.4. Alternatives Considered

  • Per-item version check against InvoiceItem.version directly: rejected — it would introduce a second, item-scoped notion of "conflict" alongside the header’s invoice-scoped one, forcing the UI to distinguish and explain two kinds of optimistic-lock error for what a user experiences as one document; checking the aggregate’s one version once is simpler, at the cost of the explicit forceVersionIncrement call above to make that version actually advance on every item change (it does not do so on its own — see Context).

  • Pessimistic (row) locking held for the duration of an open dialog: rejected — a lock held across user think time (seconds to minutes) blocks every other session from even reading the row, defeats the multi-tab/multi-user editing the application already supports, and risks the same AB-BA lock-ordering hazards Locking exists to prevent, for no correctness gain over optimistic checking at commit time.

  • Last-writer-wins, unversioned: rejected — silently discards a concurrent edit neither user is warned about; acceptable for nothing a user deliberately typed.

  • Merge/reconcile concurrent edits automatically: rejected — an invoice position has no field-level merge semantics a user would trust silently (quantity, price and tax rate are not independently mergeable); rejecting and asking the user to re-apply their edit against the current state is the safer, simpler contract.

9.27.1.5. Consequences
Positive

  • One conflict notion, one notification, one dialog-stays-open behavior across header, items, trips, recipient, article and reference data — a user learns the pattern once.

  • No lost updates: every accepted write is provably against the version the editing session last saw, for every entity in the table, not only the header.

  • Deadlock risk is not increased: optimistic checks add no new lock, so the existing lock-first ordering rule (Locking) is untouched; the new deadlock-delta invariant exists to keep proving that under load, not merely by inspection.

Negative

  • InvoiceItemCommand/TripCommand grow one more field the UI dialogs must thread through correctly; a dialog that fails to refresh its loaded expectedVersion after a partial save would see spurious conflicts against its own prior write.

  • Reusing Invoice.version for item/trip writes means an unrelated header edit (e.g. a note change) and a concurrent item edit can conflict with each other even though they touch disjoint fields — a deliberately coarser granularity than per-field merging, traded for the single, explainable conflict notion above.

Mitigation

  • The four-level test proof (see Decision) runs with the build, so the contract is verified continuously rather than assumed once this decision is recorded.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/invoice/InvoiceItemMutationService.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/invoice/TripService.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/repository/InvoiceRepository.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/reference/CatalogueWrites.kt.

9.28. ADR-029: A Single Injectable Time Source, Two Readings of One Moment

  • Decision: TimeSource replaces direct now() calls; points in time (instant(), nowUtc()) are UTC, calendar dates (today(), localNow()) are read in Europe/Berlin. Database, event streams, and RustFS store timestamps strictly in UTC, while UI presents local time (default Europe/Berlin). Invoice numbers are drawn for the invoice’s own business date, not the allocation instant.

  • Consequence: Year/month-boundary behavior is deterministically testable by fixing one clock; business logic no longer depends on the container’s TZ. The database never reads its own clock: no time defaults, the audit trigger stamps from app.now. Multi-timezone operations for branch offices are inherently enabled. Enforced by ArchUnit in every module.

9.28.1. ADR-029: A Single Injectable Time Source, Two Readings of One Moment

9.28.1.1. Status

Accepted, implemented.

9.28.1.2. Context

Calendar dates and timestamps were read through roughly seventy direct LocalDate.now() / OffsetDateTime.now() calls scattered across the codebase. Each call silently depended on the container’s TZ setting rather than on a product property, and the yearly reset of number ranges — an invoice number carries the year it was issued in — could not be tested deterministically: a test could not fix "now" at the year boundary without controlling every call site individually. A java.time.Clock bean already existed, but only five services actually used it.

9.28.1.3. Decision

One component, TimeSource (de.littletrotter.shared.time, kotlin-shared), replaces every direct now() call in every module. ClockTimeSource implements it over a java.time.Clock. kotlin-shared has no CDI, so each deployable produces its own @ApplicationScoped instance over the system UTC clock: TimeSourceProducer in kotlin-invoice, a producer of its own in kotlin-xpdf. kotlin-facturx has no CDI either and receives the moment from its caller as a parameter.

TimeSource exposes two readings of the same instant, not one:

  • Points in time — instant(), nowUtc() — are UTC. Everything stored, compared, or measured uses these.

  • Calendar dates — today(), localNow() — are read in the business zone, Europe/Berlin (GermanTime.ZONE, kotlin-shared), the zone the user actually works in.

The distinction matters at midnight: an invoice sent at 00:30 on 1 January in Berlin belongs to the new year, although UTC still reads 31 December.

A stored moment becomes a calendar day only through GermanTime.dayOf(…​) (kotlin-shared), never through the moment’s own offset (OffsetDateTime.toLocalDate() on a UTC value) or the process zone (ZoneId.systemDefault()). The offer’s day of sending, for example, is the German day of the actor’s UTC timestamp, so an offer dated today can be sent at 00:30.

Invoice numbers are drawn for the invoice’s own business date, not for the instant the allocation happens: InvoiceNumberService.generateNextNumber(sender, date, …) takes invoice.invoiceDate, which is stamped once at the invoice’s → SENT transition. The number’s embedded year and the printed invoice date can therefore never diverge across a midnight boundary.

Persistence and transport stay UTC throughout: every database timestamp column is TIMESTAMPTZ, Hibernate reads and writes UTC (quarkus.hibernate-orm.jdbc.timezone=UTC), event stream envelopes and payloads (NATS JetStream) carry instants in UTC, and object storage metadata (RustFS) adheres strictly to UTC. The UI renders timestamps in the user’s local time (DST-aware, winter/summer time), defaulting to Europe/Berlin (GermanTime.ZONE), and paving the way for multi-timezone operations across distributed branches for mid-sized enterprises. Entity audit stamps (AuditableEntity.createdAt/updatedAt) are written by AuditTimestampListener, creation moments of the remaining entities by CreationStampListener (StampedOnCreation); both are JPA entity listeners that Quarkus runs as CDI beans — so the domain itself never needs a clock for its own bookkeeping, and Hibernate’s generated timestamps are not used at all.

The database does not invent time either. The schema has no DEFAULT NOW()/CURRENT_DATE; every writer supplies its dates and timestamps. The invoice audit trigger stamps occurred_at from the session setting app.now instead of clock_timestamp() and rejects a write without it. AppNowConnectionInterceptor (an Agroal pool interceptor) sets app.now from the TimeSource on every connection the application takes from the pool; any other writer — an admin script, the datagen bulk loader — sets it itself. Native SQL binds timestamps as parameters instead of calling CURRENT_TIMESTAMP; seed rows carry the fixed baseline moment. Domain methods that need a date or instant take it as a parameter instead of reading the clock themselves.

9.28.1.4. Enforcement

ArchUnit forbids in production code any now(…​) call on a java.time type, any Clock.system* factory call, System.currentTimeMillis(), System.nanoTime(), building a ClockTimeSource or a fixed/offset/ticking Clock, and Hibernate’s @CreationTimestamp/@UpdateTimestamp/ @CurrentTimestamp, outside ClockTimeSource and the module’s own producer. It also forbids OffsetDateTime.toLocalDate() and ZoneId.systemDefault() outside GermanTime, so calendar days are read in German time only. The rules exist once, in TimeRules of kotlin-architecture-rules (ADR-038), self-tested there against violating and permitted specimens; every module (kotlin-shared, kotlin-facturx, kotlin-xpdf, kotlin-invoice, the offer and mail modules) checks its own production classes with them in TimeSourceArchitectureTest.

9.28.1.5. Testability

Unit tests fix time with ClockTimeSource.fixedAt(instant) or fixedAtLocal(localDateTime), or a mock. Quarkus tests swap the application-scoped bean per test with QuarkusMock.installMockForType. A year-rollover test fixes the clock at 31 December 23:59:59 and at 1 January 00:00, both Berlin time, and asserts the numbering and date behavior on each side of the boundary.

9.28.1.6. Alternatives Considered

  • A static/ambient clock holder: rejected — global mutable state invites test cross-talk between parallel test classes and hides the dependency from a bean’s constructor.

  • Injecting raw java.time.Clock everywhere: rejected — every call site would then choose its own zone conversion independently, reintroducing the inconsistency this decision removes.

  • Relying on the container’s TZ=Europe/Berlin: rejected — a deployment setting is not a product property; the business zone must not depend on how a given container happens to be configured.

9.28.1.7. Consequences
Positive

  • Year- and month-boundary behavior (number ranges, reporting cutoffs) is deterministically testable by fixing one clock.

  • Business logic no longer depends on the container’s TZ configuration.

  • The business zone is defined in exactly one place and can be changed there alone.

Negative

  • Every bean that needs "now" takes a TimeSource constructor parameter instead of calling a static method — more constructor wiring throughout the codebase.

  • Every writer outside the application — admin scripts, datagen — must set app.now before it touches a journaled table; a forgotten setting fails the write instead of silently stamping the database’s clock.

  • Elapsed-time measurements use the wall clock of the TimeSource rather than a monotonic counter; a clock step during a measurement skews that one log value, never a stored business value.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-shared/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/shared/time/TimeSource.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/infrastructure/persistence/AppNowConnectionInterceptor.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/resources/db/migration/ddl/V1__baseline_schema.sql, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/shared/TimeSourceProducer.kt, kotlin-shared/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/shared/GermanTime.kt, kotlin-architecture-rules/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/architecture/rules/TimeRules.kt.

9.29. ADR-030: Typst as the Only Layout Engine

  • Decision: The base PDF is rendered by the Typst CLI, a pinned, checksum-verified binary running as a sandboxed subprocess of kotlin-xpdf (TypstCompiler), never as a separate service. Typst is the product’s only layout engine: there is no engine switch, no second engine and no uploadable template format. Templates are layout only and receive preformatted data; output is byte-deterministic.

  • Consequence: The pipeline after the base document (Mustang, post-render mark, veraPDF) runs on the Typst base document. Exactly one standard Typst template ships in the worker image; layouts change in code only, and there is no template catalogue or administration tab. The Typst fixtures are the only layout reference; an ArchUnit rule keeps net.sf.jasperreports.. out of kotlin-xpdf. Verified by TypstCompileIT against the containerised pinned CLI.

9.29.1. ADR-030: Typst as the Only Layout Engine

9.29.1.1. Status

Accepted, 2026-09-27

9.29.1.2. Context

The base PDF of every invoice is a typeset document that Mustang turns into a Factur-X attachment with XMP and that veraPDF then checks. The layout engine has to express DIN 5008 geometry in millimetres, produce byte-identical output for identical input (golden tests, archive content hashes), export PDF/A-3b with embedded files natively, and run in the worker without a heavy reflection-based dependency tree.

Typst is a typesetting compiler with a native PDF/A-2b/3b export, embedded-file support, absolute millimetre positioning, repeatable table headers, German hyphenation and byte-identical output for identical input. It is a Rust binary without a JVM binding, pre-1.0 (0.15.x), released under Apache-2.0. There is no official container image and no visual designer.

9.29.1.3. Decision

Typst is the layout engine — the product’s only one, not a deployment choice. No other layout engine, engine switch, fallback or uploadable template format exists: Jasper was retired on 2026-09-29 and there is no Jasper module, no Maven profile for one, no JRXML catalogue, no ServiceLoader extension point for a second engine and no template-admin upload. An ArchUnit rule pins net.sf.jasperreports.. absent from `kotlin-xpdf’s dependency graph as the guard against reintroduction.

Engine boundary

The Typst CLI runs as a subprocess of kotlin-xpdf (TypstCompiler), exactly the shape veraPDF already has (VeraPdfCliValidator): a pinned, checksum-verified binary installed into the worker image (kotlin-xpdf/Dockerfile, INSTALL_TYPST, default on), never a separate service. A service would add a network hop, a second deployable and an authentication question to a call that takes milliseconds and has exactly one caller. The pipeline after the base document — Mustang attachment and XMP, color-space normalization, post-render mark, receipt embedding, veraPDF — runs on the Typst base document.

Sandbox

Every run gets a fresh, owner-only working directory; --root confines file access to it, --ignore-system-fonts plus --font-path pin the glyphs to the fonts the project ships (the Liberation Sans files), and Typst has no network or shell access. A template is data, never code.

Determinism

The creation timestamp is passed explicitly from the shared time source (ADR-029), so identical inputs yield byte-identical PDFs. Golden tests and archive hashes rely on it.

Layout only

Templates receive preformatted strings (data.json) — amounts, dates, quantities are formatted by the Kotlin side with its Money and locale rules. A template never calculates or rounds; the figures on paper are the figures the domain computed.

Engine selection

There is none — Typst renders every invoice through the role interface BasePdfRendererPort (kotlin-shared, InvoiceDto → PDF bytes), implemented by TypstInvoiceRenderer; no XPDF_RENDER_ENGINE variable exists, so a deployment cannot change how invoices render. /status/typst reports Typst’s own availability and its installed and pinned version, mirroring /status/verapdf.

Shipped layout only

The worker image ships exactly one template, standard-invoice.typ; the layout lives in code. Layout changes are made in code or by the operator through admin tooling — not by users. There are no user-uploaded template rows, no shipped-template manifest, no compile-verify endpoint and no template fingerprint on generation_job.

The payment block also carries an optional GiroCode (EPC QR code), generated by the Typst renderer and controlled by the XPDF_PAYMENT_QR environment variable (on/off, default on, case-insensitive); a value that is neither fails the service at startup rather than silently dropping the code from every invoice.

The visual design system — two card styles, color discipline and the five-step type scale — is implemented in the template and described in the section "Design system" below.

9.29.1.4. Target structure

TypstInvoiceRenderer (kotlin-xpdf) builds data.json from InvoiceReportModel and compiles the shipped standard-invoice.typ through TypstCompiler. BasePdfStage and RenderWarmup depend on BasePdfRendererPort only. The report model carries structured fields (info-box rows, identifier sublines, fitted footer lines) and FreeTextMarkup a structured rendering, so the template composes its cells from data and never parses markup.

9.29.1.5. Verification

TypstCompileIT runs against the containerized pinned CLI and proves PDF/A-3b identification, output intent, embedded fonts, byte-identical reruns, repeated table header across pages and diagnostics on a broken template. The full export (receipt embedding, Mustang attachment, brand mark) is accepted by veraPDF 3b on the Typst base.

9.29.1.6. Flow layout, geometry only in Typst

The template comprises two layers. The head is absolutely positioned on the DIN 5008 fixed points (in points): teal and orange letterhead, sender name at y = 20 pt, return address line at y = 77 pt with separator at y = 90 pt, address window at y = 100–140 pt, subject at y = 255 pt, fixed head height 290 pt, footer top at 764 pt. The head’s right column — an absolutely anchored stack at x = 265 pt, y = 22 pt, width 216 pt — holds content-sized info cards (invoice data, tax IDs) bounded to 245 pt in height; card content wraps to grow the card, font sizes never shrink.

The flow body below the head contains no absolute positioning: free text, items table, tax breakdown card, totals card, payment card and notes. Every box is sized by its content. All geometry lives in design tokens at the template’s head (colors, typography scale, 4-point spacing grid, line pitch, named column widths); Kotlin delivers preformatted strings in data.json and no measures.

Flow rules enforce document structure: tax and totals cards are unbreakable as a unit; payment and notes stay together on one page by measuring and using sticky as fallback; the items table header repeats across pages, rows never split. Gaps between blocks come only from spacing tokens. The VAT column is auto-width with a minimum so values like "19,00 %" stay on one line. Kotlin no longer fits any text for Typst: the sender name, the return address line and the footer contact lines arrive raw in data.json (sender.name, sender.returnLine, footer.contact[].text). One template function, fit-line, measures each of them in context and picks the first size of a token ladder that fits its column (sender name 16/14/12 pt on one line, then two wrapped lines down to 9 pt; return line and contact lines 7.5/7/6.5/6 pt); when nothing fits, the smallest size prints with the text shortened and an ellipsis. The free-text placement rule (inline versus after the table) is a model decision, not a template one.

9.29.1.7. Design system

The template follows Bauhaus principles (form follows function, one signal color, no ornament) with a Material-style tonal surface instead of card borders: exactly two card styles exist, a plain tonal area and the same area with a 2 pt accent bar down its left edge for the one emphasised card (payment). The two brand colors are each pinned to a short, named list of places so neither drifts onto a third use by accident.

Token Value Used for

ink

#2C3E50

body text, values

muted

#7F8C8D

labels, sublines, footer

surface

#F4F6F8

the one card/zebra fill; filled cards carry no stroke

border

#D5D8DC

hairlines only — a table’s bottom edge, the totals card’s rule above the gross row

teal

#16A085

the header bar and the table head, nowhere else

accent

#E67E22

header-bar block, gross total, footer line

Typography is a five-step scale; every text size in the template names one of these tokens (the DIN 5008 address window is the one exception, kept literal since its geometry predates and is independent of this design pass):

Token Size Used for

t-headline

16 pt

sender name

t-title

14 pt

subject line, the totals card’s gross row

t-body

9.5 pt

totals net/VAT rows, payment rows, note text, payment hint

t-table

8.5 pt

items table body, info-card rows, tax rows, item description

t-label

7.5 pt

card/table/note labels, sublines, footer, return line, QR caption

Spacing keeps the existing 4 pt grid (s1…s6) and two line pitches (12 pt in a card, 15 pt in the payment card). The payment card carries the due date as a plain sentence, the GiroCode to its right, and a single-line caption sized to the code’s own width; the amount is printed once, in the totals card, and not repeated in the payment card (product-owner decision). The note and the tax note share one title-column width so their body text always starts at the same x, whichever combination is present.

9.29.1.8. Consequences

  • Two binaries in the worker image (JVM app and Typst CLI) instead of one; both pinned, both checksum-verified, both reported by status endpoints. TypstToolchainPinTest keeps the runtime image, the test image and the reported version in lockstep.

  • Typst is pre-1.0: a version bump is a task of its own, with TypstCompileIT rerun.

  • No visual template designer, and none needed: one standard Typst template ships with the product, changed in code or by the operator. Customers do not author layouts.

  • Dockerfile.native does not yet install the binary or fonts; a native worker needs the same stage before Typst is used there.

9.29.1.9. Alternatives Considered

JasperReports 7 with .jrxml templates (rejected; it rendered the base PDF before Typst):

  • .jrxml templates are compiled at runtime through jasperreports-jdt and may contain arbitrary Java expressions — an uploaded layout is executable code, which is why user-supplied templates needed a signature and reconciliation scheme.

  • Geometry is expressed in pixel bands of a report designer, not in the millimetres DIN 5008 is written in; the layout inspector had to measure what the engine actually produced.

  • Output is not byte-deterministic (timestamps, object ordering), which weakens golden tests and the archive’s content hashes.

  • The runtime class generation is at odds with a native image (Dockerfile.native) and keeps a large, reflection-heavy dependency tree in the worker.

  • Keeping it as a reserve fallback module behind a Maven profile and a ServiceLoader hook (briefly the case on 2026-09-27) added code that could only rot, since Typst needs no fallback engine in production.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-xpdf/src/main/resources/typst/standard-invoice.typ, kotlin-xpdf/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/xpdf/impl/mustang/MustangTransactionBuilder.kt.

9.30. ADR-031: EXTENDED Profile Escalation for Cash-Discount Invoices

  • Decision: EN16931 remains the default B2B profile. An invoice carrying a cash discount on the Mustang route is escalated to EXTENDED (opt-in via the admin feature flag feature.extended-profile.enabled, off by default) so the discount travels as structured ApplicableTradePaymentDiscountTerms in addition to the BR-DE-18 SKONTO text line. The profile is resolved once in kotlin-invoice (resolveExportProfileId, Invoice.requiresExtendedProfile) and consumed by both the finalization gate and the XML export. XRechnung is never escalated; Factur-X engine is pinned to EN16931.

  • Consequence: Receivers without EXTENDED support may reject the profile, although the document remains EN 16931 conformant. EXTENDED is an EN-16931-conformant extension, so escalation occurs only where the data justifies the extra profile layer. XML-level validation confirms CII-XSD and Factur-X EXTENDED Schematron conformance; the KoSIT stage is skipped for EXTENDED exports with a dedicated PROFILE stage carrying a WARNING finding; overall validation stays VALID. The validation_profile registry holds an EXTENDED row in state EXPERIMENTAL (Factur-X 1.09.2 Schematron, Mustang 2.26.0).

9.30.1. ADR-031: EXTENDED Profile Escalation for Cash-Discount Invoices

9.30.1.1. Status

Accepted, 2026-09-28

9.30.1.2. Context

The ZUGFeRD EXTENDED profile adds structured ApplicableTradePaymentDiscountTerms to EN 16931, allowing the invoice’s cash discount to be machine-readable alongside the plain-text BR-DE-18 line. EN 16931 is the default B2B profile for German e-invoices and supports only text-based payment terms in BT-20.

By default, a cash-discount (Skonto) invoice on the Mustang route exports as EN16931. Receivers with EXTENDED support can extract CalculationPercent and BasisAmount, but receivers without it see only the text form in BT-20.

9.30.1.3. Decision

EN16931 remains the default B2B profile, even for cash-discount invoices, because the KoSIT XRechnung validator cannot validate EXTENDED. An invoice carrying a cash discount on the Mustang route is escalated to EXTENDED so the discount travels as structured ApplicableTradePaymentDiscountTerms in addition to the BR-DE-18 SKONTO text line. The escalation is opt-in per installation: it applies only when the admin feature flag feature.extended-profile.enabled (FeatureFlag.EXTENDED_PROFILE) is switched on; by default, cash-discount invoices export as EN16931.

Profile resolution

The function resolveExportProfileId(sellerVatId, buyerVatId, extendedFeatures) in kotlin-invoice (domain/invoice/ExportProfile.kt) decides the profile once. It returns XRechnung if a VAT ID appears on only one side (seller or buyer, never both), otherwise EXTENDED if Invoice.requiresExtendedProfile() evaluates to true (= documentEngine != FACTURX and cashDiscount() != null), otherwise EN16931. XRechnung is never escalated.

Engine pinning

The Factur-X engine route (documentEngine == FACTURX) is pinned to EN16931; the engine knows only this profile and cannot output EXTENDED.

Single source of truth

Both consumers use the same profile-resolution function: InvoiceHeaderDtoMapper.buildMeta (setting InvoiceDto.meta.profileId) and FinalizationPolicy (the export gate) consult it identically.

XML markup

In kotlin-xpdf, MustangExportModel.exportProfile carries the resolved profile; MustangTransactionBuilder sets IZUGFeRDPaymentDiscountTerms only for EXTENDED invoices. Mustang writes the element with:

  • BasisAmount = gross amount (currency EUR), calculated by Mustang.

  • CalculationPercent = the cash discount percentage.

  • No BasisDateTime or BasisPeriodMeasure (Mustang writes these only together with a base date, and a base date alongside the due date is forbidden by the schema).

The cash-discount deadline remains in the BT-20 text line #SKONTO#TAGE=… (BR-DE-18 format).

Conformance

The XML form matches ZUGFeRD-2.5.2-EXTENDED example X18. The Guideline-URN is urn:cen.eu:en16931:2017#conformant#urn:factur-x.eu:1p0:extended; the attachment name stays factur-x.xml.

9.30.1.4. Consequences
Profile expansion

Receivers without EXTENDED support may reject the profile, although the document remains EN 16931 conformant per §14 UStG. EXTENDED is an EN-16931-conformant extension, so the invoice’s core facts stay valid; stricter receivers may simply need configuration to accept it. Escalation occurs only when the data (cash discount) justifies the extra profile layer.

Validation

CII-XSD validation must pass; Factur-X EXTENDED Schematron (FacturXSchematronValidator) must return VALID with no findings. The bundled KoSIT XRechnung 3.0.2 configuration has no scenario for the EXTENDED guideline, so the KoSIT stage is skipped with an explicit reason for EXTENDED exports and the Factur-X EXTENDED Schematron stage is authoritative; a dedicated PROFILE stage (outcome VALID) carries one WARNING finding LT-PROFILE-EXTENDED telling the user that receivers without EXTENDED support may reject the file; overall validation stays VALID.

Verification

ExportValidationStagesTest (KoSIT-Skip only for EXTENDED, PROFILE stage with WARNING, overall VALID), XmlExportServiceImplTest verifies the XML fragment, XSD validity and Factur-X EXTENDED Schematron acceptance. Unit tests for ExportProfile, InvoiceDtoMapper, FinalizationPolicy, MustangTransactionBuilder, MustangExportModelMapper and InvoiceDtoArityTest confirm the escalation logic, profile-ID mapping and data transport. KositValidationIT confirms that EXTENDED URNs are not VALID in KoSIT.

Registration

The validation_profile registry holds an EXTENDED row in state EXPERIMENTAL (Factur-X 1.09.2 Schematron, Mustang 2.26.0), so finalization is allowed without a KoSIT proof. ValidationProfileMigrationIT confirms the EXPERIMENTAL status and that exactly one ACTIVE row exists.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/invoice/ExportProfile.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/invoice/FinalizationPolicy.kt, kotlin-xpdf/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/xpdf/impl/mustang/MustangTransactionBuilder.kt, kotlin-facturx/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/facturx/FacturXSchematronValidator.kt.

9.31. ADR-032: One Receipt, One Embedding

  • Decision: A receipt is embedded exactly once in a produced e-invoice — as the BG-24 AdditionalReferencedDocument/AttachmentBinaryObject (Base64) inside the ZUGFeRD XML (MustangTransactionBuilder). The rasterized annex pages and the separate PDF/A-3 embedded file are both removed; ReceiptAnnexBuilder is deleted. Receipts are reduced at upload (ReceiptUploadService → ReceiptPdfReducer → PdfBoxReceiptPdfReducer, PDFBox) towards ReceiptSizePolicy.TARGET_RECEIPT_SIZE (768 KiB) instead of only being rejected when oversized; the pool stores only the reduced derivative, never the original upload.

  • Consequence: A 19-receipt invoice’s container size is bounded by one copy of each receipt instead of the former three, keeping it well inside mail-gateway and archive-volume limits. Base64WithReserveEstimator (kotlin-shared) sizes the budget on the Base64-encoded receipt bytes plus a fixed reserve. Upload-time rasterisation (stage 2) loses a receipt’s text layer; a user may still decline reduction up to MAX_UNREDUCED_RECEIPT_SIZE (1 MiB), and the export worker’s backstop (PdfExportServiceImpl.enforceSizeBackstop) still refuses an oversized container rather than truncating one.

9.31.1. ADR-032: One Receipt, One Embedding

9.31.1.1. Status

Accepted

9.31.1.2. Context

The export worker used to embed every linked receipt three separate ways in a produced e-invoice: once as a rasterized JPEG page appended to the visible PDF (ReceiptAnnexBuilder, default mode=raster, 200 dpi), once more as a PDF/A-3 embedded file alongside the ZUGFeRD XML, and a third time as Base64 text inside the XML itself (BG-24 AdditionalReferencedDocument), which XRechnung requires regardless of what the visible PDF carries. A 19-receipt invoice at the old 1 MiB per-receipt cap could reach roughly 47 MiB once all three copies were counted — past common mail-server attachment limits (10–25 MB) and several public-sector e-invoicing gateway caps, and three times the archive volume every stored copy carries over the ten-year retention period (Green IT Principles). ADR-014's budget was sized against raw receipt bytes and did not account for any of this multiplication; nothing in the pipeline proactively shrank a receipt before it multiplied, either — an oversized upload was refused outright rather than reduced.

9.31.1.3. Decision

A receipt is embedded exactly once: as the BG-24 AdditionalReferencedDocument / AttachmentBinaryObject (Base64) inside the ZUGFeRD XML, built by MustangTransactionBuilder. The rasterized annex pages and the separate PDF/A-3 embedded file are both removed; ReceiptAnnexBuilder is deleted. The produced PDF holds exactly one embedded file — the ZUGFeRD/Factur-X XML — and nothing else changes about how a human reader finds a receipt: it was never the primary way a receipt was reviewed, since the receipt pool and the invoice’s own receipt list already show it directly.

To keep the resulting container small despite this being the only copy, receipts are now reduced at upload rather than only rejected when oversized: ReceiptUploadService calls the outbound port ReceiptPdfReducer (adapter PdfBoxReceiptPdfReducer, PDFBox), which downsamples oversized embedded images (150 dpi, JPEG 0.75) and, if that alone is not enough, rasterizes every page at the same 150 dpi — see Receipt normalisation at upload. A user may still decline reduction up to a smaller hard cap (MAX_UNREDUCED_RECEIPT_SIZE, 1 MiB); nothing above MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE (10 MiB) is accepted regardless. The pool stores only the resulting derivative, never the original upload — the pool is generation material for this invoice, not an archive of the supplier’s original document, which remains the supplier’s own bookkeeping responsibility under its own GoBD retention duty.

Alternatives Considered

Keep the rasterized annex pages (for a human reviewer flipping through the visible PDF) and drop only the PDF/A-3 attachment:: Rejected: the annex was still a second full copy of every receipt’s pixels, so the size problem this decision solves would only have been halved, not removed, and XRechnung recipients — who never see the visible PDF pages at all, only the parsed XML — would still receive a receipt they cannot use twice as large as necessary.

Keep the PDF/A-3 embedded file (for a viewer that surfaces PDF attachments) and drop only the rasterized annex:: Rejected for the same reason in reverse, plus the PDF/A-3 attachment reproduces the original receipt file byte-for-byte, which is strictly more data than the Base64 XML copy already carries and offers no capability the pool’s own receipt view does not already provide.

Reduce receipt size only at export, leaving the pool and upload untouched

Rejected: reducing at export means every generation re-derives the same reduced bytes from the same original receipt, repeating work on every regeneration of the same invoice, and the pool itself — browsed independently of any one invoice — would still hold oversized originals with no size guarantee of its own. Reducing once, at upload, means the stored bytes are already the bytes every future embedding uses.

9.31.1.4. Consequences
Positive

  • A 19-receipt invoice’s container size is bounded by one copy of each receipt instead of three; the worst case implied by the policy invariant drops from roughly 60 MiB (three embeddings at the old 1 MiB cap) to 20 MiB, and typical invoices land well under that since normalised receipts are usually smaller than the 768 KiB target.

  • ReceiptAnnexBuilder and its raster/merge mode switch are deleted outright, removing a PDF/A-3-compliance concern (mode=merge was never veraPDF-clean) along with the code.

  • Deduplication logic collapses to the one place that still needs it, MustangExportModelMapper.collectReceipts — the export worker no longer deduplicates twice (once for the annex, once for the XML attachment) against two different definitions of "the same receipt".

Negative

  • Rasterization (stage 2 of upload normalization) loses the receipt’s text layer; a receipt that needed OCR/copy-paste of its printed text before now only offers that on whatever stage-1 result preserved it. This is an accepted trade-off against the alternative — refusing the upload outright — which was already this project’s prior behavior for an oversized receipt.

  • A user who explicitly declines reduction can still land a receipt at the unreduced cap (MAX_UNREDUCED_RECEIPT_SIZE, 1 MiB) rather than the target the budget invariant is built on; this is a deliberate escape hatch (the receipt is legally the user’s supporting document, and the system does not get to decide it may not be kept exactly as received), not an oversight — the export backstop still catches the resulting document if enough such receipts push a single invoice over 20 MiB.

  • No original upload is retained anywhere in this system once normalization has run; a user needing the pixel-exact original must keep it themselves, outside the receipt pool.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-shared/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/shared/receipt/ (ReceiptSizePolicy, Base64WithReserveEstimator), kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/invoice/ReceiptUploadService.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/invoice/port/outbound/ReceiptPdfReducer.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/infrastructure/receipt/PdfBoxReceiptPdfReducer.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/masterdata/ReceiptNormalisationStage.kt, kotlin-xpdf/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/xpdf/impl/mustang/MustangTransactionBuilder.kt.

9.31.2. ADR-033: Quote Number Range — Definition Central, Counter Decentral

9.31.2.1. Status

Accepted (2026-10-06)

9.31.2.2. Context

Quote numbers used to be a fixed format (AN-{year}-{sequence}) inside the optional quote module (kotlin-quote), while every other number is configured as a number range in the reference data. Two constraints shape where a quote number range can live. The quote module is an optional package (reactor profile with-quote, QUOTE_ENABLED=false by default), and as a self-contained system it is the only writer of the database schema quote. A gapless quote number must also be drawn in the same transaction as the status change that assigns it, so that a rollback releases the number again; a synchronous call into the invoicing module would break exactly that.

The quote settings (validity in days, machine-readable XML format) raised the same placement question for their persistence and, separately, their user interface.

9.31.2.3. Decision

Definition central, counter decentral.

  1. The definition of the quote number (prefix, date part, counter width, reset cycle) is reference data of the invoicing module: a number range with the new subject QUOTE, delivered as QUOTE_DEFAULT with the pattern AN-JJJJ-NNNN, which reproduces the former fixed format exactly. The counter stays in the quote module: quote.quote_number_range, one row per (range_code, period_key). The gapless draw runs inside the status-changing transaction of "submit for review"; only the quote application writes schema quote.

  2. The definition reaches the quote module over the existing master-data replica (NATS events masterdata.> plus the snapshot), restricted to the subject QUOTE. No second channel exists; the snapshot resync covers fresh installations and late consumers. The quote module reads the replica table number_range_replica.

  3. The shared formatting types DatePart, ResetCycle, NumberPattern and NumberFormatter.compose / periodKey live in kotlin-shared, the only code both modules may share. The ordinal converters for persistence stay in the invoicing module; the order of the enum entries is unchanged.

  4. A QUOTE range permits only the prefix sources NONE and FIXED, scope GLOBAL and continuity GAPLESS. Issuer-dependent prefixes would need context the quote module does not have.

  5. The revision suffix -R{n} stays with the quote module as part of the lifecycle; it is not part of the definition. Parsing a quote number only separates this suffix and assumes no format.

  6. The quote settings stay in quote.quote_setting; they cannot move into the reference data, because the module is optional and owns its schema. Only the user interface moves: a card "Angebot" in the system settings page, shown only when the quote module is deployed, writing changes immediately without a save button.

  7. The card has no runtime toggle. Its status chip shows the deployment fact (QUOTE_ENABLED), not a switchable state.

If the replica holds no active default quote range (for example directly after a fresh start, before the snapshot resync has run), "submit for review" is rejected with HTTP 409 and a hint to A never-replicated instance requests a master data snapshot at startup, so the state ends once the invoicing app has answered. the number ranges tab of the reference data; the quote stays a draft. The state is transient.

9.31.2.4. Alternatives Considered
Synchronous call into the invoicing module for each number

Rejected: the draw could no longer share the status-changing transaction, so a rollback would burn a number, and the self-contained-system rule against cross-module database or synchronous dependencies would be violated.

Counter and definition both in the quote module

Rejected: the number would stay a special case outside the reference data and could not be configured like the other ranges.

Quote settings as reference data

Rejected: with the module not deployed, a reference datum would be an orphan, and the quote module would depend on writes into a foreign schema or a second replication path.

Runtime toggle for the quote card

Rejected: hiding quotes has no demonstrated benefit, creates a second source of truth next to QUOTE_ENABLED and would leave running quotes (validity, acceptance events, takeover into invoices) silently active but invisible.

9.31.2.5. Consequences
Positive

  • Quote numbers are configured in one place with the other number ranges; the delivered default changes no existing number, template placeholder, mail or document.

  • Gapless allocation keeps its transactional guarantee; the replica needs no additional channel.

  • Formatting logic exists once, in kotlin-shared.

Negative

  • The invoicing module and the quote module must be deployed with compatible versions of the master-data contract; the numberrange entity type is part of that contract.

  • Until the replica is filled, quotes cannot be submitted for review.

  • A change of the definition applies to the next draw; an unchanged code continues its counter, a new code starts a new counter.

9.31.3. ADR-034: Missing or Invalid Original Document

9.31.3.1. Status

Accepted, 2026-10-01

9.31.3.2. Context

An issued invoice has exactly one document of record: the PDF of its latest completed issued generation job, stored under a per-job key with its SHA-256 in invoice_document. That object can be gone or altered (storage loss, an operator error, a race between two jobs). The application is not a document archive: there is no bucket versioning, no retention and no archive package that counts as an independent copy. The only independent, byte-identical copy that may exist is the attachment the mail component sent, kept in its own journal bucket. Users still need a valid document on every access, and the audit trail must show what was served in place of the original.

9.31.3.3. Decision

Access to the document of record is resolved deterministically, without a confirmation step:

  1. The stored object is read and hashed. A matching hash serves the original. A missing object and a hash mismatch are the same case, "no valid original"; a mismatching file is never served. An I/O error of the store is neither: the access fails with "storage unavailable" and nothing is regenerated.

  2. If the invoice was never sent by mail, the document is regenerated through the normal job pipeline. The result carries no tag. This includes an invoice in status SENT that was handed over outside the mail component: only the mail component’s SENT result counts as "sent".

  3. If the invoice was sent, a byte-identical copy is restored first: another object of the same invoice in the document store, then the attachment held by the mail component (asked over the existing integration endpoint by invoice number and hash). The first copy whose hash equals the recorded one is written back under the original key.

  4. Only when nothing can be restored is a sent invoice re-rendered. That result is a reproduction: tagged on the document row (reproduced, reproduced_at, reproduction_of_sha256), shown as a tag in the UI and never dispatched — the mail path, the attachment hand-over and the UI action reject it server-side.

"Sent" is the invoice’s own projection of the mail component’s SENT result; the invoice side never reads the mail journal. The row of a lost original moves to the generation status LOST, so the pipeline’s "completed documents only" filters exclude it and the completion of the replacement job knows which hash it replaces.

Every step is journalled through the existing trigger-only audit journal. A transaction-local setting app.audit_context lets the application add reason, source, hashes and job id to the detail column as _context; the value is part of the entry hash. Row-less events (a rejected dispatch, a restore of an already valid row) bump the row’s version so the trigger fires.

The explicit "restore document" action is limited to the permission DOCUMENT_REPRODUCE (roles INVOICING and ADMIN); the automatic path on access needs no further permission.

9.31.3.4. Consequences

  • No warning dialog and no cancel-and-reissue choice in this flow; cancellation stays an independent action.

  • Regeneration is asynchronous: the access answers "being regenerated" with the job id, the job list shows the result.

  • The mail-journal source exists only for invoices sent through the mail component; without it a sent invoice with a lost original always becomes a reproduction.

  • The document bytes carry no mark; the tag lives in the database and the UI only (and in the audit journal entry DOCUMENT_REPRODUCED).

  • A regenerated document of an invoice never sent by mail is not marked anywhere except by the audit journal entry DOCUMENT_REGENERATED. A voided invoice is treated like a sent one.

  • Because the application is not an archive, the operator’s own archive remains the authoritative copy of what was sent (ADR-035).

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/domain/invoice/DocumentReproductionPolicy.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/invoice/InvoiceDocumentRecoveryService.kt, kotlin-invoice/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/application/invoice/InvoiceDocumentRecoveryTransactions.kt.

9.31.4. ADR-035: Archiving Is the Operator’s Responsibility

9.31.4.1. Status

Accepted, 2026-10-07

9.31.4.2. Context

Invoices must be kept for 8 years, books and annual statements for 10 years. The application is a pure invoicing program: issued invoices are handed over to the business’s bookkeeping (for example DATEV, Lexware or the tax advisor’s system). It is not a document archive (ADR-034): there is no bucket versioning, no retention and no archive package that counts as an independent copy. Invoices are normally dispatched by e-mail, so the recipient-bound copy exists in the mail path as well.

9.31.4.3. Decision

Archiving and retention are the operator’s responsibility. Sent invoices, including the XML of the e-invoice, are stored in the bookkeeping or archive system. Because invoices are sent by e-mail, an e-mail archive that stores mail immutably is recommended. The application keeps its document store for operation and for the recovery described in ADR-034, not as a retention medium.

9.31.4.4. Consequences

  • The operator must archive every sent invoice with its XML outside the application.

  • An immutable e-mail archive covers the dispatched copy; invoices not dispatched by mail must reach the archive or bookkeeping system by another route.

  • Backups of the application database and object store are an operational safeguard, not an archive.

  • Data access during a tax audit is provided by that archive or the bookkeeping system; the application delivers XML and PDF.

  • Bookkeeping, DATEV export, payment tracking, dunning and receiving incoming e-invoices are out of scope of the application.

Architecture Decision Record — source: Product owner decision, 2026-10-07.

9.31.5. ADR-036: Offer life cycle is a single status

9.31.5.1. Status

Accepted, 2026-10-07

9.31.5.2. Context

The previous offer model described a sent offer in two dimensions: a workflow status (DRAFT, REVIEW, SENT, INVOICED, CANCELLED) and an outcome attribute (OPEN, ACCEPTED, REJECTED, SUPERSEDED). On top of that, an expiry was derived from the validity date and a revision created a new offer with a number suffix and a predecessor/successor link. Lists, filters, statistics and tests had to combine status, outcome and derived expiry to answer "where does this offer stand"; the revision chain added cross-references in both directions.

9.31.5.3. Decision

The life cycle is one status with seven values: DRAFT, REVIEW, SENT, ACCEPTED, INVOICED, REJECTED, CANCELLED.

  • Transitions: DRAFT → REVIEW/CANCELLED; REVIEW → SENT/DRAFT (return with a note)/CANCELLED; SENT → ACCEPTED/REJECTED (a reason is required); ACCEPTED → INVOICED, only through the event invoice.created-from-quote. INVOICED, REJECTED and CANCELLED are terminal.

  • No outcome attribute, no derived "expired" state and no revision: no number suffix, no predecessor/successor, no revise permission. The validity date is a document field on the PDF only.

  • Log entry kinds: NOTE, STATUS, SEND, ATTACHMENT, INVOICE.

  • The send action in REVIEW is labelled "E-Mail senden"; without the mail module there is no send action.

  • Acceptance rate = (ACCEPTED + INVOICED) / (SENT + REJECTED + ACCEPTED + INVOICED).

9.31.5.4. Consequences

  • Lists, filters and statistics read one column; every state has one meaning and one test path.

  • A change request after sending is handled as a rejection with a reason and a new offer with its own number; there is no link between the two.

  • An offer whose validity date has passed stays SENT until the user records acceptance or rejection.

  • The previous model is kept only as the git tag archive/quote-outcome-model and is not merged.

9.31.5.5. Amendment 2026-10-07

The hand-over to the invoicing application no longer follows from the acceptance. An explicit invoice order (quote.invoice-order.requested, answered by invoice.created-from-quote or invoice.creation-from-quote.rejected, both carrying the order id) is allowed from SENT (acceptance is recorded in the same transaction) and ACCEPTED. INVOICED is still reached only through the reply invoice.created-from-quote; a rejected order leaves the offer ACCEPTED.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-quote, module quote-core (Quote, QuoteStateMachine).

9.31.6. ADR-037: Archive Handover via SMTP Journal Copy

9.31.6.1. Status

Accepted, 2026-10-08

9.31.6.2. Context
