ADR-035 makes archiving the operator’s responsibility and recommends an immutable e-mail archive. Until now the application only recommended it: nothing handed the dispatched mail to that archive, and the journal could not show whether it arrived. The per-template BCC does not fill the gap: it is optional, editable by users, visible in the message header and leaves no result in the journal.

The mail component also builds a signed archive package (manifest.json, original, Ed25519 signatures) when a mail is sent. This looks like a conflict with ADR-035, which states that no archive package counts as an independent copy. It is not one: the package is an operational and evidence artifact of the component, not a retention medium.

9.31.6.3. Decision

  • Interface: the complete dispatched message (MIME/EML, byte-identical to the copy sent to the customer, including PDF and XML) is delivered by SMTP to one archive address per instance. Most mail archives accept journal mail, so the interface is vendor-neutral. A push to an object store (package export) and a webhook were considered and are not built.

  • Addressing: the archive address is an additional envelope recipient (RCPT TO). It is not part of any header, cannot be edited in a template and is invisible to the customer. Only administrators can change it.

  • Optional address: without an address the status is NOT_CONFIGURED and a permanent notice in the message center points to the missing setting.

  • Per document family: a switch for invoices and one for quotes, both on by default. A disabled family gets the status DISABLED.

  • Status axis: MailArchiveHandoverStatus with NOT_CONFIGURED, DISABLED, PENDING, HANDED_OVER and FAILED, separate from MailArchiveStatus of the signed package. The contract changes additively (schema version 5); existing enum values are not extended because consumers with an exhaustive when would break. Translation happens only in the acl adapters.

  • Trigger: the durable outbox handler for MailSent performs the handover, not the inline post-send hook, so a crash after sending does not lose it. PENDING becomes HANDED_OVER when the archive server accepts the message (SMTP 250) and FAILED with the error text otherwise. The result is committed to PostgreSQL before success is reported.

  • No send lock: a failed handover never blocks or reverts the dispatch to the customer (warning instead of lock, as with the receipt budget). FAILED is retried automatically with limited attempts and backoff; a manual retry exists on the journal endpoint. A failure and a missing address raise a notice to administrators.

  • Signed package kept: the internal archive package remains as an evidence artifact of the mail component. It is not a retention medium, and ADR-035 stays valid unchanged.

9.31.6.4. Consequences

  • The journal shows per mail whether the operator’s archive accepted it. Acceptance by the archive server is the receipt; the application does not check what the archive stores.

  • The operator still runs, secures and retains the archive (ADR-035); the application offers the interface and records the result.

  • A handover failure does not delay the customer’s invoice; the cost is a window in which the archive copy is missing until the retry succeeds or an administrator acts.

  • Quotes are handed over on the same path because they are business letters.

  • Delivery is at least once: if the process stops after the archive server accepted the copy but before the result is stored, the retry sends it again. The archive then holds two copies with the same Message-ID.

  • The archive address and switches live in the mail component’s schema (baseline only); the invoicing UI shows the status through a dedicated input port.

  • No vendor adapters, retention periods, WORM or object lock are added.

Architecture Decision Record — source: Product owner decision, 2026-10-08.

9.31.7. ADR-038: Central Architecture Rules

9.31.7.1. Status

Accepted, 2026-10-09

9.31.7.2. Context

The single time source (ADR-029) was enforced by eight copies of one ArchUnit test, one per module, each with its own violating specimens. The copies drifted: the rule that calendar days are read in German time existed in two modules only. Copying the next cross-cutting rule (input ports, suppressions) would repeat the problem.

9.31.7.3. Decision

  • One module, kotlin-architecture-rules: parameterised ArchUnit rules in package de.littletrotter.architecture.rules, first the time rules (TimeRules) and the import of a module’s production classes (ProductionClasses, which also leaves out a sibling’s packaged test fixtures).

  • Test scope only: modules depend on it with <scope>test</scope>; the root POM’s enforcer rule fails the build for any other scope, so ArchUnit never reaches a deployable.

  • No knowledge of modules or domain: the module depends on ArchUnit and the Kotlin standard library only, not on kotlin-shared. Classes it refers to (ClockTimeSource, GermanTime) are matched by fully qualified name. Each module passes its own package roots and the classes it allows (its time source producer).

  • Self-tested: every rule is checked in the module against negative specimens (each forbidden form must be reported) and positive specimens (look-alike calls must not be), so a rule cannot pass by matching nothing. Module gates keep only the call and, where present, a check that the import is not empty.

  • Mail component: its dependency rule (only kotlin-mail-contract, kotlin-edge-identity, kotlin-shared) concerns production dependencies; a test-scope rule library does not touch the component’s independence.

  • Incremental: further cross-cutting rules move here in their own changes.

9.31.7.4. Consequences

  • A rule changes in one place and applies to every module at once; the German business-day rule now holds in all modules, and email-core and email-service have their own time gate.

  • The image builds copy the module like every other reactor module.

  • Rule texts in failure reports are identical across modules.

Architecture Decision Record — source: kotlin-architecture-rules/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/architecture/rules/TimeRules.kt, kotlin-architecture-rules/src/main/kotlin/de/littletrotter/architecture/rules/ProductionClasses.kt, pom.xml (enforcer execution architecture-rules-test-scope-only).

10. Quality Requirements

10.1. Quality Tree

The tree below maps the six quality goals from Introduction and Goals to the concrete quality attributes and enforcement mechanisms that make each goal verifiable rather than aspirational. Domain correctness ranks first because the system’s output is a legal document. Test coverage and quality gates rank second because they keep every other goal from silently regressing. Ergonomics, operability, traceability, and green IT follow, each with its own measurable attributes.

Figure 26. Quality Attributes — Little Trotter X-Invoice

10.2. Quality Scenarios

The table below identifies each scenario by ID and states it as source → stimulus → response → measurement, so a scenario can be checked, not just read.

ID Quality Goal Source Stimulus Response Measurement

QS-1

Domain correctness

User

An invoice is transitioned from draft to issued.

The system runs XSD schema validation, then EN 16931 Schematron rules, then the embedded KoSIT validator against the generated X-Rechnung/ZUGFeRD XML (Mustang Project validator 2.26.0, org.kosit:validator 1.6.3).

The invoice cannot reach SENT state while any validation stage reports a rejection; the KoSIT verdict is persisted and downloadable alongside the PDF/XML.

QS-2

Domain correctness

User

A user assigns receipts to a position or to the invoice as a whole.

The system judges the invoice’s whole resulting receipt set against the shared document size budget (ReceiptSizePolicy) and refuses the assignment that would push the count past 19 distinct receipts, naming the limit; an estimated document size past 20 MiB warns instead of refusing.

No invoice can be saved with more than 19 distinct receipts (deduplicated by object key); document generation (GenerationJobCreationService.createJob) and the export worker (PdfExportServiceImpl.enforceSizeBackstop) each independently refuse an estimated or actual document size above 20 MiB as later, harder backstops.

QS-3

Green IT / lean payload

User

An invoice carries 19 receipts, each normalized at upload to ReceiptSizePolicy.TARGET_RECEIPT_SIZE (768 KiB).

Each receipt is embedded exactly once, as Base64 inside the ZUGFeRD XML (ADR-032); no rasterized annex or PDF/A-3 attachment duplicates it.

Target, not a production measurement: the produced container stays ≤ 6 MiB for typical receipts, on the assumption that reduced receipts land well below the 768 KiB target. The only verified bound is the hard one: 19 receipts at exactly 768 KiB export to ≤ 20 MiB (ReceiptContainerBudgetIT, ReceiptNormalisationE2EIT).

QS-4

Green IT / lean payload

User

A user uploads a 300 dpi scanned receipt above ReceiptSizePolicy.TARGET_RECEIPT_SIZE.

PdfBoxReceiptPdfReducer downsamples embedded images to 150 dpi / JPEG 0.75 (stage 1), then rasterizes pages at 150 dpi if still oversized (stage 2), stopping as soon as the target is met.

Target: upload-time reduction cuts the stored size by ≥ 80% versus the original 300 dpi scan for the common case (a single-page photo/scan receipt). Evidence so far is synthetic only — ReceiptNormalisationE2EIT reduces a generated two-page 300 dpi scan of about 4 MiB to well under the target; real-world scans are not yet measured.

QS-5

Test coverage and quality gates

Developer or agent

A developer or agent runs mvn verify on the root reactor.

JaCoCo computes line and branch coverage per module against the configured BUNDLE minimums.

The build fails below jacoco.line.coverage=0.90 / jacoco.branch.coverage=0.90 (root pom.xml default, including kotlin-invoice); selected critical classes (e.g. Money, tax rule evaluation) carry a 100%/100% CLASS-level override.

QS-6

UI system correctness (full-stack) — planned

Developer or agent

The Playwright E2E suite (e2e-test-playwright/, sibling little-trotter-admin-tools repository, 39 spec files) runs against the dev instance on the server or from the Mac through a tunnel.

Every spec exercises the browser → Vaadin → application → NATS → kotlin-xpdf → RustFS chain the way an Office Manager would, using i18n-label-driven selectors.

Target: the full suite green with zero test.fixme scenarios on a freshly deployed dev instance; the first verified run is the tracked next step (see Risks and Technical Debt).

QS-7

Domain correctness — concurrent editing

Users

Two users load the same invoice position, both edit it, and both save; separately, the ConcurrentCrudStressE2EIT 100-thread storm runs its weighted-random CRUD mix (Concurrent Editing, ADR-028).

The invoice aggregate’s @Version is checked before either save applies: the first commit wins and advances the version; the second is rejected before any row is touched, with an optimistic-lock notification and its dialog left open rather than silently overwriting the first user’s edit.

No lost update is possible: the second saver’s rejected write never reaches the database, proven per entity by the coverage table in Concurrent Editing; the 100-thread storm’s deadlock-delta invariant shows pg_stat_database.deadlocks unchanged before and after the run, matching the stress-suite gate at full-stack scale.

QS-8

Ergonomics / accessibility

Developer

A developer changes UI color tokens or adds a new UI surface.

The change is checked against the Google Material color palette convention (no brown), in both light and dark theme, before merge.

Text and interactive-element contrast meets the WCAG AA target (≥ 4.5:1 for normal text); the theme stylesheet (kotlin-invoice/src/main/frontend/themes/littletrotter/styles.css) records the specific WCAG AA adjustments made.

QS-9

Restart resilience

Operator (platform restart)

The kotlin-xpdf renderer/worker container or the interactive application is restarted while a GenerationJob is DELEGATED or RUNNING.

GenerationJobProcessor’s single configurable-interval recovery scan (`invoice.jobs.recovery-interval, default 1h) resets a DELEGATED job back to PENDING and re-publishes it over NATS JetStream in the same pass, incrementing attempts, but only once StaleDelegatedResetCoordinator has checked the job’s own message against the real stream and found it genuinely gone — a job merely queued behind a backlog, or still in flight under the worker’s own JetStream lease, is left DELEGATED untouched; after invoice.jobs.max-attempts (default 5) resets, the job transitions terminally to FAILED with last_error set and a Message Center notification raised.

No job is silently lost: every GenerationJob ends in a terminal COMPLETED or FAILED state, the Office Manager is notified rather than having to notice a stuck job by polling, and a worker outage of any length never by itself exhausts a job’s attempt budget.

QS-10

Restart resilience (daily maintenance)

Operator (platform restart)

The interactive application is restarted at an arbitrary point in the 24-hour interval of ChainVerificationScheduler or SystemMessageRetentionScheduler.

Each scheduler checks the most recent DatabaseMaintenanceEvent of its own type (CHAIN_VERIFICATION / SYSTEM_MESSAGE_RETENTION) before running: no prior event (fresh deployment) runs immediately, an event less than 24 h old is skipped, an event 24 h or older runs again.

A restart never re-runs a daily task within minutes of its last real run, and never silently skips a day it missed while the process was down.

QS-11

Backup / restore

Operator (cron schedule)

The scheduled (cron-driven) or manually invoked backup.sh runs.

PostgreSQL (primary, plus Authentik if present) is dumped and the entire RustFS bucket (all years) is archived; the combined archive is encrypted with age before any plaintext copy is removed, and retention keeps 7 daily plus 4 weekly archives.

restore.sh against the newest retained archive reproduces master data, invoices, and receipts; an archive that fails age decryption or is missing its manifest is treated as invalid and does not count toward retention.

QS-12

Operability on small hardware — evening power-off

DevOps

DevOps switches the server off in the evening while a print job is RUNNING and a daily task has not run for two days, then switches it on the next morning.

Containers stop within their grace period; on start the worker catches up: pending jobs are re-published immediately, overdue daily tasks run once, and the stale in-flight job is redelivered or ends FAILED with last_error.

Within 60 s of start every GenerationJob is in a terminal state, scheduled_task_run shows one completed run per daily task, and exactly one document set exists per job in RustFS.

QS-13

Operability on small hardware — idle footprint

Operator (idle system)

The stack runs for 10 minutes with no user activity and no pending job.

The worker issues only its minute-scale safety-net queries and no maintenance-status rows are written; the browser side still polls every open invoice list, print-job list, and detail view at its fixed LIVE_UPDATE_POLL_INTERVAL_MS/DETAIL_POLL_INTERVAL_MS interval (2–5 s) regardless of whether a job is pending — narrowing this to poll only while a job is pending is a specified but not yet implemented improvement.

Hibernate query metrics show at most 3 job-table queries per 5 minutes in the worker; the database_maintenance_event row count is unchanged; a UI session left on the invoice list still issues its fixed-interval poll requests until that narrowing lands.

QS-14

Responsiveness under bulk load

Office Manager

Issues an invoice and opens its e-mail editor while a bulk of other document jobs is queued.

The interactive document runs in its own lane (subject document-jobs-interactive, consumer kotlin-xpdf-interactive) and does not wait behind the bulk backlog. Limit: at most 10 s from the send action until the document is READY. If the limit is exceeded the editor does not fail: the send button shows "Dokument wird erzeugt …​" and the editor opens when the document exists (see Open the Invoice E-Mail Editor).

E2E InvoiceMailDocumentPendingE2EIT creates a bulk of synthetic invoices (default 500, property e2e.bulk.invoices), queues them as bulk jobs, triggers one send action and checks that the interactive document is READY within 10 s. Residual risk and the pending load test on the test host: R-13.

11. Risks and Technical Debt

Known risks and open technical debt, surveyed from the program READMEs and specifications, the decision records in Architecture Decisions, and the code itself (re-checked 2026-10-02; states only, individual task files are not enumerated here).

11.1. Risks

ID Title Description Probability / Priority Impact Mitigation

R-1

Targeted fault tolerance only

smallrye-fault-tolerance guards exactly three seams: DocumentJobDispatchGate.dispatch (@Bulkhead, ack wait bounded by invoice.publish.timeout-ms on the returned future, no in-process @Retry — the durable GenerationJob recovery loop is the retry), the workbook exports (@Bulkhead), and HttpMailDispatchAdapter (@Retry/@Timeout). No generic layer wraps every cross-service call (KoSIT validation, S3/RustFS access, renderer calls).

Low

A transient failure in a call outside those seams is not retried at the infrastructure level; it surfaces as a failed step in whatever domain-level flow triggered it.

Deliberate. The document-generation pipeline is covered twice — the bulkhead sized against LT_INVOICE_MEMORY_LIMIT (see Deployment View) and the GenerationJob attempt budget (see Quality Scenarios). Widening fault tolerance to further components waits for a concrete failure mode, not speculation.

R-2

Prototype / not-production-ready status

The project is explicitly declared a research/prototype in the repository README: feature-complete for the core invoicing flow, but not production-ready, provided as-is without warranty.

High

Adopters cannot assume a support commitment, an SLA, or that every edge case is handled; quality is asserted through gates, not through field-proven operational history.

Automated quality gates (90/90 coverage default, zero-finding linters, zero-error SonarQube, passing Gitleaks/Trivy security scans) are the primary risk-reduction lever in lieu of a formal hardening or production-readiness program. The disclaimer is kept explicit rather than implied, so expectations are set correctly.

R-3

Market: comparable products and projects

Other projects and vendors bring similar e-invoicing products to market — the German B2B e-invoicing mandate creates the same demand for every provider, from open-source tools to established accounting suites adding X-Rechnung/ZUGFeRD output.

Medium

The window in which a self-hosted, single-operator solution for freelancers, engineering offices, specialty craft businesses and SMEs is distinctive may close; adopters may prefer a product with a larger ecosystem, hosted operation or an existing accounting integration.

Differentiation is deliberate and documented: data stays on the operator’s own hardware (Architecture Constraints), small-budget hardware is a first-class target (ADR-020), the compliance gate and the number journal are verifiable rather than asserted, and the license keeps the concept open. The market is watched, not modelled; no feature is built solely to match a competitor.

R-4

Shared native services on one host

On the production-style Ubuntu server, PostgreSQL, RustFS and Caddy run natively once and are shared by every instance (prod, dev, demo); see Deployment View.

Low

A maintenance error or an upgrade of one shared service affects all instances at once; a runaway dev or demo workload competes with prod for the same database cluster.

One database, role trio and bucket per instance keep the data separate; the instance overlay never starts a second Postgres or RustFS; backups run per instance. A dedicated host per environment remains the escalation path when isolation matters more than footprint.

R-5

X-Rechnung / ZUGFeRD standards maintenance

EN 16931 semantic rules, the KoSIT validator, and country-specific extensions (e.g. BR-FR-CTC) evolve independently of this project. Validation is provided by pinned third-party libraries: Mustang Project validator 2.26.0 and org.kosit:validator 1.6.3.

Medium

A missed upstream update can let a since-invalidated invoice structure pass local validation, or an upstream rule tightening can reject previously valid invoices without a corresponding local change.

Dependency versions are pinned explicitly with rationale comments (kotlin-xpdf/pom.xml), following the project convention of never downgrading and always moving to the latest version absent a contraindicating CVE. There is no automated upstream-changelog watch yet for KoSIT/EN 16931 releases — open.

R-6

Single-person operations

The project is specified and reviewed by a single Product Owner working with AI coding agents under a spec-then-delegate workflow (see Organizational Constraints in Architecture Constraints); there is no second human reviewer besides the automated gates.

High

Bus factor of one for architectural sign-off; agent-produced changes are checked by automated gates plus a single human review pass rather than a team review process.

Heavy reliance on automated enforcement (linters, SonarQube, Gitleaks/Trivy, coverage gates) and on written decision records (ADRs, architecture decisions) to substitute for a second reviewer. This is a deliberate, documented trade-off of the project’s operating model, not an oversight.

R-7

Browser end-to-end tests (Playwright) — planned

The idea: a real browser drives the whole chain — Vaadin UI, application, NATS, renderer, object storage — against the dev instance on the server, from the Mac through a tunnel or on the server itself, with one scenario per use case (happy path plus edge cases) and a UI-surface versus test-surface comparison that shows which screens have no scenario yet. The suite lives in little-trotter-admin-tools/e2e-test-playwright/ (39 spec files) and is planned, not yet part of any gate.

Medium

Until the suite runs, no automated check confirms the full browser-to-object-storage chain; the unit, integration and Karibu component tests cover the layers separately.

First verified run against the dev instance, then the suite joins the acceptance gates; the remaining scenario gaps close program by program (dev-server offload specification, e2e-test-playwright/README.md).

R-8

Kotlin daemon / containerized build fragility

Under host memory pressure, the Kotlin compiler daemon can silently emit a partial compile — a module reports success but is missing part of its classes — which then surfaces as a confusing failure in an unrelated, later module. Disabling the daemon outright is not a fix; it is documented as a cause of this failure mode, not a cure.

Medium

Intermittent, hard-to-diagnose build failures, worse when multiple agent sessions and Testcontainers-heavy test phases compete for the same host resources.

Mitigated by an in-process compiler execution strategy for containerized runs, a sentinel-class compile-integrity check that fails fast with a clear diagnosis, a machine-enforced build mutex (a lock directory, since flock is unavailable on macOS), and bounded container memory.

R-9

No defined recovery targets; off-site copy optional

No recovery point or recovery time objective is defined. The off-site copy of a backup (external SSD) is optional, and a regular restore test is not part of the routine.

Medium

On a single host, a disk failure, theft or fire can lose all data written since the last backup that left the host.

Open, product-owner decision. Proposal: define the recovery point and recovery time targets, make an off-site copy mandatory, and test a restore periodically.

R-10

Greenfield baseline ends with the first productive instance

The schema exists as one baseline migration and one seed migration without upgrade scripts (see Database Baselines: What Runs When). This holds only while no instance has data that must survive an upgrade.

High

Once a real instance holds invoices, a schema change without a tested migration path would lose or corrupt data.

Open. A migration strategy must exist before the first productive installation.

R-11

Operator database writes can change finalised invoices

The operator may write to the database directly (see Manual Database Writes), including to finalised invoices. The audit trigger records each change, but the operator sets the timestamp (app.now) in the session.

Medium

A finalised invoice can be altered, and the recorded time of the change can be wrong or false.

Open. Proposal: a database-level write lock for finalised invoices and a plausibility check of app.now against the real clock.

R-12

Restoring an older backup can reuse invoice numbers

The number counters and the number journal live in the same database as the invoices, so a restore of an older backup rolls them back together. Numbers already sent to customers after that backup could be issued a second time. The journal self-check and the comparison with sent invoices run inside the restored data and do not see this; only a seal recorded outside the system (see The Number Journal) would show the older chain head.

Medium

Duplicate invoice numbers after a restore.

Open. After every restore, compare the journal head with the last externally recorded seal and with the numbers already sent, before issuing new invoices; a documented restore procedure should state this step.

R-13

Interactive documents wait behind bulk jobs

Document jobs a user waits for (send, print, credit note issued from the UI) run in an interactive lane: own subject document-jobs-interactive and own durable consumer kotlin-xpdf-interactive with dedicated worker threads, inside the same stream LITTLE_TROTTER_JOBS. Bulk jobs (import, regeneration, reproduction, sweeps, admin maintenance) stay on document-jobs. The lane is stored on the job at creation (see Generate E-Invoice). Residual: the results subject document-job-results is shared by both lanes; interactive and bulk workers compete for CPU on the xpdf host; the load test on the test host is pending at wave close.

Medium

Backlog no longer delays interactive documents on the queue; the remaining delay comes from CPU contention on the xpdf host and the shared results subject.

Mitigated: separate subject, consumer, dispatch bulkhead and fast-path executor for the interactive lane (QS-14). The pending state is still shown instead of an error if the limit is exceeded. Open: load test on the test host at wave close.

11.2. Technical Debt

ID Title Description Probability / Priority Impact Mitigation

TD-1

Manual accessibility walkthrough outstanding

A full manual accessibility walkthrough of the application has not yet been performed.

Medium

Accessibility defects that automated checks cannot detect may remain unnoticed.

Per-change contrast checks (WCAG AA) and automated UI-logic assertions exist; the manual walkthrough is the open item.

TD-2

No structured logging, tracing or alerting

kotlin-invoice logs in the default console format; there is no project-wide structured logging or trace propagation (see Observability), and no alerting.

Medium

Failures such as a full disk, a failed backup or an expiring certificate are noticed only when someone looks.

Open. Minimal alerting for disk space, failed backup and certificate expiry is sufficient; full tracing is not needed at this size.
Note
 Backup/restore is not tracked here: it is a deliberate architecture decision (PO, 2026-09-14), not a deferred feature — backups run exclusively through DevOps and little-trotter-admin-tools / little-trotter-admin (Phase 1 shell automation until Q4/2027, Phase 2 lt CLI suite from Q4/2027, see Operations and Admin Tooling), and no in-app backup UI is planned.

12. Glossary

German legal and domain terms are kept in German (not translated) and explained in English alongside their official German designations below, per project convention. Official foreign-language VAT-ID designations are kept in their own language, never translated to a German or English equivalent. Always spelled "Factur-X" — never "Factor-X".

Note
Notice on AI-Assisted Documentation and Synthetic Test Data
Transparency and AI Assistance

This architecture glossary and its technical definitions were compiled, structured, and maintained with the assistance of generative AI systems. All concepts and interfaces reflect the actual implemented system state. All company names, personal data, addresses, and monetary figures used in examples represent randomly generated synthetic test data (datagen).

12.1. Domain & E-Invoicing Standards

Term Definition, Architectural Intent & Context

EN 16931

European standard for electronic invoice data modeling: Defines the core semantic business terms (BT-Codes) independent of serialization formats, implemented via CII (Cross Industry Invoice) or UBL (Universal Business Language).

ZUGFeRD

German hybrid e-invoicing specification (FeRD): Integrates human-readable PDF/A-3b presentation with embedded, machine-readable EN 16931 CII XML.

X-Rechnung

German national standard for structured XML e-invoices in public sector procurement (B2G), executing EN 16931 business rules via Schematron.

Factur-X

Franco-German hybrid e-invoicing format (PDF/A-3b with embedded EN 16931 XML), technically identical to ZUGFeRD 2.x. Official mandatory spelling: Factur-X.

BT Number

Unique business term identifier in the EN 16931 semantic model (e.g. BT-1 Invoice number, BT-10 Buyer reference, BT-25 Preceding invoice reference).

KoSIT Validator

The authoritative Schematron-based reference validator from the German Coordination Office for IT Standards, verifying XML invoices against EN 16931 and national business rules.

veraPDF

Open-source PDF/A conformance validator (ISO 19005-3) executed during document generation to ensure strict PDF/A-3b container compliance.

Invoice Types (UNTDID 1001)

Standard UNTDID 1001 document codes: 380 for standard commercial invoices, 384 for formal invoice reversals, 389 for self-billed invoices, and 381 for commercial credit notes.

Invoice Lifecycle States

State progression: DRAFT (mutable, unnumbered) → SENT (finalized, sequentially numbered, immutable) → PAID (settled) or CANCELLED (neutralized via reversal).

Document Size Budget

Strict total payload budget for e-invoice files (e.g. 20 MiB limit including up to 19 attached receipts normalized to 768 KiB plus 1 MiB document reserve).

Money Value Object

Dedicated domain value object encapsulating monetary scale, currency, and rounding semantics, avoiding IEEE-754 precision issues and raw floating-point arithmetic.

Finalization Gate

Architectural validation barrier blocking transition to SENT if Schematron validation or compliance criteria are unmet.

12.2. Architecture & Technical Terminology

Term Definition, Architectural Intent & Context

Aggregate Root

The primary transactional gatekeeper of a DDD aggregate (Eric Evans): all external mutations, invariant enforcement, and repository access must exclusively pass through this root entity.

Command

An explicit, immutable request object representing the intent to change system state (Command-Query-Separation), completely decoupled from persistence entities or presentation models.

CommandEntryPoint

The architectural boundary marker for write operations: represented by inbound use-case ports to enforce a strict boundary between state-mutating commands and read-only queries.

CommandResult

The immutable response value returned by a command execution, conveying execution status and outcome identifiers without exposing internal domain entities or mutable state.

Composite Interface (Prohibited)

An architectural anti-pattern that aggregates disparate interfaces solely via inheritance, creating broad coupled dependencies and violating the Interface Segregation Principle. Automatically verified and enforced via architectural unit tests.

Domain Event

A past-tense, immutable record of a significant state transition within the domain (Eric Evans), published transactionally to trigger side effects across bounded contexts.

Claim Check (Envelope Messaging)

A decoupled messaging pattern (Hohpe & Woolf: Enterprise Integration Patterns) where large payloads are stored in an external data store, while lightweight messages containing reference pointers and metadata are transmitted across the message bus.

Fat Interface

An anti-pattern violating the Interface Segregation Principle (Robert C. Martin): interfaces bloated with unrelated methods, forcing clients to depend on operations they never call.

Input Port (Role Interface)

A client-focused role interface in the application inbound layer (Alistair Cockburn: Ports & Adapters; Martin Fowler: Role Interface), defining the exact contract required by incoming adapters (UI, REST, events) to reach the core domain.

Modulith

A modular monolith architecture: a single deployable artifact whose internal subsystem and package boundaries are strictly defined and statically verified against architectural drift.

Outbox Pattern (Transactional Outbox)

A resilient messaging pattern (Chris Richardson) that guarantees at-least-once event delivery by atomically persisting outgoing messages into a dedicated database table within the business transaction.

Role Interface

An interface designed specifically around the tailored needs of a consumer (Martin Fowler) rather than the exhaustive surface of a service implementation.

Sealed Result

A strongly typed algebraic data type (e.g., a sealed class or sum type hierarchy) representing all discrete domain and business outcomes of an operation, enforcing compile-time exhaustive evaluation.

Self-Contained System (SCS)

An autonomous, independently deployable subsystem with its own data storage, business logic, and UI/API surface, interacting with other systems only through minimal asynchronous contracts.

Sweeper (Reconciliation Worker)

A resilient background worker that periodically inspects persistent state to detect, recover, and retry stalled asynchronous jobs without relying on volatile in-memory state.

Lean Operations Rollout

A staged operational lifecycle model: establishing an operational baseline with core automation and standardized infrastructure, scaling into a unified management and observability plane upon satisfying strict reliability gates.

Dispatch History (Versandverlauf)

Append-only audit journal within the mail subsystem (kotlin-email) tracking outgoing dispatch lifecycles (QUEUED, SENT, FAILED), transport hashes, and template lineage.

Signed Archive Package (tar.gz)

A deterministic, byte-reproducible archive container (original/, signed/, manifest.json) sealed with detached Ed25519 signatures, providing cryptographic immutability for statutory records.

Appendix A: C4 Reference

Visual guide to C4 Model elements and their official notation, following C4 Model standards.

Official C4 Notation Diagram

The C4 model defines four levels of abstraction — Context, Container, Component, and Code — each with a specific shape, color, and purpose, as defined by Simon Brown. The three levels this documentation actually models are cross-referenced here rather than repeated: system context in System Scope and Context, containers and components in Building Block View.

The notation legends below show the element types and relationship styles used across this documentation’s diagrams.

System Context Notation

The System Context diagram shows the high-level scope of the system and its external actors.

Figure 27. System Context elements and notation

Container and Component Notation

Container and Component diagrams build on System Context notation by adding internal structure.

Figure 28. Container elements and notation
Figure 29. Component elements and notation

Deployment Notation

The Deployment diagram extends these notations to show how containers are mapped to infrastructure nodes.

Figure 30. Deployment elements and notation

The Deployment View is orthogonal to the four levels of abstraction — it maps containers onto the real infrastructure nodes (Docker containers, servers) where they run. See Deployment View.

The Dynamic View traces a specific runtime scenario (e.g. issuing an invoice) across containers and components.
